‘This fresh and informative volume by young scholars who came of
age as information technologies were significantly altering the ever
dynamic landscapes of protest and response is most welcome! With

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 3 ---
clarity and insight, its contemporary case studies cross and fuse
disciplinary and national borders, while respecting the complexity and
sometimes irony of the topic. Should be in the library of anyone
interested in technology, social movements and democracy.’
Gary T. Marx, Professor Emeritus MIT, author of Windows into the
Soul:
Surveillance and Society in an Age of High Technology
Protests in the Information Age
Information and communication technologies have transformed the
dynamics of contention in contemporary society. Social networks such
as Facebook and Twitter, and devices such as smartphones have
increasingly played a central role in facilitating and mobilizing social
movements throughout different parts of the world. Concurrently, the
same technologies have been taken up by public authorities (including
security agencies and the police) and have been used as surveillance
tools to monitor and suppress the activities of certain demonstrators.
This book explores the complex and contradictory relationships
between communication and information technologies and social
movements by drawing on different case studies from around the
world. The contributions analyse how new communication and
information technologies impact the way protests are carried out and
controlled in the current information age. The authors focus on recent
events that date from the Arab Spring onwards and pose questions
regarding the future of protests, surveillance and digital landscapes.
Lucas Melgaço is an Assistant Professor in the Department of
Criminology of the Vrije Universiteit Brussel (VUB), Belgium where he
combines his background in geography with his specialization in
surveillance, security and policing studies. He holds a doctorate
degree in Geography from a partnership between the University of São
Paulo (USP) and the University of Paris 1 – Panthéon Sorbonne. He
has also worked on translating and introducing the theories of Milton
Santos to the English- speaking community. Lucas is co- editor of the
book Order and Conflict in Public Space (Routledge, 2016) and lead

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 4 ---
editor of the journal Criminological Encounters.
Jeffrey Monaghan is an Assistant Professor at Carleton’s Institute for
Criminology and Criminal Justice, Canada. He has a PhD in Sociology
from Queen’s University, where he studied at the Surveillance Studies
Centre. His research is focused on the surveillance of social
movements with a focus on environmental and indigenous
movements; knowledge construction practices associated with
contemporary policing of ‘radicalization’; and domestic security
governance in the context of the ‘war on terror’. His recent book,
Security Aid (University of Toronto Press, 2017), examines the
securitization of humanitarian aid. He lives in Ottawa, Canada.
Routledge Studies in Crime, Security and Justice
Edited by Adam Edwards
Cardiff University
Gordon Hughes
Cardiff University
and
Reece Walters
Queensland University of Technology
Contemporary social scientific scholarship is being transformed by the
challenges associated with the changing nature of, and responses to,
questions of crime, security and justice across the globe. Traditional
disciplinary boundaries in the social sciences are being disturbed and
at times broken down by the emerging scholarly analysis of both the
increasing merging of issues of ‘crime’
and ‘security’ and the unsettling of traditional notions of justice, rights
and due process in an international political and cultural climate
seemingly saturated by, and obsessed with, fear, insecurity and risk.
This series showcases contemporary research studies, edited
collections and works of original intellectual synthesis that contribute
to this new body of scholarship, both within the field of study of
criminology and beyond to its connections with debates in the social
sciences more broadly.

Contributors
Lucas Melgaço is Assistant Professor in the Department of
Criminology of the Vrije Universiteit Brussel (VUB), Belgium where he
combines his background in geography with his specialization in
surveillance, security and policing studies. He holds a doctorate

Jeffrey Monaghan is Assistant Professor at Carleton’s Institute for
Criminology and Criminal Justice, Canada. He has a PhD in Sociology
from Queen’s University, where he studied at the Surveillance Studies
Centre. His research is focused on the surveillance of social
movements with a focus on environmental and indigenous
movements; knowledge construction practices associated with
contemporary policing of ‘radicalization’; and domestic security
governance in the context of the ‘war on terror’. His recent book,
Security Aid (University of Toronto Press, 2017), examines the

Manuel Maroto Calatayud is a researcher and Assistant Professor at
the University of Castilla- La Mancha, Spain, where he teaches
criminal law, prison law, and on white- collar crime. In 2012 he
obtained his PhD with a thesis on political corruption and the illegal
funding of political parties. His research interest includes economic
crimes, repression of political dissent and the right to protest, and the
criminological significance of surveillance technologies and big data
in the ‘post- Snowden society’. He has authored a number of
publications on the intersections of criminal policy and online
behaviour, such as the regulation of identity theft or the legal and
technological evolution from traditional wiretapping practices towards
the use of data mining and widespread use of social media platforms
as a source of police and criminal justice intelligence.

Introduction
Taking to the streets in the
information age
Lucas Melgaço and Jeffrey Monaghan
The idiosyncrasies of protesting, rioting, demonstrating, taking to the
streets in the information age can be highlighted through a few recent
anecdotes. If you were in Kiev, Ukraine, while anti- government
protests took place on 21 January 2014, you possibly received the
following message on your cellphone: ‘Dear sub-scriber, you are
registered as a participant in a mass riot.’ The message, in Ukrainian,
was sent from an unrecognized phone number to people in the vicin-
ity of the streets where the riots took place. The three main Ukrainian
cellphone companies, however, denied any participation in the
delivery of these messages or having forwarded any confidential
geolocation data of their cus-
tomers to third parties.1
On 22 July 2013, in Rio de Janeiro, Brazil, the riot police acted
violently towards a group of protesters after a molotov cocktail was
thrown at the officers.
By the next day, videos made with cellphone cameras were uploaded
on YouTube (dozens of them) and shared on social networking sites
such as Facebook and Twitter, showing that the person who threw the
molotov cocktail was in reality an undercover police officer. It seems
that the police had planned that action as a way to justify their violent
intervention.
On 15 October 2015, in Brussels, Belgium, Thomas Michel,
cameraman for the alternative media collective ZinTV was stopped by
the police when filming a protest against the Transatlantic Trade and
Investment Partnership (TTIP).

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 16 ---
The police, in an illegal action (Beys, 2014), forced Michel to delete
all pictures and videos he had taken with his camera. The officer who
conducted the action claimed that he had the right to delete images in
which police officers could be identified, particularly in those
moments when Brussels was under an alert level 3 (out of 4) for
potential terrorist attacks. Later, Michel, with the help of some
hackers, managed to recover some of the pictures from the memory
stick of his camera and subsequently exhibited them as an art
installation.
More recently, on 11 August 2017, Amer ican neo- Nazi groups under
the banner of the ‘alt- right’ took to the streets of Charlottesville
carrying tiki torches and chanting racist, xenophobic and anti- Semitic
slogans. After the event, a twitter account named @YesYoureRacist
asked their more than 200k followers at the time to participate in a
crowd sourcing effort to help in the
2 Lucas Melgaço and Jeffrey Monaghan
identification, naming and shaming of people who took part in the
demonstration. Among the protesters was Cole White, the first person
to be exposed by the Twitter account, who got fired as a consequence
of his association with these neo- Nazi groups.
These anecdotes shed light on some particularities of contemporary
protesting, such as the presence of smartphones (and their geolocation
features) in the sites of protesting, the role of social media sites such
as YouTube in the new visibility (Thompson, 2005) of protests, the
practical impossibility of remaining anonymous when protesting in
public spaces, and how control over data, images, and certain
technologies relate to power and contestation.
Taking to the streets, demonstrating in public space, is, however,
nothing new. It is one of the most common, ancient and disseminated
forms of channel-ling public dissatisfaction. In basically any recent
historical period and any place of the occidental world one can recall
events in which crowds gathered at symbolic public places, waving
flags, hoisting signs and shouting slogans to express their demands for
social change. When movements confront the status quo or entrenched
systems of power or exploitation, confrontations ensue and protests
rarely unfold without being marked by various levels of scrutiny,
control, and/or repression from policing and security agencies. While,
aesthetically speaking, many similarities can be drawn in protests
across the time, this book shows new characteristics about protesting
today. Such novelties do not only relate to new ways of voicing

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 17 ---
discontentment online, through online petitions for example, but also
to how Information and Communication Technologies (ICTs) permeate
the logics of taking to the streets in the current era.
ICTs and protests
By way of comparison let us suggest two images of cars being
overturned and destroyed by angry protesters. The first image dates
back to November 1904 in Brazil and is one of the rare photographic
testimonials of the protest that took place in Rio de Janeiro that
became known as The Vaccine Rebellion (Carvalho, 1987). Citizens
unhappy with a government policy of mandatory vac-cination against
an outbreak of smallpox took to the streets of Rio, the then capital of
the country, as a way to protest against state hygienist practices
(Figure I.1).
A bit more than a century later, in June 2013, very similar images
could be seen in Rio (Figure I.2) and in many other Brazilian cities in
a wave of protests that appeared under the nicknames V for Vinegar
Movement, Brazilian Spring or, most prevalently, June Journeys
(Vainer et al., 2013). Over the course of a month, people took to the
streets to protest against a number of contentious issues, including
political corruption and police brutality. However, what sparked those
demonstrations was a general dissatisfaction over poor transportation
services. Such dissatisfaction was materialized in actions against
vehicles, which included cars being overturned and, in many cases, set
on fire.

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 18 ---
Figure I.1 Streetcar overturned in Rio de Janeiro, Brazil. The
photograph was originally published in the newspaper Revista da
Semana on 27
November 1904.
Figure I.2 Car overturned and set on fire during the June Journeys in
Rio de Janeiro, Brazil. Photograph by Sandro Vox, taken on 17 June

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 19 ---
2013.
4 Lucas Melgaço and Jeffrey Monaghan
Despite the aesthetical similarity of these two images of a car being
overturned in public space by protesters, they carry many differences
that go beyond the distinct motivations behind each of the
demonstrations. Among these differences is the amount of data
produced and collected in each of the cases.
What little information is available to us today about the Vaccine
Rebellion and can be found in history books is based on the
testimonials of those involved in the riots, news reports and a handful
of images such as the photograph presented above. On the other hand,
the amount of data produced in the June Journeys case is countless.
To give but a few examples, the same images of overturned cars were
taken from many different angles by demonstrators, bystanders, video
surveillance cameras, and journalists and were shared extensively on
social media. In 1904, it was easier to protest in an anonymous way. If
a rioter had not been seen by bystanders or if those bystanders did not
manage to register someone’s facial traces, anonymity was
guaranteed. In contrast, today it is more difficult to – at the same time
– call attention to a performance (which is incidentally the main
purpose of any demonstration, that is to say, to demonstrate, to bring
attention to something) and remain anonymous. Despite attempting to
conceal their faces during the June Journeys, many protesters were
identified and arrested at a later time through the images and digital
traces they left.
Protests and ICTs are becoming increasingly present and progressively
impacting the logics of protesting. There was a trivialization of the
image with, first, the dissemination of photography (first analogue,
then digital) and the emergence of television. It is unquestionable that
television played a role in steering demonstrations around the world,
either inflating or delegitimizing them (McLeod, 1995). In Belgium,
for example, ‘The White March’ of 1996, the largest mass
demonstration in the history of the country, would probably not have
been as successful in gathering more than 300,000 participants to
protest against the government if it was not for television. Television
played a role in creating a public sentiment of revolt among the public
outraged by the inefficiency of the country’s judicial system in the so-
called Marc Dutroux scandal (the country’s most famous case of
paedophilia). Together with newspapers and the radio, it was mainly
through television, 13 days prior to the event, that the parents of the
victims invited the general public to take part in the march (Walgrave

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 20 ---
& Manssens, 2000).
Over the past few decades, ICTs have progressively taken up a more
prominent role in the dynamics of protesting. There have been,
however, moments when the relevance of ICTs increased dramatically.
This was certainly visible in the movements and global solidarities
associated with the Arab Spring (Castells, 2012).
From then on, ICTs have been central in many other protests that
erupted around the world. To cite but a few: Spain in 2011
(Indignados), Canada in 2012
(Quebec students movement, sometimes referred to as the casseroles
protests and the Indigenous- led Idle No More movement), Mexico in
2012 (Yo Soy 132), United States (Occupy in 2011 and later Black
Lives Matter from 2013 on),
Taking to the streets in the information age 5
Turkey in 2013 (Gezi Park protests), Brazil June Journeys in 2013,
Hong Kong in 2014 (Umbrella Revolution), Paris in 2016 (Nuit
Debout), as well as a growing number of smaller grassroots anti-
austerity and anti- corporatization social movements demanding
meaningful political transformations.
Despite the temporal proximity, these new protests are significantly
different from the Belgian White March, and other protests that
occurred around that time, which could roughly be described as
protests in the Web 1.0 era. Indeed, the first phase of the internet era
(that of mailing lists, chat portals and web pages) was marked by a
boost in the global anti- austerity movement. Events such as the Battle
in Seattle in 1999 were strongly influenced by internet 1.0, which
helped activists to mobilize, amplify their claims and also counter the
discourses of hegemonic media (Wall, 2002). However, this boost
pales in comparison to the impact of the so- called Web 2.0, which
was characterized by the appearance of technologies such as mobile
internet, social media, and smartphones. Since the mid- 2000s, these
technologies have provided movements with even more powerful
organizational tools, as well as platforms for expression, critiques, and
actions that can reach multiple and global publics.
Protests in the Information Age focuses on a number of recent
developments that have arisen from the Arab Spring onwards.
Scholars have recently focused on the dual inflection of ICTs for their
(de)mobilizatory potentials (Mozorov, 2012; Gillham, Edward &

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 21 ---
Noakes, 2013) and this book furthers these conversations through a
range of case studies from Brazil, the United Sates, Spain, Palestine,
the United Kingdom, Germany and Canada. The book’s subtitle refers
to ‘social movements, digital practices, and surveillance’ to underline
how collective action is increasingly mediated by what we call digital
practices and surveillance.
Social movements in the information age:
(de)mobilizatory potentials of ICT
Information and communication technologies are increasingly central
to the understanding of collective action. Scholars from a range of
disciplinary backgrounds have contributed to the analysis of the
transforming domains of protests and politics that are mobilized and
mediated through Web 2.0 (Barassi, 2015; Juris, 2012; Gerbaudo,
2012; 2017a; Mattoni, 2012). Not only does Protests in the Information
Age further our understanding of how ICTs contribute to what Earl
and Kimport (2011) have called our ‘digital repertoire of contention’
but the volume also points to how the same technologies used for
mobilization have been harnessed by policing and security agencies in
efforts to demobilize contemporary social movements. Advancements
in the centrality of ICTs to contemporary social movements are visible
in the growing list of mobilizations, which are organized, mediated
and – at times – suppressed through their entwinement with Web 2.0
platforms and applications.
Social media and ICTs have opened new repertoires for social
movements to contest power (Castells, 2012; Juris, 2012). ‘Repertoires
of contention’ are
6 Lucas Melgaço and Jeffrey Monaghan
actions or organizational decisions adopted because they make sense
to social movement participants given contemporary conditions and
contexts of resistance (Tarrow, 1993; Tilly, 1993). Social movements
scholars have traced how technological transformations have had a
major impact on the tactical repertoires used for movements
demanding politic change (Juris, 2008; Tarrow, 1994; Traugott,
1995). McAdam (1983) demonstrated that the use of new innovation
and creative social movement repertoires are produced by integrated
dynamics that spring from both the effectiveness of tactics as well as
the responses of social control agencies. The interplay of contention
and reaction between ICTs and policing agencies has dramatically
accelerated through the innovativeness and rapid transformations of

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 22 ---
technologies (Noakes & Gillham, 2007; Tarrow & McAdam, 2005).
Both policing agencies and movements have been increasingly agile
with their tactical innovations and responses and della Porta and
Tarrow (2012) have used the notion of ‘interactive diffusion’ to
highlight the rapidly changing interactions between protest
movements and policing responses.
The growing evidence of transformative protest politics includes many
movements against neoliberalism across the world but also a re-
emergence of right and even extreme right movements such as the alt-
right in the US. In addition to the more well- known movements
mentioned above, Protests in the Information Age documents localized
campaigns in a number of locations, such as Chicago and Palestine,
which illustrate the transformative and agonistic capacities enabled by
Web 2.0. Although a diversity of movements have developed
techniques of mobilization using digital platforms, this volume is
focused on what Butler (2015, p. 11) calls pluralist movements that,
through demanding and exercising a ‘right to appear’ for a plurality of
people ‘deliver a bodily demand for a more livable set of economic,
social, and political conditions no longer afflicted by induced forms of
precarity’. Particularly in the current moment of neoliberalism and
conflict, Protests in the Information Age addresses how social media and
Web 2.0 have become embedded in social movement practices to
challenge oppression and domination. Moreover, the innovative uses
of social media are constitutive of a digital co- presence and elements
of collective actions that transcend the traditional requirements of
time, space, and physical co- presence, blurring distinctions between
movements and the techno-sphere in what Butler points out are
movements to make our collective presence
– and resistance – visible. Or we can borrow from Santos’s (2017)
vocabulary to say that social movements today experience an
‘enlargement of contexts’.
Contemporary interactive diffusions are notable in that they present
an element of novelty in terms of the technological evolutions, yet the
interplay of protest innovation and public order controls demonstrates
a continuity with recent scholarship on transnational protest policing.
These control practices were highly refined during the period of
transnational protests, where police authorities devised a multitude of
techniques for control (della Porta, Peterson
& Reiter, 2006; Ericson & Doyle 1999; King & Waddington, 2005;
Starr,

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 23 ---
Taking to the streets in the information age 7
Fernandez & Scholl, 2011). This involved the physical control of space
(Waddington & King 2007; Zajko and Beland, 2008), techniques of
psychological control and disruption (Boykoff, 2007; Graeber, 2010),
and – perhaps most notably – increasingly sophisticated techniques of
surveillance and targeted interventions (Fernandez, 2008; Gillham,
2011; Gillham, Edwards & Noakes, 2013; Monaghan & Walby, 2012a,
2012b). In the interaction between social movement strategies and the
innovation of police responses, the possibilities of ICTs have both
enhanced the repertoires of contention at the disposal of protest
movements, as well as the possibilities of surveillance directed against
them.
Digital practices
‘Digital practices’ can refer both to surveillance and control efforts
practised by policing agencies and to the entanglement of ICTs with
social movements. By using the term ‘practices’, we underline that
new medias and technologies are not simply technology, but
technology utilized by social movement participants within broader
social justice struggles. It is through the spectrum of potential opened
up by the technologies that digital practices can be harnessed – as well
as utilized by control agents in the suppression of these possibilities
for transformation. In either repertoire – control or liberation – the
technologies are never neutral but embedded facets of collective
actions. Specifically related to the digital practices of movements, we
suggest there are three elements of how engagement with Web 2.0
marks a novel characterization of contemporary activism.
First, digital practices have horizontalized political mobilization in a
manner that increasingly sees traditional social movement
organizations (SMOs) dis-placed by heterogeneous actors enabled by
platforms and applications (Earl, 2015; Toepfl, 2017). Bennett and
Segerberg (2012) have noted the horizontalist potential of social
media platforms in mobilizing mass movements, underlining how the
participatory and performative practices create a personalized nature
of
‘digitally networked action’ (DNA) formations. Cycles of contention
can be amplified through specific social media tactics or campaigns,
dramatically transforming the character of the campaign as well as the
potential for broader participation ‘through inclusive and diverse
large- scale personal expression rather than through common group or
ideological identification’ (Bennett & Segerberg, 2012, p. 744).

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 24 ---
Authors such as Rovira Sancho (2014) underline the democratic,
horizontal potentialities of social media organizing and the tendencies
for more participatory decision- making as well as organizational
principles that privilege openness, transparency, and egalitarianism.
These practices entangle both tactical and strategic elements in that
they are grounded in both contestation (tactical decisions about the
mobilization) and becoming (strategic decisions about the social
relationships we wish to create in the world).
Second, digital practices include the tactical affordances provided by
Web 2.0 as well as the tactical innovation that has meant that digital
repertoires of
8 Lucas Melgaço and Jeffrey Monaghan
contention increasingly shape social movement identities and,
perhaps, larger strategies of social transformation. Scholars have
underlined how ‘social media affordances’ (Calo 2016; Earl and
Kimport 2011; Wood 2015b) have advanced the repertoires available
to movement participants, yet we stress the mingling of tactics into
the domain of Web 2.0 produces a reciprocity between movements
and tactics that are mutually constitutive. Following Earl and Kimport
(2011, p. 181), we agree that ‘… the sum of the effects is not just to
add new tactical options but to entirely change what is common
between tactics and hence the fundamental repertoire’. Underlining
Tilly’s (1993) point that repertoires of contention require two
elements: culturally situated available tactics and the
‘common’ characteristics shared by the tactics (e.g. the strategy), the
novel case studies in this volume point to the importance of viewing
social media affordances as more than simply tactical, or
technopolitical, innovations. The entwining of digital practices into
social movement identities itself presents a radical transformation to
the character of collective action in the information age (Gerbaudo,
2014; Treré, 2015). As Barassi (2015, p. 8) has argued, ‘development
of mobile technologies and Web 2.0 platforms has marked a new and
complex transformation of the repertoires of mediated political
action’. Our contention here is not one of techno- optimism, or even
less of techno-determinism, but an acknowledgement that the tactical
repertoires mobilized by social movements are no longer simply
technologies to be used, but are constitutive of both the agents and
structures in which they are situated.
Third, digital practices are productive: they produce media, identities,
knowledge – all of which are combined in a dynamic of contention

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 25 ---
that is increasingly mediated by capitalism and societies of control.
Harcourt (2015, p. 21) has claimed we have become immersed within
a social and political condition characterized as a ‘new expository
power’ where pleasure and punishment ‘can no longer be decoupled’ –
and we suggest that protests are a site in which the productive
entanglements of collective empowerment and collective control are
markedly visible. Much attention has been directed towards social
media and Web 2.0 but we emphasize how platforms continue to
proliferate as do the portable technologies and digital photography,
the broad element of communications devices, that enable the
horizontalist productivity of (and verticalist controls over)
movements, not to mention the new possibilities opened by the
emergence of the deepweb.
While many of the major platforms are controlled by capitalist
corporations and the security agencies whom these corporations are
inseparable from, there are also efforts to produce more secure – and
less corporate – infrastructures for social movements. This is the case,
for example, of the app FireChat that was used during the 2014 Hong
Kong protests as a way to create and alternative network to
the internet.2 By making use of the app, protesters were able to
exchange messages
through a network based only in the WiFi connection of cellphones.
As other case studies in this volume attest, the race to harness
technological advantages by movements is matched by efforts of
control agencies who mobilize their own digital practices for purposes
of surveillance and suppression.
Taking to the streets in the information age 9
Surveillance and protests: an (in)visibility
dialectics
In the case of protests, visibility is a synonym of power. Social
movements make use of digital practices to call attention to their
claims and amplify their performance in public spaces. On the other
hand, as already mentioned, visibility means exposure and makes way
for control and surveillance. In other words, while underlining efforts
to engage the rights of a pluralistic collective future, an emphasis
should also be placed on the dynamic ways in which state surveillance
uses digital technologies in efforts to control and demobilize. In what
can be characterized as a struggle over the control of visibilities, social

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 26 ---
movements have engaged a pluralistic performativity to make their
rights and demands more visible while agencies of state surveillance
have likewise mobilized new organizational and technological
practices to surveil and control these movements.
When one talks about surveillance studies, the most common
reference is the Panopticon, as designed by Jeremy Bentham in the
eighteenth century. Foucault (1975) reinterpreted Bentham’s idea of
the Panopticon as a metaphor to describe the ‘disciplinary’ society.
While Foucault’s panopticism has been essential to the consolidation
and founding of surveillance as a field of study, authors have
questioned and modernized his theories. Such is the case of Gilles
Deleuze (1992), who suggested a move from a disciplinary society to a
society of control, where disciplinary incarceration gives way to
continuous and instantaneous control. Following Deleuze, one can say
that in the current information era, surveillance is, or is able to be,
present everywhere. As Bauman and Lyon (2012) suggest, surveillance
has become ‘liquid’, in the sense that it has become diffuse, complex
and ubiquitous, in a similar logic to Haggerty and Ericsson (2000)
proposing the concept of ‘surveillant assemblage’. Moreover, the
number of agents involved today in surveillance has increased. Rather
than a centralized Foucauldian panopticon in which one watches
many, we have evolved towards a situation that includes what
Mathiesen (1997) called ‘synopticism’, where many can watch one.
Deleuze’s idea of rhizome (Haggerty & Ericson, 2000) is also useful in
illustrating the change to a less hierarchical framework where
everyone can be simultaneously subjected to, and an agent of,
surveillance. This means that the totalitarian condition suggested by
Orwell’s Big Brother metaphor, where surveillance is centralized in
the hands of the state, does not fully explain the current surveillance
society. The same surveilled citizens can on occasions become the
watchers themselves and this is increasingly being done by social
activists, as will be seen in this book.
In the context of current protests, surveillance thus goes beyond a
mere panoptical model. On the one hand there are top- down
surveillance practices promoted by public authorities, as described by
Ulrich and Wollinger (2011). The authors show that in addition to
CCTV cameras, other technologies such as mobile CCTV vehicles and
drones have increasingly been deployed in public demonstrations. On
the other hand, alongside top- down surveillance practices,
10 Lucas Melgaço and Jeffrey Monaghan
other more participatory and less hierarchical methods have also

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 27 ---
appeared.
Authors such as Andrejevic (2005) and Reeves (2012) use the idea of
lateral surveillance to stress how citizens are not only being watched
by public authorities but also becoming the watchers of their peers. A
similar idea can be found in Trottier’s (2014) concepts of
crowdsourcing surveillance, and digital vigilantism (Trottier, 2017),
the latter of which fits perfectly in the case of the anecdote on the alt-
right protests mentioned at the outset. In addition to this lateral
surveillance, bottom- up surveillance practices also emerge when
citizens not only watch themselves but also dare to watch their
watchers, as in the case mentioned above of the undercover police
officer throwing molotov cocktails at his colleagues in Rio de Janeiro.
These bottom- up strategies were considered by different authors, such
as Mann (2004), who discusses ‘sousveillance’, and Monahan (2006),
who speaks of ‘counter- surveillance’. They could also be framed
under the concept of ‘counter- rationality’, suggested by Santos
(2017).
The emergence of lateral and bottom- up perspectives does not mean,
however, that top- down state and corporate surveillance is ceasing to
exit or losing its power. Both aspects coexist, influence each other and
are in a process of intensification.
The dialectics of surveillance, however, goes beyond such a simplistic
direc-tional scheme. One of the reasons for that is the fact that
surveillance does not only emerge from technologies deliberately
created to serve as monitoring tools but is also a consequence of an
increasing digitization of everyday practices.
These expansionary characteristics is what Marx (1988) named
‘surveillance creep’. Expansions of data and communications practices
have increased the efficiencies and intimacies of surveillance in
everyday life – and the control practices targeting social movements
are inseparable from these broader social trends. ‘Our lives’, write
Bennett et al. (2014, p. 3) ‘or rather, those traces and trails of data,
those fragments of reality to which our lives can be reduced – are
visible as never before, to other individuals, to public and private
organizations, to machines’. Critical consideration towards the
centrality of data practices –
collection, sharing, algorithmic predictions and profiling – have called
attention to the prolific assemblages that produce knowledge – and
control regimes – in contemporary society. While aspects of data
surveillance that align with the rewards and conveniences of

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 28 ---
surveillance capitalism (Zubof, 2015) are acknowledged – and we
frequently acquiesce or participate in these data practices –
state surveillance regimes are more opaque, shrouded by what
Huysmans (2014) calls ‘security thickness’. Such opacity, on the other
hand, is not a guarantee that states (and companies) are not going to
be monitored, wiretapped and scrutinized by denizens and activists.
In Huysmans account, the dissipation of security practices throughout
the social creates increasingly authoritarian and counter- democratic
security and policing agencies, while proliferating the (in)securities
that authorities seek to govern. Scholars have noted how bureaucratic
regimes that engage in data collection and biopolitical management
have a long genealogy of governance, and
Taking to the streets in the information age 11
the role of surveillance has been particularly central to the modern
state (Scott, 1998). Noting that the management of ‘visibilities lies at
the core of all forms of social control’, Brighenti (2010, pp. 148–149)
has argued that surveillance is
‘predicated upon the effort to achieve and subsequently manage, in a
routine way, the visibility of various identities, conducts and events to
the advantage of the specific agent or agency that promotes the
activity of surveillance’. Particularly in the domain of social
movements, state surveillance has proliferated and, despite its
thickness, has been shown to be increasingly preoccupied with all
elements of contemporary movements: from the most mundane
activities to the most contentious (Crosby & Monaghan, 2016). Within
these ongoing transformations, social movement scholars have
grappled with the novel characteristics of contemporary movement
repression, suppressions, and/or demobilization efforts from the state.
Protests in the Information Age furthers critical examinations into these
unfolding domains. We underline that the monitoring capacities of
Web 2.0
have accelerated the already- present trends towards more intensive
surveillance of social movements. While scholars noted a decline in
police antagonisms against social movements in the 1980s and 1990s
that in part softened the surveillance directed against movements
(della Porta & Reiter, 1998), there has been a marked acceleration of
police antagonism against movements beginning in the 1990s with the
rise of the Global Justice Movement (della Porta, Peterson & Reiter,

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 29 ---
2006; Gillham, Edward & Noakes, 2013; Wood, 2015a; see also
Graeber, 2010). Key elements of policing have included a heightened
use of no- go zones and large barriers, a more diverse and punitive
array of ‘less’ lethal violence and technologies, mass arrests and,
perhaps most germane for our purposes, a proliferation of
surveillance, pre- emptive techniques, and security intelligence
practices (Beare et al., 2014; Gillham, Edward & Noakes, 2013;
Monaghan & Walby, 2017; Scholl, 2012; Starr, Fernandez & Scholl,
2011).
Such a surveillance of social movements is strongly supported by
something that has become even clearer since Edward Snowden’s
revelation: private entities are providing tools and data to
government, security forces and the police.
The Ukrainian anecdote that opens this chapter is only a very small
and almost irrelevant example when compared with the amount of
data exchanged between companies such as Google, Facebook, Apple
and Microsoft and the Amer ican National Security Agency (NSA). The
mutual relationship between companies and the state is thus crucial
for understanding the surveillance of protests in the information age.
This relationship occurs from both a more local scale as in the case of
Ukrainian telephone companies allegedly passing geolocation data to
the government, or police forces replacing covert policing in protests
sites by undercover profiles on social media, to a more global scale,
such as those revealed by Snowden.
12 Lucas Melgaço and Jeffrey Monaghan
Taking to the (digitized) streets
The emergence of digital practices and different forms of surveillance
give way to the existence of what could be roughly called digitized
streets, or digital landscapes. This is clear when one looks at how
public spaces used by demonstrators have drastically changed and
have not so much to do anymore with that public space in which
protesters in Rio overturned a car in 1904. They are being increasingly
monitored by surveillance technologies that range from video-
surveillance and police body cams to drones and cameras embedded
in smartphones or even algorithms scanning social media contents.
Digital landscapes are a type of big data producer, recording devices
that keep track of actions taking place in public spaces. There is a
quasi- ubiquitous recording of street protests in which being
anonymous has become almost impossible. Actions in public space
leave digital prints that can be used in a later phase to curtail and

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 30 ---
criminalize protesters’ activities: their recorded actions in public
spaces can lead to various forms of post facto reactions and
repercussions from public authorities.
Digital landscapes however do not only mean scrutiny but also the
possibility to broaden repertoires of contention. The internet has taken
to the streets. Protesters do not have to wait until they go back home
to upload a video they recorded during the event, as they can do it on
site, through their smartphones.
Or they can do even more and, instead of simply recording videos that
can be confiscated by the police, as in the Brussels anecdote, they can
broadcast their videos live (Thorburn, 2015; Gerbaudo, 2017b), in real
time, through apps such as Bambuser, Justin.tv, Periscope, Livestream,
Facebook Live, YouTube Live among many others. Despite the
contradictions among activists on the ethics of the live broadcast of
protests (and how they, instead of helping movements, can create
extra evidence for police intervention and investigation), it can be
said, quoting again Santos (2017), that today we live in a
‘convergence of moments’.
The book
This book explores the complex and contradictory relationships
between communication and information technologies and social
movements by drawing on different case studies from around the
world. The contributions analyse how new communication and
information technologies impact the way protests are carried out and
controlled in the current information age. The focus is put on recent
events that date from the Arab Spring onwards and pose questions
about the future of protests, surveillance and digital practices.
The volume is divided in two parts, each containing four chapters. The
first part focuses on the use of digital practices as part of social
movement repertoires of contention while the second part deals with
control practices of policing and security agencies. The book opens
with the chapter, ‘Mobilisation and surveillance on social media: the
ambivalent case of the anti- austerity protests in Spain (2011–2014)’
by Manuel Maroto Calatayud and Alejandro Segura Vázquez. The
Taking to the streets in the information age 13
authors use the recent cases of anti- austerity protests in Spain to
promote a broader analysis of the role of social media in the
mobilization of protests and in the surveillance of political dissidence.

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 31 ---
In Chapter 2, ‘#RahmRepNow: Social Media and the Campaign to Win
Reparations for Chicago Police Torture Survivors, 2013–2015’,
Andrew Baer discusses the role social media played in the successful
campaign for reparations for victims of police torture in Chicago. He
provides an account of how social media worked as a significant
addition to, rather than a substitute for, traditional modes of activism.
The third chapter, ‘Cracks and reformations in the Brazilian
mediascape: Mídia NINJA, radical citizen journalism, and resistance in
Rio de Janeiro’ is by Tucker Landesman and Stuart Davis.
In their chapter they discuss the case of Mídia NINJA, a loose network
of activists recording and, for the first time in the country’s history,
live streaming street- level footage. The authors call attention to the
limitation of a techno- centric approach to the role of ICTs in protest
by exposing the political complexities behind the NINJA case. The first
half closes with the chapter ‘Applying privacy- enhancing
technologies: One alternative future of protests’ by Daniel Bosk,
Guillermo Rodríguez-Cano, Benjamin Greschbach, and Sonja
Buchegger. The authors analyse, through a more computer- science
perspective, a selection of privacy-enhancing technologies that
activists can apply during the life cycle of a protest, that is to say,
before, during and after a rally takes place.
The second part of the book opens with the chapter, ‘Settler colonial
surveillance and the criminalization of social media: Contradictory
implications for Palestinian resistance’ in which Madalena Santos
analyses how Israel uses social media to monitor Palestinians and to
criminalize expressions of resistance against Israeli settler colonialism.
The chapter also discusses how anti- terror measures result in a
reinforcement of abusive surveillance practices towards Palestinians.
In the sixth chapter, ‘Between visibility and surveillance: Challenges to
anti- corporate activism in social media’, Julie Uldam moves the focus
from state to corporate surveillance. The author examines how British
oil and gas company, BP, monitored activists who had criticized the
company. By analysing internal BP files the author details the
corporate surveillance of activists and how these practices dovetail
with policing efforts aimed at movement suppression. The next
chapter, ‘The impact of video tracking routines on crowd behaviour
and crowd policing’, by Marco Krüger, examines overlaps between the
dynamics of football supporters and protesters. The text presents
empirical materials from a video tracking project by German police
and confronts it with a Foulcaudian discussion of the panopticon
model. The book closes with the chapter, ‘Surveillance- ready-subjects:
The making of Canadian anti- masking law’, by Debra Mackinnon. The
chapter examines discourses surrounding the passage of an anti-

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 32 ---
masking law in Canada and how it relates to pre- emptive control and
criminalization of protests.
Protests in the Information Age is highly representative of many
geographies of contention in our contemporary world, as well as the
research practices that are entwined with these transformatory
processes. It provides a range of scholarly
14 Lucas Melgaço and Jeffrey Monaghan
voices, as well as a diverse range of case studies, that animate these
critical discussions. As a contribution to our understanding of the
interactive dynamics between progressive movements and the policing
efforts that aim to control them, the volume provides important
theoretical and empirical accounts of digital practices as intimate
components of how movements take to the streets in the information
age. Understanding both the influences for strategic or tactical
enhancements, as well as demobilization efforts against social actors,
can provide scholars and movements with critical tools for
consideration in our collective future efforts towards creative
resistance and achieving a more humane and egalitarian society.

Part I
Digital practices as part of
social movement
repertoires of contention
Chapter 1
Mobilisation and surveillance on
social media
The ambivalent case of the anti- austerity
protests in Spain (2011–2014)
Manuel Maroto Calatayud 1 and
Alejandro Segura Vázquez
Introduction
The 2008 financial crisis and the bursting of the property bubble,
which was one of the foundations of the Spanish economy, had a huge
impact on the material and ideological structures of the country’s
welfare state. Under strong international pressure to introduce strict
deficit and budgetary control measures, in 2010 the social democratic
party in government at the time agreed with the conservative party to
amend the constitution, making the payment of public debt to
international creditors an absolute priority. In May 2011, the

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 40 ---
conservative party won the elections and intensified the policy of
social spending cuts initiated by its predecessor.
During the change of government, there were massive public protests
against austerity measures and the political system sustaining them.
One of them gave rise to what is now known as the Indignados
movement (after Stéphane Hessel’s pamphlet Indignez- vous! ,
published in Spain in early 2011) or, most often, the
‘15 May movement’ (‘15-M’), in reference to the date of the 2011
demonstration that led to the protest camp ( Acampada Sol) at Puerta
del Sol in the centre of Madrid. This protest camp, which lasted 28
days until it was disbanded on 12
June, quickly spread to other cities where fundamental hubs of the
movement were formed such as in Barcelona ( Acampada BCN).
15-M represents the most intense moments of the anti- austerity
protests during the 2008–2014 period. It can be said that, in 2014,
particularly after a massive ‘March for Dignity’ (22 March 2014,
Madrid), mobilisation on the streets had declined and gave way to a
renewed interest in the electoral scene.
The emergence of new political groups that presented themselves as
an alternative to the two major parties and the forthcoming elections
helps explain the dissipation of the protests.
In this chapter we analyse the use of social media during this period
from the perspective of the relationship between social media’s role
in, on the one hand, the mobilisation of protest and, on the other, the
surveillance of activism. In this respect, it is important to highlight the
methodological limitations attached to the lack of transparency and
difficulty accessing official data on surveillance
22 Manuel Maroto and Alejandro Segura
practices in Spain.2 Surveillance practices by Spanish police and
intelligence agencies are very scarcely documented both in official
statistics and reports and scientific literature. The data remain in most
cases beyond the scope of the legislation on transparency and access
to public information. The Spanish law on transparency provides
indeed for exceptions on the right to access in those cases where
public security and ongoing criminal investigations are involved.
The more than questionable legality of surveillance of social
movements for political reasons adds additional obscurity to this kind

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 41 ---
of practice. The information analysed here covers the period of strong
mobilisation of the 15M movement, and is aimed at illustrating the
theoretical hypothesis on the ambivalence of social media for the
purposes of mobilisation. The study gives special weight to indirect
sources, in particular information and news reports appearing in the
media usually as a result of scandals regarding police and intelligence
services.
We will also present information from anonymous leaks to the media,
in some cases quite illustrative of surveillance dynamics. We will also
briefly describe some aspects of the legal regime of police and
intelligence surveillance, to contextualise the normative and
institutional background of these practices, as well as refer to
scholarly sources on social movements and data reported from social
network platforms.
Social media, an ambivalent instrument
In the second decade of the twenty- first century, after having reached
an extremely high number of users, social media have become a
fundamental instrument for expressing public discontent through
organised movements that demand more democracy and social rights.
The Arab Spring in countries such as Tunisia or Egypt, Occupy Wall
Street in the US, Yosoy132 in Mexico, or the Kitchenware Revolution
in Iceland are just some examples. In the case of 15-M
in Spain, social media and mobile phones make up the main material
infrastructure of its communication model. It would be impossible to
comprehend the organisation and development of these public protest
movements without considering the role of social media in its online
communication practices and strategies (Monterde & Postill, 2013).
Castells (2009) had already identified the many to many horizontal
communication model as mass self- communication, in comparison with
the from one person to many people vertical model that is more typical
of the press, television and radio. With the subsequent emergence of
all these citizen initiatives, this author then argued that digital
networks and wireless platforms endow social movements with
communicative autonomy to coordinate actions that evade the control
that governments and corporations have over traditional mass media
(Castells, 2009).
On the other hand, other authors such as Morozov (2012) argue that
digital media, far from evading this control, favour the proliferation of
surveillance mechanisms. Although he does not question the

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 42 ---
conceptualisation of Castells’s
Mobilisation and surveillance on social media 23
horizontal communication model, Morozov sees surveillance as an
inherent part of digital environments, adopting different formulas
depending on the system in which it is operating. These formulas
involve direct surveillance by states, as well as alliances between
states and big internet corporations such as Facebook.
The latter has been shown by Edward Snowden’s revelations about the
US government’s widespread espionage practices, and Morozov (2014)
believes that it could become more common as the development of
data processing technologies puts these companies at the forefront of
algorithmic surveillance. In a similar way, from a very different
stance, the Executive Chairman of Google, Eric Schmidt, and the
President of the company’s geostrategic think tank, Jared Cohen,
indirectly draw similar conclusions when stating that, ‘in countries
where the government is very technically capable, dissidents may feel
as vulnerable online as they do on the streets’ (Schmidt & Cohen,
2014, p. 150).
Ultimately, we are facing an ambivalent state of affairs where social
media have become crucial for uniting masses of people around a
common cause while, at the same time, they are being established as a
potential tool for controlling the activists behind these causes. In the
case of Spain, it is during the short period analysed here when these
media become decisive social mobilisation tools and when they start
to acquire more prominence as state surveillance tools.
The mobilising role of social media
The use of social media as a technopolitical innovation reaches its
height in Spain with the 15-M movement. The movement’s success at
mobilising masses of people indicates that social media, while not
exhaustive, are key to understanding these events. At these protests,
there were no leaders in the media or recognisable faces. Behind them
there were no trade unions or political parties.
Nor had they been announced by the press, television or radio. Being
a self-organised movement, it was able to exploit social networks as a
vehicle to articulate an extensive organisation, dissemination and
participation strategy that was capable of undermining the limits to
coverage imposed by mass media.
This is demonstrated by 94 per cent of 15-M supporters stating that at

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 43 ---
some point they had participated in the protests through social media
(Gather Estudios, 2011).
Social media encourages users to go beyond the role of receivers,
enabling them to act as transmitters at the same time (Aparici, 2010).
The digital stage involves the proliferation of entry and exit points for
information on the circuits of mass communication. Information is no
longer transmitted vertically and communication processes in public
space are adopting more horizontal formulas. When the mass self-
communication described by Castells (2009) arrives on the scene, a
complex dynamic arises in which the horizontal and vertical formulas
converge and mutually affect one another. The way information
travels through the (both material and symbolic) public space now
appears to be
24 Manuel Maroto and Alejandro Segura
strongly influenced by the ability of social media users to interact with
it.
This is not only because users can edit and remix content distributed
by traditional media and then recirculate it; it is also because users
can become reporters, creating their own content and associating with
other actors on the Web who function as a soundboard. This is in line
with the idea that journalism in the digital communication era has
become a big online conversation (Gillmor, 2004).
What happened in Spain was directly influenced by various citizen
protests that were taking place in other parts of the world. There are
many similarities but the most obvious are the strategies of protest
camps and occupying symbolic places (Hardt and Negri, 2012).
However, beyond this and the social, political and economic factors
that help explain the rise of these initiatives (systemic crisis,
questioning of the model of government, high rates of youth
unemployment, etc.), what is interesting to highlight are the
coincidences with respect to the role of social media. As the latter
operate on information flows, they transform the way news is created
and how political narratives are developed in the public sphere.
In the case of the Spanish 15-M, the Institute for Biocomputation and
Physics of Complex Systems (BIFI) statistically analysed the messages
exchanged on Twitter between 26 April and 25 May 2011 by
searching key-words related to the protests. The BIFI study (2011)
reveals a reticulated fabric in which information flows around
influential nodes, represented by hashtags, that allude to activist

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 44 ---
communities and other social agents that form part of the information
system under examination. According to this research, the interactive
structure of the more active nodes concerning 15-M includes tags
related to organisations directly connected to the protests (e.g.
#democraciareal), as well as political parties (e.g. PSOE), the media
(e.g. #elpaís), journalists (e.g.
#iescolar) and popular figures (e.g. #Berto_Romero). This means that
the activists’ digital productions – catchphrases, slogans, photos and
videos of the demonstrations and protest camps – went viral at an
exponential rate as they were intertwined with influential social
agents from other media who, even if they did not belong to the
movement, boosted the circulation of these messages both in
traditional and social media. Hence, we could speak of a transmedia
narrative strategy in which key elements of the story are designed
‘from below’ and distributed on the Web in order to exert influence
upwards; or an affectation and modelling process where activists-
cum-reporters grasp the potential for the movement to introduce the
symbolic elements of their story onto the largest media companies’
information circuit through Twitter, Facebook or YouTube.
In the same vein, we could consider the movement’s interest in
extending its communication practices beyond the squares and social
media. In a recent study, Barbas (2015) has identified and classified
23 communication projects that have emerged from 15-M. They are
generally modest press, television and radio projects aimed at
exploiting the convergent potential of the digital environment with
more classic means of information distribution. The transformation of
Mobilisation and surveillance on social media 25
information flows, provoked by these types of projects, is precisely
what made it possible to create a network capable of organising a
massive protest without the deliberate support of large media
corporations. In 15-M, social media were used as a tool to undermine
the capacity of these corporations – in strategic alliance with the state
and financial system – to impose a media blackout. This movement
demonstrated the potential of social networks to unite subjectivities
around shared emotions, organise the ‘Indignados’ protest, break the
fear barrier, and get a vast amount of the population to take to the
streets under a set of symbols and a discourse that pitched itself as an
alternative to the established institutional order (Castells, 2009).
In this sense, in Spain we could see the 15-M digital activists as the
first collective to exploit the potential of social media in the national

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 45 ---
political arena.
Much like in other places where these tools have been crucial for
organising protest, the existence in Spain of a cyberactivist culture
originating from the struggle for net neutrality, cyberfeminism, free
culture and, in general, global justice movements, was a decisive
precursor (Castells, 2012). On 20 February 2011, a group of citizen
platforms linked to these movements and with digital activist
experience joined together to create a Facebook group called ‘Pro
Mobilisation Groups Coordination Platform’ ( Plataforma de
Coordinación de Grupos Pro- Movilización). Subsequently, on 16 March,
this group became ‘Real Democracy Now’ ( Democracia Real Ya), the
main organiser of the initial protests. Using a method that permits the
study of migrations from some hashtags to others, the work of the
research group @Datanalysis15-M, coordinated by Toret (2015),
showed how a significant percentage of the people involved in these
initial movements went on to participate in 15-M. For example, 31 per
cent of the profiles that used #spanishrevolution had previously used
#noles-votes, a hashtag linked to the campaign against corruption in
the major political parties and the struggle against a new piece of
legislation on intellectual property ( Ley Sinde) aimed at regulating
downloads, which was met with fierce resistance from internet users
(Toret, 2015).
Nonetheless, social networks also unveiled new social control
mechanisms.
In the same way that Srinivasan (2012), after two years of fieldwork
in Egypt, maintained that the use of social media could no longer be
considered the domain of the rebellious sectors; in Spain, the
traditional political parties and law enforcement authorities are also
exploring the potential of these tools and gradually using them more
skilfully. Political parties are placing more and more importance on
digital electoral marketing, implementing strategies based on
monitoring the internet activity of their potential voters (Bennett,
2015). As we will see in the following, in the specific case of the
Spanish intelligence services and State Security Forces there is also a
heightened interest in developing surveillance and control strategies
for digital activism.
26 Manuel Maroto and Alejandro Segura
The use of social media as a surveillance tool
Probably due to the absence of publicly available official data on

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 46 ---
online surveillance practices, Spanish academic literature on the
repression of the last period of protest has focused attention more on
analysing the traditional offline police tactics, and more generally the
subjacent political and ideological evolution of control practices (Blay,
2013; Benedicto, 2015). Online surveillance of social movements is
also a relatively new phenomenon that, we will argue here, was still in
an early moment of development during the period analysed.
The 1978 Spanish constitution and legislation on data protection
prohibits ideological discrimination and protects the privacy and
secrecy of communications. The processing and storage in files of
personal data that reflects a person’s political preferences is also
illegal. Given these legal restrictions, the State Security Forces, Civil
Guard and National Intelligence Centre (CNI) deny conducting
surveillance of political activists (Europapress.es, 2011). When they
acknowledge having done so, it is in reference to ongoing criminal
investigations or security threats ( El Mundo, 2011). In accordance
with the provisions of the Spanish Criminal Procedure Act and the law
governing the CNI, for the interception of communications to be legal,
a judge must grant authorisation, either in the course of an
investigation of possible criminal offences or intelligence activities
dealing with threats to state security. However, there are various
mechanisms whereby the scope of surveillance extends beyond these
cases to people participating in social protests:
a monitoring without judicial control in the course of generic police
procedures of prevention and enquiry;
b police use of ‘open sources’ such as public information published by
users in social networks (e.g. tweets, public posts in Facebook);
c the breadth of intelligence activities conducted in the interest of
national security and antiterrorism strategies, and especially within
the scope of the CNI;
d contracting companies that provide monitoring services;
e the recent broadening of the scope of terrorism and public disorder
offences to include acts that verge on the exercise of fundamental
rights, particularly with the amendments to the Criminal Code in
2015.
The protesters accepted government surveillance as a reality from an
early stage (Martín Hernández, 2012). At the assemblies held in public
places across the country from 2011, it was common to assume that

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 47 ---
among the participants there could have been ‘infiltrators’ from state
institutions.3 Such covert operations were confirmed on various
occasions. In May 2012, Catalonian police disguised as protestors
started using visible police arm bands, a tactic aimed, according to the
authorities, at sending out ‘the message that there are a lot of plain-
clothes police, i.e. that you can see the one with the arm band but not
the rest’ (Elperiodico.com,
Mobilisation and surveillance on social media 27
2012; Elpais.com, 2012a; Elmundo.es, 2012b). In another particularly
remarkable episode occurred during the ‘Occupy the Congress’
demonstration (25 September 2012, Madrid), the police forcefully
immobilised a hooded protester who was, in fact, an undercover
agent. This episode was captured in a video (which soon went viral)
showing the covert agent repeatedly shouting ‘¡Que soy compañero,
coño! ’
(‘Fuck, I am a colleague!’) (Elpais.com, 2012).
In terms of online surveillance, there are several telling episodes of
how the activities of these social movements were monitored. Some of
these episodes are known because of what police or intelligence
sources told the press. For example, on 9 June 2011, one of the first
concentrations after the dismantling of the protest camp in Puerta del
Sol was held in front of the Congress of Deputies.
During an assembly of more than 2000 people, the CNI, which was
monitoring social networks, alerted police and the congress’ security
services of ‘the sudden rise in the exchange of online messages
referring to an attempt to attack the congress building’ (La
Vanguardia, 2011). This led to the deployment of up to 50 armoured
police vehicles and the Minister of the Interior maintaining contact
with the President of the Congress all evening.
In May 2012, the National Police announced the creation of a special
unit for monitoring activist networks with the aim of conducting
prediction activities and getting ‘ahead of events’, investigating the
calls to set up protest camps and the ‘most active internet users who
support and disseminate them’
(20minutos.com, 2012). ‘Social networks are open networks and they
are being investigated to get ahead events. It is about prediction’, said
a police spokes-person in statements to the press (20minutos.com,
2012). The publication of this information gave rise to various

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 48 ---
questions in the Congress of Deputies about the content and scope of
the investigation. The Minister of the Interior denied these
surveillance operations, acknowledging the surveillance of ‘anti-
systemic groups’ that acted ‘as part of the activities of the so- called
“Movement” ’ (Congreso.es, 2012, p. 454).
Months before, in June 2011, the National Police had revealed that it
carried out digital monitoring to identify and detain the suspected
‘leaders’ of the Spanish cyberactivist collective ‘Anonymous’, including
‘searches with false
“nicks” in chat rooms, telephone tapping and even face- to-face
encounters during the Puerta del Sol protest camp’ (Elmundo.es,
2011). The report also indicated the intensive use of SITEL, an
acronym for ‘Integrated Telephone Monitoring System’. This
controversial software for intercepting communications was developed
by Ericsson and has been used in Spain for many years along with
other similar monitoring programs (La Marea, 2014). The
announcement of the arrest of the ‘leaders of Anonymous’ in Spain
was received with scepticism by activists, who saw the operation as an
attempt to manipulate public opinion, as well as a reflection of the
authorities’ ignorance of how a leaderless and non- hierarchical
collective such as Anonymous works.
Another source of information on online surveillance operations has
been confidential information leaked by cyberactivists. A rather
important example
28 Manuel Maroto and Alejandro Segura
regarding the online monitoring of protesters was the leaking by
Anonymous, in October 2013, of documents from the Catalan
Information Security Centre (CESICAT) (Int.15-Mbcn.org, 2013). The
leaked documents revealed that CESICAT had monitored 15-M
activists, journalists and political figures, compiling information on
protest camps, calls to protest and various events. It is significant that
the leaked surveillance reports, mostly based on public information
obtained from social networks, were considered by the activists (in a
reaction not unlike that to the arrest of the ‘leaders of Anonymous’) as
poor in quality and an example of the intelligence services’
unsophisticated use of social networks in comparison to the protest
movements (Diagonal, 2013).
The Wikileaks (2015) revelation of a million emails from the Italian
company Hacking Team has also provided information. The

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 49 ---
documents published by Wikileaks in relation to Spain reveal that the
National Police and the CNI contracted (for amounts of up to €3.4
million in the second case) the services of the company to conduct
online surveillance. Especially notable is the use of the Remote
Control System programme, which is a Trojan capable of giving
almost full control over the infected user’s communications (Xnet-
x.net, 1200
