Mobilisation and surveillance on social media 31
The relationship between mobilisation and
repression and the ambivalent activist resistance
and adaptation to surveillance practices
There are at least two significant reasons to analyse the ambivalent
importance of the online surveillance of 15-M. On the one hand, the

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 56 ---
centrality of internet use for the protesters and, on the other, the
state’s interest in resorting to means of control that are less visible
than direct police intervention.
It is important to remember that the foundational event of 15-M, the
camp protest in Puerta del Sol in Madrid, took place as a response to
the police disband-ing the protests organised on 15 and 16 May 2011.
The use of force by the police, widely discussed on social networks,
had a mobilising effect that expanded the scope of the protest to the
whole country. The increased social and media visibility of harsher
forms of social control (prohibition of demonstrations, aggressive riot
control tactics, stop and search, detention and arrests, criminal
prosecution) made state institutions more inclined to resort to less
‘spectacular’ means of intervention: fines, infiltrating movements and
protests, or imposing different types of bureaucratic hurdles (such as
the requirement to announce all protests in advance, changing protest
routes, prohibiting the use of megaphones or distribution of flyers in
accordance with the by- laws relating to noise and cleanliness, etc.;
see Maroto Calatayud, 2013; Oliver Olmo & Urda Lozano, 2015a).
The literature on social movements (Davenport, Johnston & Mueller,
2005; Earl, 2013) has focused considerable attention on the
relationship between repression and mobilisation. Most of these
studies assume that institutional repression arises as a response to a
perceived threat, and that this repression simultaneously has the effect
of demobilising social movements, or at least altering their tactics.
However, certain forms of repression can generate an even greater
mobilisation against them (della Porta & Diani, 2009). This seems to
be the case of 15-M in 2011, from the perspective of subsequent
analysis and in the minds of some of the participants in the events
(Grueso, 2012). In summary, the relationship between repression and
mobilisation forms a complex continuum: mobilisation generates
repression, which at the same time may provoke new expressions of
resistance, and this resistance may then produce police and
institutional adaptation to it (Davenport et al., 2005).
In the Spanish case, it is difficult to argue that the government’s use of
the internet as a surveillance tool in the 2011–2014 period played a
decisive role in demobilising the protest movement or that it had a
very significant impact on the reshaping of activist tactics and culture.
The factors that may have been influential are, on the one hand, the
early lack of adaptation by institutional control mechanisms to the use
of social media and, on the other, the importance of inclusiveness for
the movement: when concerns for security and semi-secrecy arose, 15-
M promoted, including in digital environments, open and transparent

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 57 ---
participation practices, encouraged by the strong social legitimacy of
the protests.
32 Manuel Maroto and Alejandro Segura
In their study of the effects of surveillance on the rights to assemble
and protest, Starr et al. (2008) list six different areas in which
surveillance can have harmful effects on social movements: resources
(material and human), political opportunities, framing, protest
culture, political consciousness and organisation of the movement.
None of these six areas appears to demonstrate how surveillance
practices could have played a decisive role in the subsequent
demobilisation of the movement.
One of the strategies against the negative impact of police infiltration
on participation ( resources) was to accept that face- to-face and digital
communication could (or even should) involve anyone . At assemblies
it was common to greet and question the people presumed to be
undercover police officers as a means to alleviate tension (Elpais.com,
2011). Episodes of police infiltration were also exploited to publicly
denounce the authorities’ bad practices and accuse them of provoking
violent behaviours (Cuartopoder.es 2012) as well as to engage in
creative responses (e.g. as a reaction to the already mentioned arm-
bands of the covert Catalonian police, protestors adopted similar ones
identifying themselves as ‘peaceful citizens’; see Elmundo.es, 2012b).
This helped the 15-M movement reaffirm its peaceful nature and stifle
attempts to stigmatise it.
That the authorities found out about each call to assemble on social
media was used in a similar way. These announcements were made
openly on the internet and often without notifying authorities in
advance, thus adopting a strict interpretation of the constitutional
principal that notification is only necessary for publicity purposes
(Legal Sol, 2013a).
Perhaps it is in the area of the redefinition of political opportunities
where soft forms of repression were most evident. The early creation
of legal support teams (coordinated around the Sol Legal Commission
in the case of Madrid) made it possible to publicise and politicise the
problem of administrative fines, as well as to deal with the numerous
legal proceedings against activists (Legal Sol, 2013b).
However, the criminalisation of peaceful protest is one of the
adaptation strategies that the Spanish authorities have most clearly
adopted and with relative success. The expansion in scope of public

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 58 ---
order offences operated through the 2015 reform of the Criminal
Code, together with the introduction that very same year of the new
Law on Protection of Citizen Security clearly points at consolidating
the previous increase on police and administrative pressure on
demonstrations, which was reluctantly received by the judiciary
(Maroto Calatayud, 2016; Oliver Olmo & Urda Lozano, 2015b).
The rejection of organisational strategies based on secrecy and
suspicion in favour of encouraging inclusiveness, transparency and
solidarity undermined attempts to cast protesters in a negative light,
despite the sustained effort of conservative media. This prevented a
move towards exclusive organisation models that excessively privilege
the participation of those who are more politically aware and active (
protest culture) and created an extraordinary opportunity for the
political socialisation of several generations of Spanish people (
political consciousness). Although the demobilisation of the movement
in 2014 marked https://sanet.st/blogs/polatebooks/
Mobilisation and surveillance on social media 33
the end of the protests, it was also the beginning of a new political
momentum in Spain. Many of the protesters have subsequently taken
part in institutional politics, particularly from 2014 with the
emergence of new parties that are connected to 15-M, such as
Podemos, Ahora Madrid or Barcelona en Común.
This does not imply that within 15-M there was no specific interest in
online organisation and privacy- enhancing technologies. This sector
of the movement was represented by hacktivists and organised around
the 15Hack commission. In this sense, over the past few years, digital
activism and security toolkits have proliferated. However, they do not
reflect a resistance logic derived from the experience of protest
surveillance in Spain. The various texts published go beyond the
security question and focus on the political aspects of technology
(Padilla, 2012) or the international surveillance apparatus as a threat
to dissidence in general (Peirano, 2015).
Finally, in terms of technological activism, the development and use of
the movement’s own communication platforms and tools was also
very important (tomalaplaza.net, #15-Mpedia, N- 1, Mumble). One of
the most notable cases during 15-M was the social network N- 1,
based on Lorea. N- 1 is a free software platform for activist groups
that was proposed as an alternative to Facebook and other social
networks (Fernández-Delgado, Franco & Haché, 2012). N- 1 had tens
of thousands of registered users in 2012, although its use never

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 59 ---
exceeded that of conventional platforms. In general, 15-M activists
used commercial and proprietary software social networks such as
Twitter or Facebook. The usability of popular commercial
communication tools appears to have taken precedence over concerns
for the security and confidentiality of communications.
Evolution of legal and police practices
There is no evidence to suggest that police and intelligence digital
surveillance practices during the 15-M protests were systematic or
indiscriminate; or that the Spanish authorities used ’big data’ analytics
to forecast events or predict behaviour. The scant data available on
the period analysed seem to reflect an approach of identifying and
monitoring relevant people during the protests, rather than mass
surveillance of the sector of the population participating. Police and
legal actions against activists and journalists depended on the visual
identification and tracking of people with conspicuous political
profiles more than the indiscriminate monitoring of activity on social
networks.
Surveillance practices appear to have focused more on classic forms of
control such as lists of activists (Diariocordoba.com, 2013) than on the
capture, analysis and visualisation of large sets of data, usually known
as ’big data’.
However, after these protests, it is important to be alert to the future
development of these practices in the international context of the
proliferation of mass surveillance technology. Several aspects indicate
an evolution towards surveillance and control mechanisms fit for a
post- Snowden world (Lyon, 2014; Segura, 2014).
34 Manuel Maroto and Alejandro Segura
The first of these mechanisms is the institutional response of
criminalising peaceful protest. In fact, this has been one of the most
obvious adaptation strategies adopted by authorities through
amendments in 2015 to the Criminal Code and the new Public
Security Act (nicknamed the ‘gag law’). Practically and sym-bolically
these amendments seek to limit the scope of what is considered
legitimate political expression. Announced during the most intense
moments of 15-M, they were passed in 2015 despite strong social
backlash and criticism from institutions such as the Council of State
and the Council of Prosecutors. Both amendments potentially turn a
large part of the protest actions that characterised 15-M

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 60 ---
into unlawful conduct, as well as broadening executive powers to
sanction in detriment to judicial control (Oliver Olmo & Urda Lozano,
2015a, 2015b).
Therefore, in the legal definition of the scope of legitimate protest,
currently the repressive legal response has prevailed. The outcome of
the various appeals against the Public Security Act on the grounds of
unconstitutionality is yet to be seen.
The scope and repercussions of its practical application are also still
unknown. In the meantime, it boasts an incredible sum of up to 30
people sanctioned every day for lack of respect towards the authorities
(Eldiario.es, 2016a).
Some recent episodes confirm the trend towards criminal control of
activity on social networks for political purposes: this is the case of the
campaign against the city councillor from Ahora Madrid, also
connected to 15-M, Guillermo Zapata, who was prosecuted for
exalting terrorism due to the publication of poor taste jokes on his
Twitter account several years ago.4 The persecution of the criminal
offence of exalting terrorism for messages published on Twitter or
Facebook has increased significantly in Spain. Dozens of people have
been detained in various recent police operations (Eldiario.es, 2016b).
On the other hand, in the area of the development of new
technologies, it is relevant to note the purchase of the software
Evident X- Stream by the Ministry of the Interior in September 2015 .
This is an electronic communications surveillance and interception
programme developed by the British company BAE
Systems. It cost the institution €11 million and was expected to be in
operation by 2017. As revealed by the user guide leaked by Wikileaks
(2011), this software
– possibly destined to substitute SITEL – is still based on judicially
controlled surveillance of certain individuals with the collaboration of
various communications operators, rather than involving mass
indiscriminate data interception. However, the recent amendments to
the Criminal Procedure Act establish the possibility of tapping
communications in the case of investigations into offences with at
least three- year prison sentences or those committed as part of a
criminal or terrorist group/organisation. This broadens the possibility
of taking these measures beyond organised crime and terrorism, which
are already loosely defined areas.

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 61 ---
Nevertheless, research on the development of big data processing
technologies for data obtained from open sources is essential, in
particular for open data obtained from social networks, where police
and intelligence authorities can operate freely and without judicial
control, making the surveillance practices for open communications
on the internet similar to those observed in public spaces
Mobilisation and surveillance on social media 35
(Ruiz, 2014). By way of example, in 2016 the Spanish Police
Foundation awarded its annual research prize to the work ‘Automatic
identification of anonymous profiles on social networks using big data
techniques for semantic recognition’ (Interior.gob.es, 2016). At the
awards ceremony, the Director General of Police stated, in reference
to the challenge the rise of social networks poses for public security,
that ‘the difficulty lies in correctly managing this big data to generate
intelligence and be able to do effective police work’. These kinds of
developments indicate the consolidation of police predictive
technologies that use masses of data. Already present in other
countries (Joh, 2015), these technologies are the inheritors of a
problematic preventative tradition of social control (Harcourt, 2008;
García García & Ávila Cantos, 2016) and ‘computational criminology’
based on big data with enormous social, political and constitutional
implications.
Conclusions
Using the information gathered in this work we have been able to
describe the progressive incorporation of social media monitoring into
the surveillance practices of the Spanish police and intelligence
services. It may be true that there is not sufficient evidence to argue
that these practices were decisive during the protests analysed;
however, it is no less true that we could see this as a germinal period
that has notably stimulated the Spanish authorities’ interest in
exploiting the surveillance potential of social media. The increase of
information requests to the main social media platforms, the creation
of lists using data collected on social networks, the implementation of
the most sophisticated tracking software and the gradual interest in
experimenting with big data technologies all point in this direction.
From the perspective of the participants in the movement, it is also
impossible to state with certainty that the concerns for being observed
had a deterring effect on protest. In this respect, the ambivalent
mobilisation–repression relationship seems to have revolved more
around the use of strategies aimed at socially legitimating protest in

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 62 ---
order to combat those that sought to criminalise it. Even so, as we
have confirmed, now that the Web is the new communication
medium, and given the open space for expression it creates, the
question of transparency as a double- edged instrument that poses
both potential and risks might be the central element in the future
problematisation of this relationship.
The commitment to the transparency of communications on social
networks has helped encourage the organisation of public
demonstrations and strengthen the legitimation of the Spanish anti-
austerity protests. However, at the same time, the period addressed
sees the beginning of the development of more sophisticated and
invasive technological surveillance devices that, in collusion with the
media, legal and punitive strategies discussed throughout this chapter,
and particularly with the climate of increasing reliance on big data
predictive analytics to deal with ‘online radicalization’, are set to
shape more intrusive forms of social control.

Chapter 2
#RahmRepNow
Social media and the campaign to win
reparations for Chicago police
torture survivors, 2013–2015

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 71 ---
Andrew S. Baer
Introduction

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 72 ---
Between 2013 and 2015, a decades- old social movement for police
accountability in Chicago incorporated social media into a successful
campaign to win reparations for survivors of police torture. In this
chapter, social media refers to social network sites, particularly
Twitter and Facebook, that allow individuals to disseminate
information and opinions to other users (boyd & Ellison, 2007).
Twitter, for example, allows users to share or view online posts, called
Tweets, affixed with an identical marker called a hashtag (#).
Hashtags let users locate posts sorted around a related theme.1
Adopting the slogan ‘Reparations Now!’
to pressure Chicago mayor Rahm Emanuel and the City Council to
pass a comprehensive reparations ordinance proposed a year earlier,
activists from a group called Chicago Torture Justice Memorials
launched the Twitter hashtag
#RahmRepNow in October 2014.2 Amidst widespread agitation for
police accountability across the United States, the local
#RahmRepNow campaign
culminated in the bill’s passage on May 6, 2015.3
Rather than supplanting traditional repertoires of contention, social
media supplemented old practices. In the hands of experienced activists
and young volunteers alike, social network sites introduced a new set
of tools to an established toolkit. Social media helped activists frame
their own narrative, recruit supporters, publicize events, and
brainstorm strategy. It provided a space –
online – where activists could participate in the movement even when
unable to attend events in person. Although traditional media outlets
often ignored the movement, activists disseminated an alternative
media on the internet. In addition to bringing pressure on public
officials, social media also helped the movement document, preserve
and commemorate the struggle. Through surviving web pages, Tweets,
and online links, researchers can access a unique online archive,
complete with real- time reactions to historical events as they
unfolded.
During the short duration of the #RahmRepNow campaign, activists
avoided some of the potential pitfalls of social media, particularly its
age and class bias, vulnerability to state surveillance, and cult of
personality.

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 73 ---
#RahmRepNow 41
Methods
This chapter employs qualitative analysis of the Twitter feed
#RahmRepNow from its launch in late October 2014 through passage
of the reparations ordinance in early May 2015. Over the course of
this six- month period, hundreds of users affixed the #RahmRepNow
hashtag to over 5500 Tweets.4 To contextualize and complement
these posts, this chapter also consults Facebook messages; archival
manuscript collections;5 reports and memoranda from official
investiga-
tions;6 legal briefs and other documents provided by the People’s Law
Office;7
articles from corporate and alternative newspapers; as well as
participant observation of several events hosted by social movement
organizations. The author also performed three oral history interviews
with participants, including Alice Kim, a veteran activist whose career
bridged older and newer campaigns in the larger Chicago torture
justice movement; Lauren Taylor, a younger activist new to the Burge
scandal; and Mark Clements, a torture survivor and experienced
activist in his own right.
The Jon Burge police torture scandal
The #RahmRepNow campaign was an outgrowth of a larger
movement to win justice in the aftermath of a well- known local saga.
From 1972 to 1991 and beyond, a group of white detectives working
under supervisor Jon Burge tortured or otherwise coerced confessions
from over 118 African Amer ican criminal suspects on Chicago’s
majority- black South and West Sides. In pursuit of confessions in
serious felony cases, detectives regularly resorted to intimidation,
threats and physical beatings; mock execution, Russian Roulette, and
suffocation with a plastic typewriter cover; and shocks from a variety
of rigged electrical devices (Taylor, 2012).8 As early as 1990, an
internal investigator concluded, ‘The type of abuse described was not
limited to the usual beating, but went into such eso-teric areas as
psychological techniques and planned torture.’9 Nearly all of the
dozens of detectives involved were white. Virtually all alleged victims
were black. Scores of torture survivors were later convicted on the
strength of coerced confessions. Over a dozen wound up on death row,
including several who later won their release. Despite official efforts
to depict Burge as a rogue outlier among an otherwise upstanding

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 74 ---
department, the torture scandal reflected the systemic racism of law
enforcement in Chicago and implicated other public officials as well,
including patrol officers, prosecutors and judges, as well as several
mayors and state’s attorneys (Taylor, 2015).
Following disclosure of the torture scandal in 1989, an evolving
coalition of activists organized a series of campaigns for justice. For
over 25 years and counting, torture survivors, their family members,
civil rights attorneys, community activists, and various allies fought to
end police torture, corroborate and publicize the scandal, punish
perpetrators, win judicial hearings for survivors still behind bars,
abolish the state’s death penalty, and provide services for impacted
https://sanet.st/blogs/polatebooks/
42 Andrew S. Baer
communities. One of the movement’s greatest accomplishments
involved changing the public narrative of the torture cases from denial
and justification to outrage and healing. When allegations first
surfaced in the 1980s, most local powerbrokers and opinion makers
supported the accused officers, highlighted the violent crimes
attributed to the torture survivors, and made little effort to check the
use of illegal interrogation. By the 2010s, however, an alternative
narrative promulgated by community activists dominated the official
response to the ongoing scandal. Abandoning years of denials and
justifications, officials echoed the movement’s assertion that torture
represented a gross violation of civil liberties and human rights in all
cases, even when the abused suspect was likely guilty of a violent
crime. Responding to changes in the larger political opportunity
structure between 1989 and 2015, particularly the proliferation of
high- profile exonerations in the 1990s and the rise of the Black Lives
Matter movement in the 2010s, the Chicago torture justice movement
moved the official response from impunity to reparations (Baer,
2015).
While social media played an important role from 2013 to 2015, the
reparations campaign represented simply the latest struggle in a
longer fight for justice in the Burge cases. Indeed, the anti- torture
movement that emerged in the wake of the Burge scandal evolved
from older traditions of confronting racist policing in Cook County,
Illinois. Many of the constituent parts of the Chicago torture justice
movement – both individuals and organizations – had roots in the
New Left of the 1960s and 1970s, particularly in civil rights, Black
Power, anti- war, feminist, gay rights and labor organizations, with
long experience fighting for social justice through collective action.

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 75 ---
For example, the People’s Law Office (PLO), founded in 1968, spent
the 1970s and 1980s representing various militants, radicals, and
ordinary people against local officials in Chicago and elsewhere.10 By
the late 1980s, the PLO had earned a reputation for helping unpopular
plaintiffs sue the Chicago Police Department (CPD) for civil rights
violations (Stainthorpe & Taylor, 1998). Meanwhile, Citizens Alert, a
police watchdog organization founded in 1967, responded to a steady
stream of police corruption and brutality throughout the 1970s and
1980s.11
When the PLO exposed the torture crisis during a civil suit brought by
a torture survivor in 1989, Citizens Alert formed a Coalition to End
Police Torture and Brutality. Over 50 organizations with years – even
decades – of experience fighting racism in the local criminal justice
system leapt into action, including Act Up, the Amer ican Friends
Service Committee, the Illinois Coalition Against the Death Penalty,
the Jewish Council on Urban Affairs, the National Council of
Churches, the National Lawyers Guild, Operation PUSH, and dozens
more.12
Together, the PLO, Citizens Alert, and their growing coalition of local
radicals and progressives rejected the official response to the torture
allegations, countered the steady stream of denials, combined
litigation with direct action, and forced the city to respond in a
meaningful way. By the time this multiracial, multigenera-tional
movement shifted gears to launch the reparations campaign in 2013,
activists had learned from numerous victories and defeats.
#RahmRepNow 43
Social movement theory and the Chicago torture
justice movement
For over 25 years after 1989, the Chicago torture justice movement
demonstrated social movement theory in practice. According to
Sidney Tarrow (1998), ‘social movements are repositories of
knowledge’ about how to organize around collective grievances using
established ‘repertoires of contention.’
Charles Tilly (2008) writes, ‘In a given time and place, people learn a
limited number of claim- making performances, then mostly stick with
those performances when the time to make claims arrives.’ In Chicago
during the 1990s and 2000s, for example, organizations such as
Citizens Alert and the Campaign to End the Death Penalty fought for

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 76 ---
justice in the Burge scandal by organizing marches and protest rallies;
filing petitions; publishing newsletters; distributing fliers; holding
monthly meetings or annual conferences; launching art exhibits; and
raising funds through membership fees, mass mailings or benefits. As
technologies change, however, social movements adapt. ‘When people
make collective claims,’ Tilly (2008) explains, ‘they innovate within
limits set by the repertoire already established for their place [and]
time.’ The Chicago torture justice movement began adding
communications technology to its repertoire in the 2000s. By the
launch of the #RahmRepNow campaign in 2014, social media had
come to complement the movement’s traditional tactics, largely by
making it easier for what Paolo Gerbaudo (2012) describes as ‘soft
leaders,’ or ‘choreo-graphers,’ to coordinate collective action. If social
media promises to radically transform protest in a digital age, a true
revolution in collective action will be a gradual, not sudden, process
in the United States.
Reparations for Burge survivors
While the Chicago torture justice movement focused on immediate
crises throughout the 1990s and 2000s – including rescuing Burge
survivors on death row and winning freedom for the wrongfully
convicted – the call for reparations in the Burge scandal tapped into a
long struggle to win restorative justice for victims of racist violence in
the United States. As early as 1991, members of Citizens Alert
discussed the need for comprehensive repair, including financial
compensation and other services for survivors, their families and
communities. 13
Attorney and activist Standish Willis organized Black People Against
Police Torture (BPAPT) in 2005. For the next four years, BPAPT
fought to bring international attention to the Burge scandal and other
police crimes in Chicago.
Willis drafted the Illinois Reparations for Police Torture Victims Act
and pushed for its passage in the state assembly. Among other
provisions, the bill called for the creation of a counseling center,
psychological treatment and vocational assistance for affected
communities, public education, and the establish-ment of a state
commission to review the torture claims of men still behind
bars.14 Struck of much of these provisions, the bill passed in 2009
with only the
44 Andrew S. Baer

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 77 ---
state commission intact.15 Despite the limitations of the Illinois
Torture Inquiry and Relief Commission, Willis’s vision of holistic
reparations for Burge torture survivors proved influential. Many of his
ideas continued to circulate among local activists even as other goals
competed for attention.
In 2010, activists, attorneys and artists founded Chicago Torture
Justice Memorials (CTJM) to commemorate the struggle against police
torture in Chicago. CTJM brought young and old activists together in
a new context to usher in the next phase of the Chicago torture justice
movement following the conviction of Jon Burge in 2011. Abiding
established traditions of grassroots organizing, CTJM prioritized the
voices of black residents most directly impacted by police violence,
favoring the needs and interests of the torture survivors – their
friends, neighbors, and family members – when determining a course
of action. While CTJM’s first public act was a speculative call for art
proposals, the resulting exhibits featured the perspective of survivors
in recorded testimonials, live music, poetry readings, dance and hip-
hop performances, as well as works of visual media created or inspired
by the torture survivors themselves (CTJM, 2016).
If most participants found solace and solidarity in commemorating the
struggle, some questioned the group’s priorities. According to activist
Alice Kim, the memorial project received some pushback, as at least
one survivor told her, ‘We’re struggling and we need jobs and we need
healthcare and you guys want to make art?’16 While more than a
dozen survivors won large financial settlements in civil suits, many
received little or no compensation whatsoever and at least 20 others
remained in prison. Even those who served their time or won their
freedom continued to grapple with the emotional, psychological and
material challenges associated with the experience of police violence,
incarceration and poverty. While torture survivor Mark Clements
appreciated the work of lawyers and activists who helped expose his
wrongful conviction and win his release after 28 years in prison, he
also urged the movement to consider doing more to help exonerees
navigate the difficult transition to life on the outside.17
According to Clements, ‘They free you, they get you out … and they
give you the [cheap] suit. You stand there. You happy. They may take
you out to eat.
Now the rest is up to you.’18 Determined to let their constituents lead
the way, CTJM insisted the art exhibit double as ‘an exercise in
imagination,’ a collaborative project designed to ‘rethink what justice
looks like.’19 Armed with ideas raised through their call for proposals,

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 78 ---
CTJM leaders joined members of other local organizations at a retreat
on May 11, 2013, where they decided to launch a fresh campaign to
push an adapted form of Stan Willis’s state reparations bill through
the Chicago City Council (CTJM, 2016).
Activists turned to local allies to rally support for the bill, formally
introduced on October 16, 2013 (CTJM, 2016). Alderman Joe Moreno,
a known advocate for social justice and former member of the local
Campaign to End the Death Penalty, co- sponsored the bill alongside
several other city council members with reputations for supporting
progressive causes. While many
#RahmRepNow 45
members of the movement wondered if the bill stood a chance of
passing, they hoped that it would at least steer the local conversation
on police accountability in a new direction.20 There was, moreover,
some precedent for putting faith in the council chambers, where
sympathetic aldermen had sponsored hearings on the Burge scandal in
1989, 1990 and 1999.21 More recently, in January 2012, members of
the Illinois Coalition Against Torture (ICAT) successfully petitioned for
passage of a City Council resolution declaring Chicago a ‘torture- free
zone.’22
With a goal of securing at least 500 signatures, the coalition’s online
petition –
created ‘to reach people in and beyond Chicago’ – netted the names of
over 3500
supporters.23 Reflecting on ‘what we could have done better’ in the
wake of the resolution victory, ICAT suggested future movements
implement strategies to
‘build ties’ with other local and national groups working on ‘different
but related areas,’ pre- emptively ‘reach out to local officials,’ and
‘develop a media strategy’ to recruit allies.24 When the reparations
ordinance stalled in committee after garner-ing brief attention, the
coalition began heeding this earlier advice, working with affiliate
organizations, nurturing relationships with elected officials, and using
social media to disseminate information through an alternative press.
By summer 2014, however, the reparations push had grown stagnant
despite efforts to attract support through several creative acts of
advocacy and protest.

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 79 ---
From October to December 2013, supporters coordinated an art
exhibit highlighting similar movements in other contexts across the
globe. Special T- shirts circulated among supporters depicting the
Chicago municipal flag with an additional black star representing the
legacy of police torture. During a national meeting of Amnesty
International in April 2014, over 100 attendees paraded black signs
with the names of torture survivors printed in white around a plaza
across from City Hall. Yet the reparations ordinance remained under
wraps following a last- minute cancellation of a hearing of the finance
committee in March 2014 (CTJM, 2016). While CTJM continued to
expand its coalition and attract support from additional aldermen, it
would take changes in the larger political opportunity structure
triggered by national events to truly revive the reparations campaign
starting in late summer.
On August 9, 2014, white police officer Darren Wilson shot and killed
African Amer ican teenager Michael Brown in Ferguson, Missouri, a
suburb of St. Louis. The incident added Brown’s name to a long list of
black men and women – often unarmed – killed by law enforcement
officials or civilian vigilantes in recent years, a list including Sean Bell,
Oscar Grant and Trayvon Martin. When a grand jury failed to indict
the officer responsible for Brown’s death, Ferguson erupted in non-
violent protest, organized rebellion and uncoordinated violence.
Ferguson emerged as the center of a national movement organized in
concert with #BlackLivesMatter, coined following the 2013 acquit-tal
of Trayvon Martin’s killer, George Zimmerman. From August 2014
through the passage of the reparations bill in Chicago in May 2015,
issues of police violence surged to the fore of national attention even
as additional police killings
occurred in New York, Baltimore, South Carolina and elsewhere.25
46 Andrew S. Baer
By the time Ferguson became a bellwether for police abolition and
accountability movements, the reparations campaign in Chicago had
already drawn additional support from local organizations infused
with youth and radicalism.
Indeed, #RahmRepNow was but one of many social movement
campaigns opposing Mayor Emanuel’s closure of 50 black schools in
2013 and State’s Attorney Anita Alvarez’s ongoing failure to address
police violence.26 Membership in groups such as We Charge
Genocide, Project Nia and Black Youth Project 100 resisted easy
generalization, but consisted largely of young people in their teens,

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 80 ---
twenties and early thirties, mostly African Amer icans and other
people of color – but also young white progressives and radicals –
voicing various socialist, Marxist, feminist and queer critiques of
contemporary institutions. According to Project Nia founder Mariame
Kaba, activists in Chicago
‘used the energy and opening created by these ongoing protests to
reanimate existing long- term anti- police violence campaigns’ (CTJM,
2016). Alice Kim stated simply, ‘Our campaign was rejuvenated by
Black Lives Matter.’27 The
#RahmRepNow social media push emerged from this local and
national milieu.
#RahmRepNow
Seizing the opportunity presented by Black Lives Matter, the Chicago
reparations coalition launched the #RahmRepNow campaign in
October 2014 to direct the surge in activism toward winning the
concrete – yet deeply symbolic
– victory in Chicago. According to activist Lauren Taylor, the torture
justice movement saw ‘a window’ of opportunity open after Ferguson,
‘a historical moment in which something more ambitious could get
done.’28 Suddenly, the reparations coalition realized they could do
more than just start a conversation; maybe they could actually get the
bill passed. Alice Kim agreed, adding that
#RahmRepNow offered everyday people ‘a very specific concrete goal
that they could get their heads around and organize for.’29 Beginning
with a Twitter
‘power hour’ on October 24, 2014, supporters tweeted over 350
messages affixed with #RahmRepNow in order to move the
campaign’s hashtag to the top of the Twitter trending list. In a
characteristic tweet, local activist Page May wrote,
‘We’re demanding @ChicagoMayor pass an ordinance in the city
council for
#reparations for police torture victims.’30 For the next six months,
the campaign involved tweeting links to articles from traditional and
alternative media, circulating a petition, flooding the Twitter accounts
of public officials, and placing the reparations bill in historic, national,
and international context.31 The online campaign also corresponded
with an escalation of protest rallies, marches, teach- ins and other

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 81 ---
traditional methods of collective action.
Indeed, #RahmRepNow involved more than simply posting tweets.
Throughout the final six- month push preceding passage of the bill,
activists sought to couple social media to on- the-ground protests.
According to Lauren Taylor, ‘It became clear that there needed to be a
concerted campaign to escalate’ the movement with ‘repeated public
actions that kept the base engaged.’ Accordingly,
#RahmRepNow 47
activists planned public events to ‘[keep] the pressure on the
administration both through social media and through conventional
media, by having public visible
moments.’32 Discussing some of the public protests of the campaign,
Alice Kim perceived ‘an overstatement in the mainstream media …
and maybe even on the part of some activists, that Black Lives Matter,
or just activism, is [just] about social media [alone].’ On the contrary,
she clarified, ‘Without the actual, real actions, what would you be
Tweeting about anyway?’33 For example, just a few weeks after
CTJM, Amnesty International and other organizations intensified the
online component of the reparations push in late October 2014,
several members of the affiliated group We Charge Genocide (WCG)
traveled to Europe to present a report outlining evidence of police
violence in Chicago before the United Nations Committee Against
Torture (CAT).34 On November 28, 2014, the concluding report of the
CAT committee voiced concern over ongoing racist police violence in
Chicago and urged the City Council to pass the reparations ordin-
ance.35 Multiple #RahmRepNow followers disseminated news of this
document through Twitter.36 The reparations campaign confirmed
that social media activism and public activism worked best in tandem.
Viewing online protest and public protest as two sides of the same
coin, activists often concentrated their tweets during planned events,
including rallies, marches, and various teach- ins, dies- ins and sing-
ins. On December 16, 2014, dozens of marchers braved the Chicago
winter in a four- mile trek from police headquarters to City Hall,
where they delivered a petition to the Mayor’s Office signed by over
45,000 supporters. On January 15, 2015, activists held a sing- in and
press conference outside City Council chambers to pressure the
finance committee to act. When the council met in full session the
following week, some 50 supporters stood in silence as Alderman
Howard Brookins called for the bill to come out of committee. On the

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 82 ---
night of February 6, an affiliated group called the Chicago Light
Brigade stood in the dark outside Mayor Emanuel’s home holding
black signs spelling ‘Reparations Now’ in electric light, an act of
creative protest custom- made to play on social media. On Valentine’s
Day, supporters packed a downtown church for a ‘people’s hearing.’
Afterwards, activists lined the street across from City Hall bearing
signs emblazoned with names of individual torture survivors. In a
‘train takeover’ a week later, protesters rode the city’s mass transit
system promoting reparations among local passengers. Other events
followed, including an art exhibit inside City Hall and a month- long
series of teach- ins at area schools, churches and community centers
(CTJM, 2016). During each of these events, activists supplied real-
time accounts from start to finish, posting photographs, videos and
links to related articles on Twitter, Facebook and other social network
sites.
While online and conventional protest proved critical in pressuring
elected officials to endorse reparations, the movement also relied on
old- fashioned political negotiation.37 On February 17, just three days
after the people’s hearing inside Chicago Temple, the mayor’s senior
legal advisor contacted CTJM
lawyers to set up a meeting. On at least three occasions – February 23,
March 12
48 Andrew S. Baer
and April 2 – a group of negotiators from CTJM, Amnesty
International, Black People Against Police Torture, and the People’s
Law Office met in secret with public officials to hammer out details of
the proposed legislation (CTJM, 2016).
Lauren Taylor, also known as LT, described the development of a
division of labor within the reparations coalition as a kind of ‘inside-
outside game.’38 For example, CTJM leadership included an older,
middle- class contingent, such as attorneys Flint Taylor and Joey
Mogul, as well as Northwestern University pro-fessor Martha Biondi.
They joined activists Dorothy Burge, Ernest Coverson, and Jasmine
Heiss at insider meetings with city officials (CTJM, 2016). Meanwhile,
outsider groups continued to hurl tweets at the mayor’s Twitter
account from the gallery of finance committee hearings. According to
torture survivor Mark Clements, ‘I would be in the City Hall chambers
and your phone just constantly going off, and you can hear the
mayor’s going off, too [ sic].’39 LT

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 83 ---
explained, ‘CTJM has to be the ones to negotiate directly with the city,
so they have to stay on better terms … But We Charge Genocide can
be the younger, rowdier crew that’s organizing people and creating
that outside pressure.’40
Together, negotiation and protest forced elected officials to act on an
issue they would rather ignore.
Speaking on behalf of Rahm Emanuel during behind- the-scenes
meetings, the mayor’s top legal adviser agreed to nearly all of the
movement’s initial demands, balking only at the price tag. Willing to
compromise on the money, the movement’s representatives agreed to
lower their asking price from $20 million to $5.5 million, drawing the
line at $100,000 for each eligible torture survivor.41 Unaware of the
closed- door negotiations, one activist tweeted, ‘I can’t believe this is
actually happening,’ when the mayor announced his endorsement of
the ordinance during a packed finance committee hearing
on April 14, 2015.42
Titled ‘Reparations for Burge Torture Victims,’ the municipal
ordinance capped off the six- month #RahmRepNow campaign to
great fanfare and scattered criticism on May 6, 2015.43 Borrowing
almost wholesale from the language of community activists, the bill
represented a landmark achievement for the larger reparations
movement. The ordinance acknowledged the verity of the torture
scandal and highlighted its systemic nature. In addition to financial
payouts, some 57 claimants would also receive privileged access to
psychological counseling, healthcare and vocational training as well as
tuition- free enrolment in City Colleges for themselves, their children
and grandchildren. The ordinance required the City to offer an official
apology, erect a public memorial, create a permanent community
center to provide services for victims of police violence, and develop a
public school curriculum to teach the Burge scandal to eighth- and
tenth- grade students.44 Some torture survivors and their allies
criticized the bill for narrowing eligibility to only those men tortured
under Burge or his men between 1972 and 1991. Others objected that
the bill forced claimants to waive their right to sue the city for civil
damages or otherwise fell short of guaranteeing survivors’ income or
employment. As for the community center,
#RahmRepNow 49
public memorial and school curriculum, at least one follower tweeted,
‘I won’t lie: I’m concerned about the implementation.’45 Nevertheless,

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 84 ---
the reparations package demonstrated the efficacy of collective action
in pursuit of justice in cases of police violence.
While the actions of community activists – on the streets, in City Hall
and on the internet – proved vital to the bill’s passage, larger
circumstances altering the political opportunity structure played a
critical role as well. In addition to the emergence of the Black Lives
Matter movement, changes in local politics proved instrumental in
opening a window for action. First, the retirement of Mayor Richard
M. Daley in 2011 after 22 years in office proved a necessary pre-
condition to the passage of a municipal ordinance acknowledging the
city’s role in the Burge scandal. From 1980 to 1989 – the height of the
Burge torture regime – Daley served as Cook County State’s Attorney.
Despite knowledge of the torture allegations, his office employed
tainted confessions in criminal trials while failing to investigate
detectives for illegal interrogation methods. Serving as mayor from
1989 to 2011, Daley consistently wielded the power of City Hall to
deny the veracity of the Burge scandal and distance himself from
scrutiny for his role in the cover- up.46 Daley’s replacement by Rahm
Emanuel in 2011
removed a powerful barrier to action in the City Council. While
Emanuel differentiated himself from his predecessor by offering a
personal apology for the Burge scandal in September 2013, the new
mayor did not emerge as a champion for victims of police violence
and remained silent on the reparations bill long
after its October 2013 appearance.47
By the time activists launched #RahmRepNow in the fall of 2014,
however, Mayor Emanuel was gearing up for a February election and
appeared vulnerable to pressure on issues of police accountability.
Asked to speculate on how the movement broke Emanuel’s disinterest
with the reparations bill, Alice Kim replied, ‘I think it was … being
able to take advantage of the [mayoral] election and leverage it.’48
Less than a week before the polls opened, the British newspaper the
Guardian began printing a series of articles on the history of police
violence in Chicago, including an election- day bombshell accusing the
CPD of operating a ‘black site’ interrogation center in an unmarked
building known as Homan Square.49 On February 24, 2015, voters
shocked the city by turning out for challenger Jesus ‘Chuy’ Garcia in
unexpected numbers, forcing a runoff. In the interim, the mayor’s
representatives worked with CTJM to deflect protest and delay
decision on the reparations ordinance until after April 7. A week after
his runoff victory, a relieved Emanuel stood before the finance

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 85 ---
committee on April 14 and finally endorsed the bill. During his
speech, one activist tweeted,
‘The mayor is posturing and blathering. Fuck him all the way to hell.
As if he didn’t fight us every step of the way.’50 While the mayor
attempted to take credit for righting an historic wrong, activists
turned to Twitter to ridicule him and
celebrate the movement’s achievements.51
In retrospect, the shooting of 17-year- old Laquan McDonald on
October 20, 2014 probably also played a role in Emanuel’s change of
heart. While the public
50 Andrew S. Baer
did not see a police dashcam video of the shooting until a judge
ordered its release some 13 months later, the mayor’s staff knew about
the video within weeks and later actively suppressed it.52 Throughout
winter and spring, with the
#RahmRepNow campaign in full effect, the city’s lawyers negotiated a
financial settlement with McDonald’s family even as the mayor
focused on re- election.53
With hindsight, many activists believed the mayor used the
reparations bill simply to place himself out in front of a controversial
issue. Alice Kim imagined the mayor and his staff saying to
themselves, ‘If we go ahead on this now, then we can say that we’re
actually doing something and that we care about police
violence.’54 The McDonald shooting, the #RahmRepNow campaign
and the runoff election persuaded the mayor to endorse reparations as
a public relations tool. ‘I mean, up until the last minute, he was not
coming out in support of reparations,’ Kim argued, ‘It had stalled for a
year- and-a- half.’55 While observers debated details, the confluence
of a changing political opportunity structure, conventional protest and
social media pressure certainly opened a space for activists to win an
unprecedented victory.
Social media and the reparations campaign
While it can be difficult to disentangle various elements of a complex
social movement and assign credit to one strategy over another,
participants agreed that social media played a critical role in the
reparations push. In Chicago, activists came to describe social media –

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 86 ---
particularly Twitter – as an important new tool complementing an
established toolkit. For example, Twitter, Facebook and other sites
allowed activists to publicize the torture scandal, the history of
collective action in support of survivors, and the current reparations
campaign.
Social media offered activists means to rapidly disseminate
information to a wide audience of potential supporters, including
details of the bill, upcoming plans, and the outcome of previous
events. Social media became a platform to recruit supporters,
coordinate volunteers and rally protestors, easing logistical problems
of timing, location and procedure. It helped activists brainstorm
strategy, develop methods, and solicit creative ideas. By aiming tweets
at personal Twitter accounts, activists were able to lobby, educate,
and pressure public officials. Social media helped sustain interest by
keeping the campaign present in people’s daily lives, engaging
individuals even when they could not physically attend an event.
While activists managed these tasks long before the proliferation of
the internet, social media altered communications in important ways,
often making such transactions quicker and easier, if less intimate.
Beyond its concrete impact, social media also generated abstract
effects as well. For example, #RahmRepNow became a crucial framing
mechanism through which activists could shape public perception of
the stakes involved and link the present campaign to a longer history
of resistance. Social media helped shape and sustain an emerging
collective. For a finite moment, a diverse group of actors came
together to support one specific goal, even as disputes
#RahmRepNow 51
surfaced and divisions lingered. Social media thus helped create a
sense of community among activists despite differences of race, class,
gender, age, sexuality, nationality and ability. If this sense of
community lived more on social media than in reality, the internet
nevertheless helped connect people across distances real and
imagined. Indeed, through the experience and memory of public
events shared through social media, electronic connections helped
forge enduring networks to be employed in future struggles.
In addition to its many benefits, social media also came with potential
costs.
For example, some activists, particularly those of an older generation,
worried that people would stop attending public events, preferring to

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 87 ---
show their support by clicking a button from the comfort of home.
According to Alice Kim, ‘There was a lot of fear among some activists
that social media and hashtag activism was gonna replace the other
kinds of activism.’56 Indeed, organizers often witnessed hundreds of
Facebook users indicate plans to attend an event only to have less
than 50 actually show up.57 Flaky volunteers, of course, were not new
to the internet age. Yet other activists expressed more alarming
concerns. Initially ‘suspicious’
of Twitter, Lauren Taylor worried that it ‘allows the government to
track your social networks,’ introduces the ‘seduction of celebrity,’ and
perpetuates a ‘myth that when things get shared a bunch of times that
means something in and of
itself.’58 Yet the reparations coalition proved less vulnerable to these
pitfalls. The organizations involved, like the Black Lives Matter
movement generally, did not rally around the archetypical charismatic
leader. Answering critics who dismissed such organizations as
‘leaderless,’ members described themselves as ‘leader- ful.’59
The diffusion of responsibility and decision- making not only fostered
a democratic spirit, but also made it more difficult for opponents –
including the police – to target leaders. The moderate goals and short
duration of the #RahmRepNow campaign also militated against state
repression and the distraction of celebrity.
Social media also threatened to skew the voice of the movement
toward the young and middle class, potentially silencing supporters –
particularly older people and the working poor – who lacked the
resources, skill or habits required to participate. Anticipating this
problem, activists deliberately worked to include the voices of their
main constituency – torture survivors and their family members – who
otherwise remained underrepresented in social media.
Mark Clements, one of the only torture survivors with a public social
media presence, credited a woman employee at a law clinic for
teaching him how to use computers following his release from prison.
‘Without that,’ he concluded, ‘I would probably be like most people
who don’t use social media. You know, because she’s the one who
opened up the … same Twitter account I use today.’60
Despite the near absence of survivors and their families on the
#RahmRepNow Twitter feed, veteran activists often went out of their
way to include them in their tweets and Facebook posts. According to
Alice Kim,

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 88 ---
It was important to our campaign that survivors and family members
were a part of it. And survivors were at every single event that we did
… and had
52 Andrew S. Baer
a voice in thinking about what events should be and what was
important to
them.61
While Kim emerged as the person most likely to tweet an image of
torture survivors and their family members throughout the
#RahmRepNow campaign, other participants regularly included direct
quotes from survivors or mentioned them in posts from protest rallies
and other events. Nevertheless, actively facilitating the participation
of underrepresented groups remained a challenge of social media
activism in the Chicago torture justice movement.
Conclusion
During the #RahmRepNow campaign, an established social movement
incorporated social media in pursuit of a specific goal while grappling
with some of its drawbacks. Rather than supplant traditions of
collective action, social media supplemented established methods.
Websites such as Twitter and Facebook offered new tools for
publicizing the campaign, framing issues, disseminating information,
recruiting support, lobbying public officials, and brainstorming tactics.
One of social media’s more innovative and distinctive features
involved its ability to document, circulate and preserve the
movement’s actions and ideas. According to Lauren Taylor, ‘It’s a way
to be your own press’ and ‘bypass more centralized … media
outlets.’62 Frustrated by corporate media’s indifference or hostility,
activists turned to electronic communications technology to publish
their own newsletters and editorials. Alice Kim marveled, ‘We were
able to make our own media.’63 Surviving on the internet, the record
of the movement’s activity also provides a chronological archive of
real- time reactions to unfolding events, expediting researchers’
process of investigation and analysis.
The #RahmRepNow campaign offers an example of a qualified success
in an age of widespread local and national activism around issues of
police violence.
