Chapter 3
Cracks and reformations in the
Brazilian mediascape
Mídia NINJA, radical citizen journalism,
and resistance in Rio de Janeiro
Tucker Landesman and Stuart Davis
In June 2013, dozens of cities across Brazil erupted in dissent.
Rallying around the slogan ‘O Gigante Acordou’ (The Giant Has
Awakened), these protests were the largest displays of citizens taking
direct action since the end of the military dictatorship in the 1980s

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 96 ---
(Davis, 2016; Lima, 2013). From the cacophonous streets,
demonstrators raised generic and specific grievances, including
arbitrary fare- hikes for public transportation, entrenched
governmental corruption, and reckless public spending on the 2014
FIFA Men’s World Cup and the 2016 Summer Olympics. Beyond
complaints lodged against politicians and government, one of the most
common claims manifested in protest chants, on placards and social
media, was that the national news media method-ically and
manipulatively misrepresents the Brazilian citizenry.
Coverage of the urban unrest from international media outlets such as
The Wall Street Journal and Guardian noted that the protests threw into
clear relief a dissatisfaction of oligopolistic control of mainstream
media and its impact on access to information and news production.
In turn, the protests created the conditions for what one international
journalist called the ‘beginning of an alternative media sphere in
Brazil’ by providing a direct avenue of communication for activists
and citizens participating in the protests (Chão, 2013). For many
commentators, the citizen journalism platform Mídia NINJA (an
acronym for Narrativas Independentes, Jornalismo e Ação
[Independent Narratives Journalism and Action] referred to here as
simply NINJA) stood at the forefront of this innovative and potentially
more democratic form of journalistic production. NINJA operatives
(dedicated full- time activists or volunteer collaborators) live-
streamed the protests through web- channels maintained through the
Japanese platform TwitCasting.1 Video clips were then re- screened
on the group’s website, ‘Pos- TV’ (‘Post- TV’), and on the group’s
various social media platforms
(namely Facebook and Twitter) (Davis, 2016).2
As the first wave of national and international media attention around
NINJA crested, activists and media practitioners within Brazil began
to raise tough questions about NINJA and its parent organization, the
cultural organization Fora do Eixo (Outside the Axis). Founded by
cultural activists in 2005 to de- center and democratize cultural media
production, Fora do Eixo’s networked
Cracks in the Brazilian mediascape 57
city- specific chapters principally organize music festivals, training for
youth activists, and debates on cultural and social policy. NINJA’s
mandate and operations are distinct from Fora do Eixo, but it is
difficult to separate the groups’

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 97 ---
finances and leadership. NINJA self- declares itself a radically
independent citizen journalism project dedicated to empowering
participants to disseminate grassroots narratives; but during the 2013
protests the organization eventually faced attacks and challenges from
all sides: conservative journalists who accused them of being less than
transparent regarding government funding and becoming a
mouthpiece for the center- left governing party, PT ( Partido dos
Trabal-hadores or Worker’s Party); traditional journalists who
questioned NINJA’s ability to ensure journalistic ethics, quality and
sustainability; and leftist media activists who questioned the core
leaderships’ proximity to PT as well as their model of production in
regards to labor and autonomy.
In this chapter we review the pivotal role of media activists, their
technology, and organizational tactics during major street
demonstrations and mobilizations in Rio de Janeiro during 2013–
2014. Based on participant- observation at sites of various
demonstrations, multiple conversations with journalists and media
activists, and selective document analysis, we examine how
independent media- activists both produced and transposed spaces of
resistance. Following Judith Butler’s (2015) work on performative
assembly, we suggest that citizen media projects provided necessary
socio- technical infrastructure for anti- status quo politics and mass
mobilization. Beyond the immediate context of the protests, we
further consider NINJA as a media organization facing challenges
related to its independence. We argue that NINJA is best categorized
as what Clemencia Rodriguez calls a ‘citizens’ media’ project.
According to Rodriguez, this label refers to any project that focuses on
encouraging individuals to act as media creators. Participating in the
meaning- making process associated with creating audiovisual
materials allows individuals to assert more control over their own
identities and their own voices (Rodriguez, 2011, pp. 24–25). With its
focus on training and encouraging more individuals to contribute,
NINJA emphasizes democratization through participation as a path
toward promoting inclusivity in the media production process. This is
built around a commitment to openness in what ‘NINJAs’ can post.
However, as we demonstrate in the final section of this chapter, the
group’s commitment to providing a platform for affiliated participants
to produce material without heavy editorial intervention was
eventually eclipsed by partisanship in higher levels of the organization
during the 2015–2016 congressional campaign to unseat President
Dilma Rousseff through legally dubious impeachment proceedings.
The contemporary Brazilian media system

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 98 ---
In order to fully address the novelty of NINJA’s position within the
contemporary Brazilian media environment, we must briefly discuss
some of the particularities of its national news and information
industries, including television,
58 Tucker Landesman and Stuart Davis
print and radio. Looking briefly at the predominance of two
longstanding trends within Brazilian news, horizontal integration and
denuncismo (defined below), illustrates the pressure economic and
political forces exert on news production.
Both of these trends heavily influenced the type of stories covered and
representational frameworks utilized by domestic news outlets.
From an economic perspective, the current television and print news
industries within Brazil can be characterized as a horizontally
integrated oligopoly where a small number of corporations own a
significant majority of holdings across media industries (Noam, 2009,
p. 44). Most newspapers and television affiliates across Brazil are
owned by small number of firms including Editora Abril, Folha, Silvio
Santos (SBT), Rede Record, and Rede Globo/Grupo RBS (Carvalho,
2015).3 This concentration of ownership emerged during the military
dictatorship in the 1960s–1970s (Amaral & Guimarães, 1994; Porto,
2007; Straubhaar, 2016), during which the military government took
a number of steps to ensure the expansion of television networks that
supported the regime including subsi-dizing programming for
networks touting their policy agenda, penalizing networks for
covering subversive content, and connecting international advertisers
with networks in good standing (Amaral & Guimarães, 1994, p. 22;
Sinclair & Straubhaar, 2013, pp. 68–70). By the end of the
dictatorship in the mid- 1980s Rede Globo and its related holdings
had developed a dominant position within the television industry.
While some critics have argued that the opening up of the television
industry to public scrutiny after Brazil’s return to democracy has led
to an increase in the professionalization of news staff and seriousness
of issues covered, the fact that the majority of media outlets are
controlled by a single organization remains relatively unchanged
(Porto, 2007, p. 371). As such, potential competitors have barriers to
entry related to both economic and political factors.
The concentration of media ownership during the dictatorship led to
extreme partisanship within the media, which activists continuously
decried during the 2013–2014 protests. As Waisbord (2000) and

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 99 ---
Matos (2008) have argued, the Brazilian journalism industry during
and immediately after the dictatorship was highly bifurcated into a
dissident press (which became a watchdog press in the post-
dictatorship years) and the complicit press that often supported, if not
openly collaborated, with the regimes in power (Matos, 2008, p. 17).
This division led to the creation of popular cadres of intellectual
muckrakers who launched assaults on government and military
officials. Labeled denuncismo because of its focus on publically
attacking, denouncing and defrocking political, corporate or cultural
figures, this practice became widely popular in the post- dictatorship
period (Lins da Silva, 1990; Matos, 2008). Despite its popularity as an
avenue for attacking corporate and government elites, many
journalists and scholars have subsequently criticized denuncismo for
not investigating sources of information, using sensationalist language
and often attacking figures who disagreed with the newspaper editors
(Waisbord, 2000, pp. 110–112, 140–165). New forms of investigative
journalism during the post- dictatorship
Cracks in the Brazilian mediascape 59
period had to contend with a model that garnered massive readership
(especially among low and middle- income workers) with
sensationalist claims but neg-lected the fact- gathering and objectivity
elements of investigative journalism (Waisbord, 2000, p. 110; Marques
de Melo, 2008, p. 195).
Between the economic issues associated with conglomerate control
and the popularity of denuncismo a s a popular form of political
journalism, media outlets committed to investigative journalism,
community input and independence from political parties have often
faced precarious conditions. In light of these considerations, a project
such as Mídia NINJA that offers a web- based platform devoted to
grassroots- generated content offers an innovative journalistic strategy
for promoting citizen engagement.
Research approach
In order to analyze Mídia NINJA’s dual role as both a communication
facilitator during the protests and as a media organization struggling
with questions of political affiliation and partisan influence, we
employed qualitative methods including participant observation and
document analysis of material disseminated by or discussing the
organization. The participant observation was limited to Rio de
Janeiro and included the major street demonstrations of June–July
2013, as well as numerous smaller demonstrations continuing through

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 100 ---
2013 up until the June–July 2014 FIFA World Cup. In addition to
street marches, which both authors attended, one author (Landesman)
also observed and conversed with media activists and independent
journalists while visiting multiple sites of occupation – including
conducting one site- visit with NINJA operatives – additional public
events (for example documentary film screenings and public debates),
and various social and political meetings at which NINJA and other
media activists or independent journalists participated. We
supplemented traditional participant observation by collecting
relevant online content, including videos, written posts and images
distributed through Facebook, Twitter, Twitcasting and other social
networking services utilized by the organization. We also examined
print and online materials from activists, intellectuals and public
figures that offer commentary on NINJA’s rise to national/
international attention in 2013.
Mídia NINJA at the protests: producing political
potency
During protests and public events, NINJA operatives most often used
smartphones to live stream what was in front of them, however some
of the more veteran NINJA’s also used professional- grade digital
cameras to photograph and record video. Live streaming videos were
made available via the Japanese streaming service Twit- casting, and
NINJA’s Facebook and Twitter profiles were updated sporadically
during and immediately after protests with still images
60 Tucker Landesman and Stuart Davis
and short videos with descriptive text: specific location, protest
chants, reports of clashes or police violence, arrests and so on. At any
given protest, occupation, or event we observed one to three NINJAs
live streaming and/or photographing.
NINJA’s physical presence at protests and events was synergetic to the
mobilization. Rarely did we see NINJA asking participants for
interviews.
Rather, they sought out those eager to speak. Such protestors were
easy to come by, and NINJA’s live stream often allowed participants to
speak at- length with no prompting or follow- up questions. The
transmission was theirs until they finished, another protestor
interrupted, or the camera’s gaze was drawn elsewhere.
As argued by Harcha and Pereira (2014), the visual aesthetics of

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 101 ---
NINJA’s content subvert the hegemonic production and circulation of
meaningful images. The constant video, uncut and edited only by the
direction the camera points, transmits a sense of documentary
rawness. Moreover the grainy low-resolution quality of video and
photographs (many in black and white), stand in stark contrast to the
‘high quality’ and highly edited corporate news media. We argue that
this aesthetic also reinforces the positionality of the citizen journalist
– media activists are not simply reporting from the ‘front line’ but are
part of that line. The live stream video and photographs go beyond
reporting the protest, they compose the scene of resistance.
NINJA and other media- activists habitually scrutinized police actions
during protests. Indeed, they were sought out by name to protectively
document interactions between protestors and police. At the
demonstrations we repeatedly observed protestors shouting for NINJA
to film police when they conducted body and bag searches. We also
saw protestors calling out for NINJA or another media activists to
document arrests and to film police officers who removed or covered
up their identifying name and badge number, a practice that was
common during the 2013–2014 protests, presumably to hamper
identifying police officers accused of misconduct and truculence.
When an arrest was made, media activists asked the protestor to say
their name into the camera and dogged the arresting officers to
divulge at which police station the protestor would be processed. This
acted to heighten the level of scrutiny after the arrest, ensure that
activist-lawyers could accompany the process (sometimes achieving
release with no charges), and ensure that any police aggression during
the arrest would be caught on camera.
NINJA and other journalists using similar tactics were not the only
bodies carrying smartphones during the protests. It was common for
anyone with such technology to employ it as a defense against the
threat of police aggression. At a relatively small protest march in
downtown Rio de Janeiro consisting of principally radical groups of
young people such as the small band of occupiers camping on the
steps of the Municipal Legislative building, Black Bloc (a non- group
of sorts), and the Popular Independent Front (known as the acronym
FIP), the military police numbered almost as many as the protestors.
Implementing a new technique of crowd policing, rather than forming
lines on the perimeters, the
Cracks in the Brazilian mediascape 61
police periodically weaved through the marchers in single file, forcing

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 102 ---
divides while stopping and searching individual protestors at will.
Given that there had occurred multiple incidences of police officers
planting incriminating evidence on protestors, ranging from large
stones to fireworks to molotov cocktails, every time the police stopped
any protestor multiple people would huddle around and begin filming
with their smartphones. Should the police take an exceptionally long
time searching, or should they manage to stop only a few protestors
unnoticed, calls would ring out for the entire procession to stop, and
immediately a group would return with camera- phones in hand.
NINJA was not always called for specifically, however an advantage of
NINJA’s live stream was an audience that often numbered in the many
thousands. Watching from home, these digital participants could take
still- image screenshots of police officers without visible identification,
especially when conducting bag or body searches or physically
pushing or shoving protestors. We observed this activity by following
the open chat function that ran alongside the live- streamed video. We
can recall two specific occasions when police officers attacked
peaceful protestors and were subsequently identified through photos
posted by NINJA and other networked activists on Facebook. The
protective quality of media activists did not go unnoticed by the
protestors themselves. At a small gathering and march outside the
then- Governor Cabral’s beachfront penthouse in the wealthy
neighborhood of Leblon (which was a common site of protest,
including an encampment called Ocupa Cabral), protes-
tors chalked the streets, writing ‘Thank you Black Bloc, Thank you
NINJA.’4
We understand the actions of NINJA and other activists employing
new technology described above as productive of the protests
themselves. They constituted in part the necessary conditions of
assembly and dissent. In an essay on street politics, Judith Butler
(2015, p. 93) asks,
is the action of the body separable from its technology, and is the
technology not helping to establish new forms of political action? And
when censorship or violence is directed against those bodies, is it not
also directed against their access to media, in order to establish
hegemonic control over which images travel and which do not?
Police officers routinely targeted press, specifically photojournalists,
with violence and arrest during the 2013 protests (Braga, 2014, p. 12).
At one demonstration, police detained a NINJA activist and justified
the arrest on an official police Twitter account claiming that

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 103 ---
transmission of clashes between protesters and police criminally
incited violent acts. At the same protest, police arrested and charged a
young man with attempted homicide for allegedly throwing a molotov
cocktail that caused burns to multiple police officers. However,
prosecutors were eventually forced to drop charges after activists
pieced together citizen- produced media and published a video
montage that not only discredited the arrest but also suggested that
the homemade firebomb was thrown by an
62 Tucker Landesman and Stuart Davis
undercover police officer presumably to defame the protestors and
justify the use of repressive force. The event and aftermath were
documented by a New York Times reporter (Sussman et al., 2013) and
witnessed by one of the authors.5
The immediateness of image and sound published by live streaming
citizen journalists and the photographs shared by protestors on
Facebook, Twitter, Instagram or within private group messaging apps
such as WhatsApp make the scenes of the streets ‘politically potent’ in
the words of Butler (2015, p. 91).
Images that feel part of the scene (as opposed to reporting about the
scene) are essential to transposing the protest beyond the physical
location. As previously noted, those Brazilians most invested in public
dissent are highly distrustful of mainstream media. Thus, critical
observers viewing reports of protests produced by Globo or another
mainstream news source are required to conduct a spon-taneous
media analysis in order to intellectually discard the editorializing. The
empathetic images produced by activists and circulated on social
media and the raw live streams of NINJA fundamentally change the
relationship between the content and the viewer- subject such that
they are potentially transformed from a passive consumer to an active
participant when they interact with the content – for example through
vigilantly taking screenshots in order to document illegal police action
or through commenting and ‘sharing’ – expanding its reach and
influence. During the 2013 street protests, attending the marches or
occupations was almost expected of critically- minded or leftwing
youth. On a few occasions we heard young people respond, when
asked whether or not they attended a protest respond, that while they
did not make the march they did watch it via live stream.
The ability of media activism to transpose scenes of protest beyond
the physical site of assembly has important implications for the
geography of dissent.

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 104 ---
During the first week of mass protests, events were scheduled in over
100 Brazilian municipalities. As the protest movement continued, only
a few of the larger cities (principally Rio de Janeiro and São Paulo,
and to some extent Belo Hori-zonte and Porto Alegre) sustained
momentum. The live streaming and status updates from marches and
encampments in the centers of resistance facilitated a form of political
agency and participation for those who lived far from ‘the public
square,’ that is, a political or politicized public space. Analysis of
Twitter hashtags associated with the street demonstrations in 2013
reveal that the majority of (re)tweets originated from accounts well
outside the geophysical location of mobilization (Bastos et al., 2014).
Physical proximity is not the only factor that affected participation.
We spoke with many citizens who supported the demonstrations and
lived close enough to participate but stayed home due to fear of
violence and police truculence. We also met Rio residents who
followed the events on social media while working long hours in
downtown or wealthy neighborhoods between grueling commutes to
and from the poorer suburbs. New media produced by activists and
disseminated through online social networks was therefore pivotal to
the political agency of subjects, facilitating participation at the
assembly, however peripheral, without being physically
Cracks in the Brazilian mediascape 63
‘at the scene.’ We hesitate to enter a debate regarding the relative
importance of online activism versus in- person activism (e.g.
Gladwell, 2010), but following Butler we maintain that the
participation of individuals outside of the specific physical spaces of
protests constituted an important part of these events.
Beyond its role in producing the scene of protest, citizen journalism
was the only media in our opinion able to capture the diverse
mobilizations and emerging social movements without representing
their complexity as contradictory and self- defeating. As noted by
Butler, one of the first questions asked of an assembly is why they are
assembled. The immediate expectation for a list of demands proves
problematic for many contemporary assemblies that prioritize radical
democratic processes and horizontal leadership. The failure to offer
explicit demands or TV- friendly soundbites by an officially designated
spokes-person was a source of frustration during the Occupy
movements in NYC and London, or the Indignados of Spain. In
comparison, at least Occupy and the Indignados had names. In Brazil
the protests quickly outgrew the message and demands of the spark –
protests organized by the radical urbanists of The MPL

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 105 ---
( Movimento Passe Livre, or The Free Fare Movement), a collective of
activists advancing the right to the city through a single- issue
campaign of free public transport – and no consensus was ever
reached (neither on the streets nor in the press) on how to name the
exceptional moment of resistance. This led some to generously argue
that the protests constituted Hardt and Negri’s multidud (Gutiérez,
2013), referring to the conceptualization of irreducible, non- unified
masses as a subject of emancipation (see Keucheyan, 2013, pp. 88–
89). However other commentators cited questionable data collected
by polling company Data-Folha, which suggested the protestors were
disproportionately middle class and college- educated, to undermine
legitimacy. Even some progressive academics questioned whether the
protests should be considered radical or popular, given the presence of
far- right and reactionary participants, and suggested that the numbers
on the streets were swelled because it was fleetingly fashionable for
college students to protest.
Brazilian political philosopher Rodrigo Nunes was a uniquely qualified
commentator during the protests. Prior to the June uprising he had
been writing Organization of the Organizationless: Collective Action after
Networks, in which he examines contemporary radical protests and
social movements engendered through layers of complex networks
rather than traditional organizations (Nunes, 2013a). In essays on the
manifestation of discontent in Brazil he identified the protests as a
mass movement without organizations of the masses (Nunes, 2013b).
However, he dismisses the notion, correctly in our opinion, that the
resistance lacked organization or structure. The lack of vertical
leadership and singular narrative of agency was either too confusing
for the media to understand, or the elites controlling the media had
little political interest in grappling with the complexity. Either way,
the vast majority of news organizations decided to push a generic
narrative of anti- status quo discontent (Nunes, 2013c). This flattened
the initial protests, and as resistance self- organized for
64 Tucker Landesman and Stuart Davis
months in Rio de Janeiro, the media coverage obsessed over violent
clashes with police, destruction of property, confrontational protest
tactics like Black Bloc, and disruption of normalcy in the city.
Content produced by NINJA and other media activists succeeded
where the corporate media failed – depicting the complexity of the
various organizing movements. They did this by not demanding a
singular or simplistic protest narrative delivered by individuals
consensually recognized as leaders and easily reproducible in

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 106 ---
newspaper columns and TV soundbites. Rather, they amplified the
plurality of the bodies gathered in resistance. It was common to watch
a corporate TV segment or read a newspaper article in which not a
single protestor was interviewed or protest materials posted online
cited. In contrast, media activists using Twitcasting, Facebook and
Twitter mixed reporting, photojournalism and content cultivation (for
example when a media collective would share a protestor’s Facebook
status with its many thousands of followers). Butler argues that all
popular assemblies engage in the performative claim ‘ we are the
people’ in order to legitimate both the assembly and their demands.
But she argues that we must be able to theorize contemporary
assemblies respecting their pluralities:
to act in concert does not mean to act in conformity; it may be that
people are moving or speaking in several different directions at once,
even at cross-purposes. And it does not mean they speak the exact
same words […].
(Butler, 2015, p. 157)
By more authentically representing the protestors and their demands,
NINJA and others diluted the power of conservative corporate media
and politically motivated journalists to determine the legitimacy of
the protest movement. The media technology became a necessary
condition of the protests, for it facilitated the performative claim, we
are the people, without relying on the newspapers and TV oligopoly to
recognize assembly as legitimate.
Mídia NINJA beyond the protests: between
participation and partisanship
Moving beyond the specific and immediate context of the 2013
demonstrations, NINJA facilitates pluralistic political engagement
through citizen media training programs. This commitment to
participation reflects an ideology of media democratization through
the incorporation of individual, non- professional producers. The
organization’s communication strategy implies that anyone can be a
NINJA. For example, when their Twitter account reached 50,000
followers, they celebrated by tweeting ‘We are 50K NINJAs on
Twitter.’ NINJA and Fora do Eixo regularly organize events and have
trained dozens of activist in new technology and online social
networks to expand their reach. Within weeks of the first major
demonstrations in June 2013, NINJA hosted a public event at

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 107 ---
Cracks in the Brazilian mediascape 65
the Federal University of Rio de Janeiro to explain their model of
participatory citizen journalism and encourage collaboration from any
interested individual.
Because NINJA had a working operating model prior to the 2013
protests and was supported by its larger parent organization, it was
well placed to dominate the alternative media market in coverage of
the mobilizations. In spite of its successes in promoting democratic
participation, NINJA has been the target of a heavy amount of
criticism. Some of the most substantial critiques of NINJA have come
from activists on the radical left who critique the group’s proximity to
the PT.6 The group’s connection to the party is both undisputed and
multifaceted. In March of 2015, two members of Fora do Eixo were
appointed to posi-tions in the federal Ministry of Culture (Gragnani,
2015). Furthermore, in public statements and interviews, project co-
founder Capilé showed unabashed support for the PT in the 2014
election, and participated in crafting campaign strategy. We are also
aware of at least one NINJA operative that was employed as a
campaign photographer by a PT candidate. Such connections between
core members of Mídia NINJA and PT politicians led to expressed
scorn from other media activists and radical independent journalists.
During a conversation between one author (Landesman), a veteran
independent journalist and another Brazilian activist before a protest
in early 2016, the journalist accused NINJA operatives of being co-
opted by PT, including handing out campaign propaganda and even
having government cars and drivers at their disposal. We are skeptical
of these accusations (especially that NINJA journalists have chauf-
feured cars); and mention them here only to demonstrate the tension
within the alternative mediascape. It is important to state that we did
not note favor-able coverage of PT candidates during the 2014
election on any of NINJA’s social media platforms, and we do not
validate the claim that Mídia NINJA became a propaganda
mouthpiece. If PT candidates gained advantage, it was the expertise
and savvy the ‘new media’ activists brought to their campaigns.
In 2015, NINJA’s relationship with the PT intensified as the party
became wrapped up in a series of scandals linked to corruption
involving many of Brazil’s political parties. Despite the systemic
quality of the corruption under scrutiny, Brazilian corporate media,
most vociferously outlets owned by Rede Globo, aligned against
President Dilma, ex- President Lula, and the PT and tacitly, and at
times explicitly, supported the impeachment of Dilma. In response,
Lula led the public fight against what PT leaders and many

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 108 ---
progressive activists and public intellectuals have deemed a political
coup d’état. It is beyond the scope of this chapter to analyze the
political scandal and merits of impeachment. What is notable to our
discussion is NINJA’s public support of Lula and Dilma as well as
mirrored rhetorical messaging. NINJA became active organizers of
anti- impeachment demonstrations through their social media plat-
forms. Figures 3.1 and 3.2 present Facebook and Twitter posts by
NINJA that reflect their support of the PT.
In these two figures, NINJA’s partisan support is evident through the
captioning. While the main goal of the message in Figure 3.1 is to
disseminate an

Feghali, federal representative of the Communist Party, and a crowd

completely full of people joining Lula and Jandira Feghali on the final

of the PT and its key figures.
The proximity between NINJA and the PT takes on more significance
when contrasted with other alternative media producers with similar
political orienta-tions. While many alternative media outlets criticized
the oligarchic and anti-democratic behaviors of the politicians that
engineered Dilma’s impeachment, they often concentrated their efforts
on exposing corporate media bias and covering the progressive
coalition of activists that organized against the persecution of Dilma’s
presidential mandate. In comparison, NINJA went beyond denouncing
the impeachment as undemocratic or illegitimate and proactively
defended PT’s legacy.

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 112 ---
68 Tucker Landesman and Stuart Davis
Figure 3.3 Twitter post by NINJA that reflects their support of the PT.
Translation:
‘Lula will speak in minutes with foreign journalists. Watch #live …’
NINJA’s defense of the PT does not present a clear case of co- optation
but pushes us to differentiate between the role of new media
technologies as part of an assemblage manifested during
demonstrations and as tools for media producers to launch strategic
messages in support of certain agendas. In Rodriguez’s definition of
citizens’ media, the production process itself takes on a political
meaning for those participating. As Atton interprets: ‘Citizens use their
own, self- managed media to become politically involved on their own
terms’ (Atton, 2008, p. 12). As a participatory model, it bases its
political intervention at the level of individual user. By offering the
tools for everyday citizens to record and disseminate information, it
offers spaces for them to create their own representations. However,
this overt emphasis on the individual participant leaves open the
question of the larger ideological or political aims of the organization
providing the training. Numerous studies on projects fitting the
citizens’ media framework reflect this tension. As Gumucio- Dagron

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 113 ---
(2001) and Plaut (2006) have found in other national contexts,
organizations that focus on training as a political act in itself often do
not explicitly or implicitly address the ideological orientation of their
projects. When working in politically charged or contentious
situations, this ambiguity can leave them open to vacillation between
political agendas or partisan co- optation. In the case of NINJA, the
way the organization prioritized a political objective of
Cracks in the Brazilian mediascape 69
Figure 3.4 Twitter post by NINJA that reflects their support of the PT.
Translation: ‘Lula and Jandira are with the people! Bangu Plaza is
completely full of people joining Lula and Jandira Feghali during the
final stretch of the elections’
promoting media democratization through citizen participation
sidelined discussions about the group’s larger political orientation.
This may have facilitated charismatic group leaders to politically steer
the organization in directions that did not resonate with all members
and certainly did not resonate with other alternative media producers
and activists.
Conclusions
Our observations and analysis presented in this chapter affirm Butler’s
assertion of interdependency between assembled bodies in public

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 114 ---
space and the networked technology those bodies wield as tools.
Butler argues that participation in politics is reliant on various
infrastructures – broadly understood as environmental, social, and
technological relations and systems of sustenance – that permit the act
of assembly (Butler, 2015, pp. 127, 130). We suggest that the strategic
networked use of new media technologies and citizen journalist
projects such as Mídia NINJA are one such example of infrastructure
that facilitate and support the right to assembly and politics of dissent.
Within the landscape of alternative media in Brazil, many other media
outlets reflect a more overt political ideology than NINJA. Brasil de
Fato, launched by social movement groups after the 2003 World Social
Forum and A Nova Democracia, founded in 2002, both have over a
decade of publishing https://sanet.st/blogs/polatebooks/
70 Tucker Landesman and Stuart Davis
radical print newspapers as well as online written and video content.
Each collective self- identifies as explicitly independent and anti-
capitalist. With strong, politically overt editorial agendas ( A Nova
Democracia is guided by a lengthy manifesto outlining its opposition to
neoliberal consolidation of media and geopolitical capitalist
imperialism), these publications follow a model of advocacy
journalism with the goal of advancing revolutionary politics. In
contrast to these organizations, NINJA offers a more inclusive and
participatory model of news production. However, the group’s
partisanship raises concerns of co-optation of content or
commandeering of the site’s reputation as a democratic
communication channel for propagandistic goals.
As a case, Mídia NINJA raises important questions about the role of
digital media activism within larger social mobilizations and
transformations. Instead of celebrating the group as an example of
‘radical self- communication through horizontal networks’ (Juris,
2005) or damning it as a co- optation of citizen journalism for the
aims and agendas of a certain political party, we have attempted to
address the organization from various perspectives: its pivotal role in
producing public resistance along with additional media activists, its
innovations to promote participatory production strategies in order to
democratize Brazilian media, and its ideological entanglements with
partisan politics.
