Part II
Control practices of
policing and security
agencies
Chapter 5
Settler colonial surveillance and
the criminalization of social media
Contradictory implications for
Palestinian resistance
Madalena Santos

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 150 ---
Introduction
Surveillance and the policing of Indigenous social movements are
commonly used to counter mobilizing tactics and construct activists as
dangerous to the stability and social order of the state. Understood as
possible threats to the overthrow of settler colonial governments or
the state itself, anticolonial movements are closely surveyed and at
times, made out rightly illegal (Parsons, 2011, p. 357). While the rise
in the accessibility of digital media is enabling Indigenous activists
and their allies to organize and disseminate information in a variety of
novel ways, the state is increasingly using new media technologies to
target social movements in the name of security (Harcourt, 2015;
Gerbaudo, 2012). There is a great deal of literature that considers the
dynamics between surveillance and the policing of social movements.
Yet much this literature fails to consider the colonial or post- colonial
character of societies (Zureik, 2011, p. 39). Colonial analyses offer a
crucial insight into these areas of study as the historical and ongoing
methods of colonization configure the socio- political ordering of who
is deemed a threat to society and in need of being surveilled.
Specifically, colonialisms have constructed whiteness as normative
and white citizens as ‘belonging’ to the nation while others are
understood as strangers or alien (Ahmed, 2000; Finn, 2006, p. 416). In
the case of Israel, the telling of the Zionist ethno- national story
renders Jewish Israelis as the natives of the land while it
simultaneously depicts Palestinians as Arab enemy others (Mbembé,
2003; Said, 1992, p. 5; Yiftachel, 2006). In doing so, it delegitimizes
Palestinian claims to Indigenity while casting them as security threats.
To deal with the security threat of Palestinian resistance, Israel has
developed some of the most sophisticated counter- insurgency
practices available (Khalili, 2010; Zohar, 2009), including those that
are focused on the surveillance of social media.
In this chapter, I offer a critical examination of the ways in which the
settler colonial state of Israel uses digital media as a mechanism of
surveillance and policing of social justice and everyday resistance in
Palestine (Herrera, 2015; Stein, 2012). I use news media coverage in
addition to reports from Israeli and Palestinian legal and human rights
organizations from 2015–2016 to discuss
98 Madalena Santos
Israel’s implementation of new measures to criminalize social media
use. Online posts on platforms such as Facebook and Twitter have led
to the arrests of hundreds of Palestinians, many of whom would not

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 151 ---
consider themselves part of a social movement (Abu Amer, 2016;
Dorell & Shuttleworth, 2016; Days of Palestine, 2016). I also discuss
the adverse implications for Palestinians following the passing of
Israel’s new Anti- Terror law, which was voted in by the Knesset
(Israeli parliament) on 16 June 2016. This law allows for ‘the
widespread use of
“secret evidence” by the state prosecution’ which according to the
Israeli legal rights group Adalah (2016) impedes the
possibility of substantively objecting to repressive decisions based on
their merits before the judiciary. The law also establishes new
criminal offenses such as public expressions of support or empathy for
terror organizations, and significantly increases the potential
sentences for such offenses.
These changes directly impact Palestinians’ ability to archive and
disseminate information as part of political organizing and
mobilization. The arrests and detainment of Palestinians for online
posts not only have detrimental implications for the use of social
media as both a tool and space for social justice activ-activ-
ism (Aouragh, 2008, 2012; Shalhoub- Kevorkian, 2011), but also for
the control and regulation of everyday life. This chapter enhances the
scholarly literature on social movements and settler colonial studies
by demonstrating the intersec-intersec-
tion of race and resistance to colonial logics (Bhattacharjee, 1997;
Chatterjee, 1989; Mohanty, 2003) and surveillance (Bonilla & Rosa,
2015). Through a multidisciplinary analysis, this chapter provides a
preliminary investigation on the digital surveillance of Palestinian
social activism, and contributes to knowledge on the criminalization
of Indigenous resistance in settler colonial states.
Methodological framework
Although surveillance literature stresses the management of
population and territory there is a lack of academic research in the
area of state surveillance and present colonialisms. Elia Zureik, David
Lyon and Yasmeen Abu- Laban’s (2011) edited collection, Surveillance
and Control in Israel/Palestine: Population, Territory, and Power, offers
critical insight into the significance of contextually situating how
colonial populations are controlled and managed. This work illustrates
the need for further studies connecting surveillance to present day
colonialism, which while acknowledged by surveillance studies

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 152 ---
scholars (see for instance, Abu- Laban, 2015; Arteaga Botello, 2015;
Browne, 2012; Finn, 2011), remains theoretically sparse. Moreover,
while criminological research and studies from the sociology of
surveillance demonstrate that surveillance efforts can result in
suppression and demobilization for social movements categorized as
threats (Bajc, 2013; Boykoff, 2007; Earl, 2011), this scholarship has
not linked the tactics and mechanisms of surveillance to impacts of
ongoing colonial
The criminalization of social media 99
control. To explore the intersection of digital media surveillance with
the techniques of settler colonial regulation and control, I focus on
news media, and legal and human rights documentation of Israeli
digital surveillance, online (il)legality, and Palestinian resistance in
relation to five social media cases over the year 2015–2016. This
period coincides with mass arrests and administrative detention
following the most recent popular uprising of 2015 (Addameer,
2016a, 2015a,b; Nashif, 2016).
My methodological framework is based on a settler colonial analysis
of Israel–
Palestine. Whether through the direct use of force or through racist
and dis-criminatory policies and practices, the structures and
processes of all colonial endeavours are violent. Despite the violence
of colonial projects, however, it is often those who resist colonialism
who are characterized as violent. Undeniably, at different points in
time, the use of arms by Palestinians has been successful as a mode of
mass mobilization and political consciousness raising in resisting the
colonial violence of Israel (see for example, Giacaman, 2013). Yet,
while unarmed forms of resistance have been part of the Palestinian
struggle against Zionist forces from the beginning of the colonial
project, the mainstream media and Western public discourse has
focused on portraying Palestinians as violent (Qumsiyeh, 2010, pp.
227–228). The framing of the situation of Israel/Palestine through
security and terrorism discourses elides the reality of Israel as a settler
colonial state. Indeed, discussion of Israel through the lens of settler
colonialism remains contentious within some Western and much
Israeli scholarship (Collins, 2011, p. 35; Zureik, 2011, p. 5).
Undeniably, however, the Zionist project of Israel is one of
colonization, which premises the survival and vitality of the state’s
Jewish settler population upon the removal and absence of the
racialized Indigenous Palestinian other (Abdo, 2008, p. 174; Abu El-
Haj, 2010, p. 29; Kanaaneh, 2002; Lentin, 2008; Masalha, 2012;

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 153 ---
Pappé, 2008). This type of project is never about situational
discriminations or circumstantially racialized practices; instead, as
Wolfe (2008, p. 103) argues, settler colonialism is based upon the
expropriation of land from Indigenous populations for the erection of
a ‘new colonial society’. State surveillance and the policing of
Indigenous Palestinian resistance have been key to enabling Israel’s
unremitting pursuit of its settler colonial project in Palestine.
John Collins (2011, p. 31) argues that all settler colonial projects rely
on similar logics ‘rooted in the modern dynamics of state formation,
racialization, capital accumulation and genocide’ and ‘the major
ideological justifications ….
[of “imaginative geographies”]’. Employing Patrick Wolfe’s framework
for examining settler colonialism, Collins (2011, pp. 31–35) details
four main logics of settler colonialism that work in tandem with one
another: (1) the logic of elimination (or genocide); (2) the logic of the
frontier; (3) the logic of exceptionalism; and (4) the logic of denial. In
this chapter, I engage with two of the main logics that intersect most
evidently with new media surveillance: (1) elimination and (2)
exceptionalism. To discuss the relationship between these logics and
the concept of surveillance, I utilize the definition offered by Kirstie
Ball
100 Madalena Santos
and David Murakami Wood (2006) in their edited ‘A Report on the
Surveillance Society’. They define surveillance, ‘where we find
purposeful, routine, systematic and focused attention paid to personal
details, for the sake of control, entitlement, management, influence or
protection’ (Ball & Murakami Wood, 2006, p. 3). In the case of Israel,
surveillance is used to control and manage Palestinians to reinforce
fear of the Palestinian terrorist other in order to maintain the
entitlement of the settler state.
According to Collins (2011, 32), the logic of elimination is enacted
through methods of exclusion including forced migration,
confinement, coercive assimi-lation, and mass killing. This logic
denies the identity of the colonized and their connection to the land,
makes claims to the land while refuting the existence of the
Indigenous people, and dismisses ‘the legitimacy of their resistance’ in
labelling struggles against colonial forces/colonialism as acts of
terror/terrorism (see Collins, 2011, p. 32). At the same time, Israel has
developed some of the most sophisticated counter- insurgency
practices available (Khalili, 2010; Zureik, 2001; Zohar, 2009),

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 154 ---
including those that are focused on the surveillance of social media.
For present purposes, I focus on the practice of confinement as a
strategy of elimination. Israel implements elimination techniques of
confinement through the imprisonment and detainment of political
prisoners, the occupation of people’s homes, and the restriction of
movement.
Tied closely to logic of elimination is the logic of exceptionalism. This
logic is based on the idea of settlers as the ‘chosen people’ constructed
often through metaphors of Manifest Destiny (Collins, 2011, p. 33).
Based on notions of superiority, settlers understand themselves as
unique and their colonial mission is as necessary and ‘even altruistic’
(Collins, 2011, p. 34). Settler colonial logics are fundamentally tied to
what Hannah Arendt (1973 [1958]) identifies as ‘race-thinking’. Based
on the uncontested notion of a hierarchy of races or ‘the ideology of
race’, race- thinking associates ‘dignity and importance’ with the idea
of racism to move the conception of hierarchical race difference
beyond that of a matter of opinion to the political construction of
knowledge and nation- state building (Arendt, 1973 [1958], pp. 158–
161). Rooted in white supremacist ideology, race- thinking is central
to apartheid policies and practices of racial segregation and
discrimination (see Articles 1–19 International Convention, 1973, pp.
75–77). Israel uses law and religion to discursively construct a racial-
racial-
ized framework of citizenship and nationality that attempts to
legitimize the practice of differential citizenship rights, property and
land theft as well as ethnic cleansing. Through complex legal and
religious structural systems and institutionalized practices, Israel’s
division between Palestinians and Jewish Israelis establishes
differential racialized categories based on ethnic and religious
groupings (Abu El- Haj, 2010, p. 29; Kanaaneh, 2002; Yuval- Davis,
1987, p. 39).
The concept of lawfare helps to articulate how law is used to justify
what would otherwise be considered criminal actions by the state. The
Amer ican-Israeli ‘Lawfare Project’ (2016) established in 2010 ‘as the
legal arm of the pro-Israel community’ defines lawfare in negative
terms as
The criminalization of social media 101
the use of the law as a weapon of war. It denotes the abuse of Western
laws and judicial systems to achieve strategic military or political ends
… [and includes] filing frivolous lawsuits and misusing legal processes

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 155 ---
to intimidate and frustrate opponents in the theatre of war (see http://
thelawfareproject.
org/lawfare/what- is-lawfare- 1/).
However, the term is now also used widely to expose the
contested legitimacy of litigation to challenge military and security
policies and practices; and efforts to sue or prosecute state agents,
government-funded contractors, and corporations who are alleged to
have engaged in or abetted serious violations of law in the conduct of
war.
(Hajjar, 2012)
According to Hajjar (2012), the Lawfare Project ‘envisages and
promotes a common US–Israeli governmental interest in combating
efforts to hold officials and agents of both states accountable for
violations of international law’
(Hajjar, 2012). Ironically, the Lawfare Project has engaged in lawsuits
and other legal battles to stop Amer ican funding of the United
Nations Relief Works Agency for Palestine Refugees in the Near East
(UNRWA) and ‘pro flotilla groups US2GAZA, Baka and Code Pink’
which they argue are ‘connected to Hamas’, in addition to attempting
to prevent Boycott Divestment and Sanction (BDS) efforts which seek
to have Israel comply with international law (see
http://thelawfareproject.org/advocacy/).
Case studies
For the period between September 2015 and August 2016, I searched
online news media stories, and reports from Israeli and Palestinian
human and legal rights organizations for arrests by Israel of
Palestinians for online posts. Using the key words: arrest, detainment,
social media, Israel and Palestine, I found numerous instances of
Palestinians who had been arrested and detained by Israel for what
has been termed ‘incitement through social media’ (Addameer, 2016a;
Nashif, 2016; Ma’an, 2016) regardless of their engagement in social
activism and resistance. I highlight five cases to illustrate how
practices of surveillance in Israel are linked to settler colonial logics.
The logic of elimination: ‘security traps’
Exemplifying the excesses of settler colonial powers of surveillance

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 156 ---
and policing, from October 2015 to March 2016, Israeli police
arrested 278 Palestinians on the grounds of ‘inciting violence’ on
Facebook (Abu Amer, 2016). By November 2015, 128 Palestinians
‘were placed under administrative detention including 31 over claims
of “incitement” through social media, 3 of whom were
102 Madalena Santos
underage children’ (Addameer, 2015c). Through fake Facebook
profiles, police surveyed the pages of those it identified as prominent
Palestinian activists to follow conversation threads it deemed as
threats (Rosenfeld in Dorell & Shuttleworth, 2016; Abu Amer, 2016).
Jeffery Monaghan (2014, p. 496) argues that security discourses allow
for experts in the management of unease to occupy expert status and
invoke security measures – securitization processes – as easement
methods that require further re- circulations of dangers. In effect,
‘security traps’ result in ‘the politics of maximal security [which] are
also politics of maximal anxiety’…. While the symbolic use of ‘traps’
evoke a representation of enclosure, contemporary entrapments of
security are not governed by a logic of enclosure that are evident in
past practices of security.
Although Israel continues its on- the-ground ‘security enclosures’,
particularly in the West Bank and Gaza (Green & Smith, 2016), but
also within Israel with the demolition and removal of Bedouin villages
(Bishara, 2015, p. 41), it now also encloses cyberspaces by invoking
security discourses related to ‘incitement’.
Indeed, Israel claims that attacks carried out by Palestinians against
Israelis have been encouraged through online posts (Dorell &
Shuttleworth, 2016); however, according to the Prisoner Support and
Human Rights Association, Addameer (2015a), Israel is using
‘incitement’ claims to target Palestinian human rights defenders and
journalists. For instance, Samah Dweik a journalist for Al- Quds
newspaper and the online Quds News Network was forcibly taken
‘from her home on 10 April after posting and sharing pictures in
support for Palestinians recently killed by Israeli forces’ (Days of
Palestine, 2016; Hassan, 2016; Zatat, 2016). She was later sentenced
to six months for ‘incitement’ for using the terms ‘martyr’ and
‘Intifada’ on her Facebook post (Silver 2016; see also Days of
Palestine). Palestinians use the term ‘martyr’ to include anyone who
has been killed by the state of Israel and not only those actively
engaged in armed resistance. As Whitehead and Abufarha (2008, p.

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 157 ---
397) state ‘the concept of shahid (martyr) [is] as a victim who falls at
the hands of oppressive occupation’. Meanwhile, Intifada (literally ‘a
shaking off ’) is widely understood by both Palestinians and Israelis as
well as those who study the region as a popular uprising. Dweik’s
work as a journalist makes it even more likely that she would be using
these terms to express everyday life in Palestine and not as an
incitement to commit violence.
Another questionable case for the determination of incitement is that
of the Palestinian poet Dareen Tatour. Tatour was arrested in her
home after she posted her poem entitled ‘Resist my people. Resist
them’ on Facebook and YouTube (Jensen, 2016). In an interview with
Mondweiss, Tatour explained that her poem was inspired by
the murder of three Palestinian civilians … Ali Dawabsheh, the 18-
month-old infant who was burned to death by right- wing settlers in
July 2015;
The criminalization of social media 103
Mohammed Abu Khdeir, the teen who was kidnapped and burned to
death
in July 2014, and Hadel Hashlamoun, the unarmed 18-year- old
student gunned down at a checkpoint in September 2015.
(Jensen, 2016)
Like Dweik, Israeli officials appear to have taken issue with possible
interpreta-tions of Tatour’s work, which they claim are provocations
to martyrs to take action, specifically in the line Wa atbaa kafilat al
shuhadayi, which translates into English as: ‘follow the caravan of
martyrs’ (Jensen, 2016). Tatour contests this reading as nonsensical
given that the Palestinians she writes about were murdered (see
Jensen, 2016). Regardless of the interpretation, however, the fact that
poetry as an art form requires nuance and clarification makes it at
odds with the law of ‘incitement’ which require ‘clear legal grounds’
(Adalah, 2015).
According to the Israeli legal rights organization Adalah (2015),
clarity is essential to establishing incitement. Moreover, the states that
there must be ‘a real and strong possibility that others will be
encouraged to commit violent acts based on this speech’ (Adalah,
2015).
While the targeting of Palestinian artists is not new – Israel has a long

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 158 ---
history of criminalizing and targeting Palestinians for their politically
motivated art, such as the celebrated Palestinian author Ghassan
Kanafani, and Mahmoud
Darwish1 among many others (Harlow, 1987) – what seems unusual
in Tatour’s case is that she was not well- known at the time of her
arrest. In fact, her video clip had only 113 views by time of arrest
(Jensen, 2016). This makes the ‘possibility that others will be
encouraged to commit acts based on this speech’
(Adalah, 2015) even that less likely. One does not have to be well
known to be imprisoned for expressing an opinion, one only needs to
express it on a publicly accessible media forum. The cases of Dweik
and Tatour illustrate how, through confinement, the logic of
elimination operates to exclude Palestinians in the context of digital
media spaces. The security trap of the incitement law creates anxiety
for Palestinians wanting to voice their opinions online whether as
activists or simply as members of society in everyday life.
Race- thinking and lawfare
The arbitrary application of the law appears to be a factor only for
Palestinians who are racialized as security threats, which includes
citizens of Israel as well as those in the West Bank and Gaza (Adalah,
2016; Silver, 2016; Nashif, 2016).
Within Israel, only Arab (read Palestinian) Israelis have been arrested,
detained and charged with incitement for social media posts. Since the
law has not been used against Jewish Israelis (Adalah, 2016; Silver,
2016; Nashif, 2016), those deemed suspect occurs along racialized
lines. Not only is the racist practice of the law evident through the
numbers of Palestinians who have been arrested, but Adalah (2015)
argues that their own attempts to have the Israeli Attorney General
open up cases of investigations and prosecutions related to Jewish
Israelis
104 Madalena Santos
who have clearly articulated incitements to violence have been denied
(see also Addameer, 2015b). These include statements made by former
Foreign Minister Avigdor Lieberman, the head of the Yisrael Beiteinu
political party and current Minister of Education and then Minister of
Industry, Trade and Labor, Naftali Bennett. In their argument to the
Attorney General, Adalah (2016) offered evidence that:
[Lieberman claimed that for Palestinian citizens of Israel] ‘who are

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 159 ---
against us, we must raise the axe and cut off their heads’ while …
Bennett, the leader of the Jewish Home political party, stated in 2013
‘I’ve killed many Arabs in my life, there’s no problem with that.’
In direct contrast to Palestinians who have been arrested and detained
for either obscure posts, such as the arrest and detainment of 15-year-
old Tamara Abu-Laban for writing ‘forgive me’ on her Facebook (see
Nashif, 2016) to the – at most – less questionable provocations of
violence, the fact that these statements were made by prominent
Ministers appears to have borne no weight to their potential impact
and influence over others.
As noted in the above section, the critical deployment of the concept
of lawfare brings together the notion of ‘law’ and ‘warfare’ to offer a
way to articulate how law and legal measures are used to justify what
would otherwise be considered criminal actions by the state (Hajjar,
2012). As ‘an amalgamation of
“law” and “warfare”, [lawfare] has been adopted and popularized
among people engaged in monitoring, judging and debating the
legality of a state’s wartime behavior vis- à-vis enemies on and off the
battlefield’ (Hajjar, 2012). It has also enabled the interrogation of the
use of arms- length organizations affiliated with states who work on
their behalf. For instance, through ‘lawfare contractors’ in the United
States, Israel has gone so far as to sue the providers of the social
media site Facebook for failing to remove posts by Palestinians that
they claim are ‘incitements to violence’ (Rotem, 2016b). The right-
wing Israeli legal group Shurat HaDin which operates mostly on
Jewish- Amer ican donations filed a law suit against Facebook for
compensation for Israeli victims in the US Federal Court (Rotem,
2016b). Shurat HaDin ‘alleges that by allowing Hamas to use its social
networking and communications platforms … Facebook provides
material support to the Palestinian group in attacks on Amer ican
citizens in Israel and the West Bank’ (Rotem, 2016b). Israel’s security
apparatus has directly pressured social media platforms such Facebook
and Twitter to remove posts it perceives as ‘criminal’, but as Yonah
Jeremy Bob (2016) notes the wide sweeping powers that Justice
Minister Ayelet Shaked and Public Security Minister Gilad Erdan
proposed in a
bill for removing terrorism- promoting content from the Internet and
various social media platforms … give the state vast powers that it
does not possess in standard proceedings. The state could potentially
seek a court

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 160 ---
The criminalization of social media 105
order for removal without giving notice to the social media platform,
introduce classified evidence and introduce evidence that would not
normally be admissible.
Israel’s use of lawfare enables the police and other security agencies to
‘entrap’ Palestinian activists as well as Palestinians who are not
actively involved in social movements but who are supportive of
resistance. Majd Atwan, a 22-year- old beautician, is a clear example
of someone who was arrested and detained for simply expressing her
opinion (Samidoun, 2016).
According to Deger (2016):
Majd’s lawyer, Tareq Barghouti, told Al Jazeera that the Israeli
military had monitored his client for two months before arriving at
her house to make the arrest. In a statement made to Aljazeera,
Atwan’s parents said that Israeli military surrounded their home at
2am in order to arrest Majd.
(Deger, 2016)
Her father remarked on the arbitrariness of her arrest. As he said ‘My
daughter is not politically active; rather, her involvements are in
beauty … Did she write stuff online? Yeah, like all other Palestinians.
I really don’t understand why they chose to arrest her’ (Deger, 2016).
As Atwan (Deger, 2016; see also Dorell & Shuttleworth, 2016) clearly
expressed in her defiant statement in Ofer military court after being
sentenced to 45 days of imprisonment and fined 3000 NIS
($794 US): ‘Your occupation to [ sic] our land does not need
“incitement” for our People to revolt. I am part of an occupied People
… so don’t expect me to greet you with flowers instead of anger’.
Bringing the issue of land to the fore, as an Indigenous Palestinian
woman Atwan voices the shared sentiment of the continued injustice
of Israeli occupation as well as the right of an occupied people to
resist.
Administrative detention, secret files
The new Israeli Anti- Terror law enacted in June 2016 covers legal
action with regard to the accusation of incitement through social
media and enables exceptional measures, which allow for arbitrary
detention and secret evidence. The new law of exceptionalism

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 161 ---
presented in the Anti- Terror law is tied to its settler colonial aims.
With this law, which encapsulates ‘incitement’, Israel has the power to
arrest and detain and also sentence Palestinians on a case- by-case
basis. According to Adalah (2016), the new Anti- Terror Law combines
British Mandate- era Emergency Regulations as well as other state of
exception regulations found in Israel’s emergency laws based on
racialized logics, which mark people as suspect ‘simply [due] to the
fact that they are Arab’. Adalah (2016) further remarks that the
twinning of Palestinian’s political activity within Israel proper with
acts of terror ‘criminalizes the social and political affinity that
106 Madalena Santos
Palestinian citizens of Israel feel with the Palestinian people and their
just struggle against the occupation’. In making even the discussion of
political and social injustice illegal, the expression of Palestinian views
whether in social movement organizing and mobilization or as
ordinary citizens becomes criminal.
Meanwhile, as Charlotte Silver (2016) notes, when it comes to calls
for vio-vio-
lence against Palestinians, Jewish Israelis are excluded in the
application of the new law. As Silver states,
While Israeli ministers have launched a global campaign against
alleged incitement by Palestinians, they have remained totally silent
about calls for violence by Israelis against Palestinians on Facebook –
perhaps the most notorious case being by justice minister [Ayelet]
Shaked herself.
The contradiction is not an anomaly but is central to the practice of
differential citizenship rights understood through the logic of
exceptionalism.
At 17 years of age, Mohammad Amarneh who had no history of
security misconduct was placed under administrative detention for
‘perform[ing] an action against the security of the state’ (Addameer,
2016). His detention was upheld by the military judge Rafael Yamini
who stated that he ‘is convinced that the child poses a threat to the
security of the state’ (Addameer, 2016).
Until today, Mohammad and his lawyer have been unable to see the
evidence against him or to know what ‘threat’ he poses because his
file is secret. His lawyer was informed by the prosecution that ‘part of
the accusations are connected to incitement through Mohammad’s

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 162 ---
Facebook account’. Mohammad has denied the accusations against
him and claims not to be ‘affiliated with a political faction’
(Addameer, 2016). Similarly, 22-year- old Jureen Saeed Omar Qadah
was issued with a three- month administrative detention order on 1
November 2015 (Addameer, 2015b). Like Mohammad, her request to
release the secret evidence against her was rejected by the military
judge, but was told that ‘she blessed and supported the murders
committed against Israelis on her Facebook account’ (Addameer,
2015b). Although the judge agreed that Jureen ‘is not affiliated with
any political faction’, she was deemed a threat to
the security of the state (Addameer, 2015b).2
According to Addameer, these cases highlight Israel’s use of
‘administrative detention as a tool of suppression, punishment,
reprisal and in violation of the right of expression’. Addameer (2016)
argues that Israel’s regular use of administrative detention is in clear
violation of ‘the Fourth Geneva Convention and other international
human rights conventions … [which guarantee] prisoners the right to
a fair trial and only allow … the use of administrative detention in
special circumstances and as an alternative for actual trial’. Israel’s use
of state of exception measures, however, enables it to violate
international law without being held accountable to what could
amount to war crimes.
The criminalization of social media 107
Possibilities and limitations of digital movements
Israel’s targeting of Palestinian activists and social movements is not a
new phenomenon. Indeed, the Israeli secret service and military have
often gone as far as assassinating political activists (Alston, 2010, pp.
5–7; Hajjar, 2012; Weizman, 2007, pp. 238, 250), while the arrest and
detainment of Palestinians involved in political parties, such as the
recent administrative detention of Khalida Jarrar (Hass, 2016), are
neither novel nor extraordinary by Israel’s standards of unrestrained
biopower. But digital technologies are making it even easier for Israel
to identify people they label as threats using the rationale of public
social media postings as ‘incitements to violence’. The control and
regulation of online technologies is also significant to the question of
possibilities for social media as a space and tool for political activism
and also for everyday conversations of daily life in Palestine since its
usage relies on having access to cyberspace. As Tawil- Souri (2012,
pp. 37–38) argues, ‘the borders of the technological may be less

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 163 ---
visible than the walls, gates, fences, and checkpoints of the physical
world, but they are no less real and significant politically’. These
technological borders are also fundamentally tied to Israel’s ongoing
control of the land, as demonstrated in its digital search for anyone
who may disrupt its settler colonial claims.
Miriyam Aouragh’s (2012, p. 527) concept of ‘bounded protest’ helps
to underscore ‘how the ability of new media to transcend domination
and facilitate political mobilization is limited while the offline
circumstances of asymmetrical power relations remain unchanged’.
Aouragh (2012) argues that while social media platforms such as
Facebook offer an opportunity for activists to share information and
mobilize for action, the structure of social media including its
ownership limits its potential as a tool and space for political activism.
As Aouragh (2012, p. 532) states ‘social networking sites like
Facebook are not a social network but a social- networking-tool’. De
Vries, Simry, and Maoz (2015) echo Aouragh’s concerns in their study
on the mobilizing possibilities for social media in the wake of the war
on Gaza in 2014. They argue that because social media is controlled
by people outside of the social movement itself, its usage is not only
susceptible to the controls of the owners of the media platform but
also to the powerful interventions of the state, including the covert
surveillance of social justice activism. The case of Majd Atwan offers
direct evidence of the use of covert surveillance since she was
monitored for two months before her arrest.
In addition to the technological realities of digital media as a tool of
state surveillance, it is important to remember that the architectures
and rules of social media platforms, although still evolving, were
designed for commercial interests, which prohibits anonymity and
could have detrimental impacts on resistance movements (Youmans &
York, 2012, p. 317).
The control and regulation of online technologies is also significant to
the question of possibilities for social media as a space and tool for
political activism since its usage relies on having access to cyberspace.
Israel restricts and controls
108 Madalena Santos
most telecommunications in the West Bank and Gaza (Bannoura
2015). Area C
which makes up 70 per cent of the West Bank is under full Israeli
control. Palestinian access to telecommunications, including network

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 164 ---
services, networking equipment, data and mobile services, is therefore
subject to ‘the consent and approval of the occupier’ (Abuznaid, 2016,
p. 91). Indeed, scholars such as Morozov (2011) warn of the use of
social media as a mechanism of state repression. The fact that
Palestinians, such as Mohammad Amarneh and Jureen Saeed Omar
Qadah, could be held in administrative detention under the pretence
of security based on secret evidence from social media posts illustrates
Israel’s continued violation of international law. Israel’s new Anti-
Terror law, through its state of emergency claims, extends its ability to
use legal measures to interrupt legal norms.
Despite the limitations for digital media as a tool and space for social
movements, while noting the potential dangers of new media, other
scholars have discussed the possible avenues for resistance that new
media may offer (Arafa & Armstrong, 2016; Bennett, 2003; Bonilla &
Rosa, 2015; Shalhoub- Kevorkian, 2011; Segerberg & Bennett, 2011).
Tawil- Souri (2012, p. 28) notes the paradoxes of high technology
which ‘has become one of the means through which Israeli occupation
continues, [yet] the high- tech infrastructure in the Gaza Strip
– that which is used by Palestinians as opposed to the Israeli regime –
is also a space of control’. In her work on women’s resistance in
Palestine, Nadera Shalhoub- Kevorkian (2011) similarly states that
while cyberspace ‘can be a source of danger when the space is bound
to the logic of security, surveillance strategies, and politics’, it can also
‘serve as a site of resistance and a tool of power in the hands of the
occupied’. Bonilla and Rosa (2015) further suggest that social media
may open up sites of contestation and offer alternative narratives to
mainstream media versions of events. As a space and tool of
resistance, social media ‘can become an important site in which to
foreground the particular ways in which racialized bodies are
systematically stereotyped, stigma-tized, surveilled, and positioned as
targets of state- sanctioned violence’ (Bonilla
& Rosa, 2015, p. 9). As Bishara (2015) argues, the regulation of
racialized Palestinian bodies is not really about security but about
colonial control. Changing the narrative by organizing and framing
the discourse and framing the conversation is arguably one of the
greatest strengths that social media offers (see Hossam al- Hamalawi
in Aouragh, 2012, p. 532). Is it possible that the relatively unknown
Tatour and Dweik will transform the conversation? In gaining more
media attention than they would have if not arrested there is an
increased possibility that their narratives of settler colonial state
suppression will be read by people who may not have known about
them otherwise.

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 165 ---
Conclusion
This chapter has focused on Israel’s recent arrests and detainment of
Palestinians who are both social justice activists and also those who
would not consider themselves directly as part of a resistance
movement, highlighting five cases that
The criminalization of social media 109
have received attention in the media as well as by human and legal
rights organizations. As argued here, the fundamental settler colonial
logics of elimination and exceptionalism are readily identifiable in
Israel’s advanced online surveillance tactics and new laws concerning
terrorism. While digital media provides a platform for social
networking and organizing as well as an arena for voicing opinion,
this chapter’s cursory look at what is happening to Palestinians in
Palestine reveals the settler colonial state’s use of surveillance into not
only organized resistance, but also everyday life.
Although research on surveillance of oppressed groups provides a rich
legal and juridical account of criminalization processes (Crosby &
Monaghan, 2012; Sethna & Hewitt, 2009; Van Gelder, 2010), it lacks a
qualitative engagement that reports on the subject experiences of
surveillance. Since most literature on colonial surveillance is archival
in its orientation, there is a need to explore settler colonial practices
as contemporary lived experiences (Coulthard, 2014; Veracini, 2015).
Moreover, given that ‘in March 2016 [alone], 148 Palestinians were
arrested … [on the grounds of incitement for social media posts and
that 28 of them were women]’ (Abu Amer, 2016; see also Days of
Palestine, 2016), further research is needed to examine the
intersection of gender, race and resistance with surveillance in settler
colonial states. This study supports the view that more research is
needed to consider the responses taken by the actors under
surveillance. The outcomes of such studies will inform broader
debates surrounding surveillance practices, social sorting, and the
intersection of surveillance, race and colonization in the digital world
and offer a critical contribution to understanding the impacts of
digital media on Indigenous resistance.
