Chapter 6

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 178 ---
Between visibility and surveillance
Challenges to anti- corporate activism
in social media
Julie Uldam
Introduction
With their popular appeal and multimodal affordances, commercial
social media have reinvigorated hopes for the potential of the internet
to provide social movements with new possibilities for increased
visibility. However, the proliferation of social media also provides
government and corporate actors with new possibilities for monitoring
social movements that they consider a potential risk. For governments,
such risks are often construed in terms of national security (Deibert &
Rohozinski, 2010; see also Pickerill, 2006). For corporations, they are
construed as reputational risks (Uldam and Hansen, 2017; Bennett,
2003). Government surveillance of activists is well- documented in
both scholarly research and the media (Juris, 2005; Monaghan &
Walby, 2012). However, corporate monitoring of social movements
remains significantly under- researched (Lubbers, 2012; Pickerill,
2003). Recent research has started to address corporate organisations’
uses of social media for identifying issues, contexts, events and groups
that can potentially damage their reputations (Trottier & Lyon, 2012),
including the predictive capabilities of ‘big data’ (Andrejevic, 2014;
Trottier, 2012). This chapter sheds light on an aspect of corporate
monitoring in social media that is so far under-researched:
corporations’ monitoring of individual activists across social media
and in offline contexts. In this way, it goes beyond the different ways
in which corporations process and analyse large datasets from online
sources and explores how they (1) monitor and (2) discuss strategies
for responding to the activities of individual activists, specifically in
social media.
The ways in which companies monitor activists in social media is
central to our understanding of the emancipatory potential of social
media, because it potentially impedes civil society actors’ possibilities
for holding business to account. It does so because corporate
surveillance is driven by an interest in profit (Mansell, 2011). This
entails protecting a carefully constructed brand from reputational
threats posed by activists who point to discrepancies between the
brand and the companies’ operations and practices (Bennett, 2003).

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 179 ---
The negative social and environmental consequences of high- profile
disasters have rendered the oil industry a site of controversy, with
civil society groups
116 Julie Uldam
bringing companies’ problematic practices into the limelight, notably
through social media (Du & Viera, 2012). In response, companies have
tried to protect and repair their reputation by participating in
voluntary initiatives and sponsorships, typically framed as
sustainability and corporate social responsibility (CSR) (Chouliaraki &
Morsing, 2010). Such initiatives have become staple elements in
corporations’ strife for legitimacy so as to meet the expectations of
many of their stakeholders. However, such initiatives are also
contested by civil society actors as greenwashing, a mechanism that
serves to divert attention from social and environmental consequences
of fossil fuel extraction and processing. In response, oil companies try
to anticipate criticism that challenges the ethico-political purposes of
their sustainability and CSR initiatives.
In exploring this aspect of corporate surveillance, this chapter is
organised in four sections. First, it outlines the theoretical framework,
which draws on conceptions of (mediated) visibility and business-
society interactions in the CSR
literature and in media studies. Second, the case of BP’s responses to
individual critics is introduced along with details about the data and
methods that inform this analysis. Third, the focus of the chapter is
contextualised by a cursory overview of contested events in the oil
industry as well as BP’s sustainability and CSR initiatives, focusing on
their sponsorship activities. Fourth, the analysis examines BP’s
responses to individual critics as practices of management of visibility.
Social media, activism and corporate surveillance
Social media have spurred hopes that the internet can help civil
society hold government and corporate actors to account for their
operations (Hestres, 2013; Bennett, 2003). For example, the internet’s
possibilities for civil society actors to bypass mass media gatekeepers
potentially enables bringing government secrets to the public domain
(Cammaerts, 2013). Similarly, it potentially enables ‘hold[ing] a
corporate logo hostage in the media until shareholders or corporate
managers regard the bad publicity as an independent threat to a
carefully cultivated brand image’ (Bennett, 2003, p. 152). However,
this is a two-way street, as the internet and its regulation facilitates

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 180 ---
both the visibility and audibility of civil society but also government
and corporate monitoring, and sometimes censoring, of dissenting
views (Curran, Fenton & Freedman, 2012; Pickerill, 2006).
On the importance of visibility
In the management literature focused on CSR, social media are often
examined in terms of reputational threats that corporations need to
reduce (e.g. Schultz et al., 2013). Less corporate- centric accounts
provide optimistic views of the empowering potential that social
media visibility affords civil society actors vis- à-vis corporations. For
example, Whelan, Moon and Grant (2013, p. 777) suggest that
https://sanet.st/blogs/polatebooks/
Between visibility and surveillance 117
within social media- augmented ‘public arenas of citizenship’,
individual citizens are empowered, relative to corporations and their
(functional/
formally organized) stakeholders, when it comes to creating, debating,
and publicizing, CSR relevant issues.
While this optimism is tempered with the acknowledgement that such
civic empowerment is ‘partly contingent upon the corporations (and
governments) who control social media (and who continue to possess
significant capacities more generally) doing so in a responsible way’
(Whelan et al., 2013, p. 786), the role of non- Information and
Communications Technology (non- ICT) companies in influencing the
empowering potential of social media remains unexplored.
In media and communication studies, corporate surveillance has been
given attention in relation to ICT companies’ and private security
companies’ surveillance of activists, often in collaboration with
government agencies (Fuchs, 2013; McChesney, 2013), as well as the
ways in which the internet and its regulation often facilitate
government and corporate surveillance of dissenting views
(Cammaerts, 2013; Dahlgren, 2013; Monaghan & Walby, 2012).
However, specific cases of online corporate monitoring and censoring
of activists remain under- researched, with exceptions including
Lubbers’ (2009, 2012) and Pickerill’s (2003) work. In her research on
corporate spying on activists, Lubbers (2009) shows how companies
such as Unilever and Shell have used external intelligence agencies to
‘help companies understand and engage with the growing living space
that is the internet’ by monitoring of activists’ online activity (Internet

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 181 ---
intelligence agency Infonic, cited in Lubbers 2009, p. 236).
Similarly, in her research on cyberactivism, Pickerill (2003) identifies
a number of strategies that companies adopt to counter activists’
criticism, including monitoring of activists’ online activity as well as
attempts to have activist websites removed. Here, she reports concerns
from the anti- Shell activist group, (S)hell, that the company had
requested to have the (S)hell website taken down, and that this was
denied by Shell’s Internet Manager at the time (Pickerill, 2003).
In this chapter, I contribute to these important exceptions by
examining responses to online criticism by BP, including the
company’s monitoring of activists’ online activity and attempts to
have their websites removed, from the perspective of mediated
visibility.
Mediated visibility
Thompson’s (2005) notion of mediated visibility can help capture and
further explore the ambiguous properties of social media. In pointing
to the role of the media as key to the relation between visibility and
power, Thompson (2005) departs from Foucault’s notion of the
panopticon and the idea that the visibility of the many works as a
means of control. Instead he argues that in our media-saturated
society, political actors are increasingly visible to wider publics, and
118 Julie Uldam
that this entails both reputational opportunities and challenges. On
the one hand, the media grant visibility to (media- savvy) political
actors independent of spatial- temporal locales. On the other hand, the
conducts of politicians are made visible in uncontrollable ways by the
media. This development is augmented and made more complex by
the proliferation of internet technologies as they simultaneously
provide new possibilities for staging self- representations and render
political leaders increasingly vulnerable to the scrutiny of civil society
actors (Thompson, 2005). These challenges are no less relevant to
corporations. Internet technologies enable civil society to expose
corporate practices and misconduct to wider publics (Bennett, 2005).
In this way, it is those in power, including political and corporate
actors, for whom it has become more difficult to hide their activities
and control the disclosure of information (Chouliaraki & Morsing,
2010; Hansen & Flyverbom, 2014). This increased synoptic visibility
has brought about new reputational challenges for companies (Whelan
et al., 2013).

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 182 ---
However, internet technologies not only make visible political and
corporate actors. They also grant visibility to civil society actors. This
entails potential for both empowerment and disempowerment.
The double- edged sword of social media visibility for
activists
Civil society actors’ exposure of corporate misconduct and
inconsistencies between a company’s CSR programme and business
operations relies on their possibilities for visibility. The popular
appeal of social media has made them key platforms for activists’
circulation of calls for action and reports from protest events to wider
publics. At the same time, this visibility enables companies to monitor
and collect information about activists’ activities (Uldam, 2016).
Consequently, social media are also key platforms for surveillance,
augmenting mutual surveillance practices (Trottier, 2012). In this
way, visibility and surveillance are increasingly interlinked in social
media contexts. In his discussion of visibility as a conceptual lens, for
understanding social, technical and political arrangements Brighenti
(2010a, p. 46) proposes three ‘models of visibility’: (1) visibility as
recognition; (2) visibility and arcana imperii; and (3) the visibility of
spectacle. In particular, visibility as recognition and visibility and
arcana imperii help capture the dual capacity of visibility as both
empowering and disempowering in the context of social media.
Visibility as recognition points to the significance of being seen to
identity formation and being acknowledged in society. Thus, issues
such as poverty, class and identity politics rely on visibility in order to
be addressed (Brighenti, 2010a). In addition, criticism leveraged
against neoliberal deregulation as in the case of Occupy Wall Street or
authoritarian regimes in the Arab Spring require visibility to be
recognised and influence public debate (Dahlgren, 2013). Similarly,
their invisibility leads to exclusion.
Between visibility and surveillance 119
The second model that is particularly salient to our understanding of
the role of social media in interactions between business and civil
society actors is visibility of control, arcana imperii. Arcana imperii
points to the role of invisibility in strengthening power. This is tied to
the capacity of invisibility to hide centres and agents of power.
Drawing on Foucault’s work on discipline, Brighenti (2010a) argues
that the invisibility of the watcher to those being watched produces
practices of self- discipline by instilling a sense of being suspected.

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 183 ---
This implies that the asymmetries of visibility are twofold. First, they
enable centres and agents of power to exercise control over subjects
through surveillance rather than force. Second, their routinisation and
invisibility creates asymmetry between those aware of the surveillance
and those unaware of it by obscuring exactly what is being observed,
on what basis and logics and instilling uncertainty in those being
watched (Brighenti, 2010b).
Brighenti’s third model of visibility, the visibility of the spectacle,
points to a regime of visibility that privileges the spectacular. This
echoes Chouliaraki’s (2006, 2007) work on media representations of
distant suffering in which she shows how the visibility of suffering in
Western media is contingent on possibilities for presenting their
situation as a spectacle, whether as a dramatised personal narrative or
a battle of universal values. In outlining his model, Brighenti draws on
Thompson (2005) to argue that the visibility afforded by the spectacle
is often uncontrollable, revealing to wider publics matters that were
intended to remain hidden. While Brighenti’s notion of the visibility of
the spectacle thus revolves around politicians and their simultaneous
need to for visibility and invisibility, it also bears relevance for
activists. Activists often stage spectacular protest events to gain media
visibility, even though the spectacular (whether material damage or
theatricality) risks rendering them vulnerable to negative media
coverage (Askanius & Uldam, 2011; Uldam, 2013). Nonetheless, the
model of the visibility of the spectacle is less imminently relevant to
the focus of this chapter – corporate surveillance of individual activists
in social media.
In particular, the two models of visibility as recognition and visibility
and arcana imperii capture the empowering and disempowering
potential of social media. Their empowering potential relates to their
widespread use, ease of access (in some countries), no- fee access, and
possibilities for bypassing mass-media filters, which provides non-
elite actors with new possibilities for making themselves and their
causes visible to wider publics – a condition for, and payoff of,
democratic participation (Dahlgren, 2013). Their disempowering risks
relate to the interrelations of visibility and control. The visibility
afforded by social media operates asymmetrically, enabling
governments and corporations to monitor citizens’ activities without
being seen themselves (Fuchs et al., 2013).
In this way, social media augment visibility asymmetries by rendering
them less transparent and accountable (Brighenti, 2010a). This
chapter explores how these dimensions of visibility are played out in
social media by examining corporate monitoring of individual

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 184 ---
activists. In doing so, it considers both the power structures within
which social media are embedded and media practices,
120 Julie Uldam
i.e. ‘what are people (individuals, groups, institutions) doing in
relation to media’ – two interrelated and essential aspects of the role
of the media in society (Couldry, 2012, p. 37).
The case study and methodological reflections
The study in this chapter draws on a variety of data related to
criticism of oil companies in the UK. The material includes files on
specific civil society individuals obtained through Subject Access
Requests (SARs) under the Data Protection Act 1998.1 The files are
based on four SARs submitted to BP. Each file is between 14 and 28
pages and dates from April to August 2012. The files contain email
correspondence between BP employees (including photos and links to
video footage), emails sent to other critics and activist networks by
the individuals who have submitted the Subject Access Requests, and
a ‘BP AGM
12th April 2012 Major Personality Report’ with biographical
information about individual critics. The BP files have been redacted.
In particular, names of senders and receivers are crossed out, as are
references to individuals other than those who have submitted SARs.
Further, some documents have been omitted, because the requested
information ‘no longer exists’ or might ‘prejudice commercial
interests’.2 In addition, the study draws on interviews with activists
from the climate justice movement in the UK conducted in 2013.
I was able to obtain the data for this study through my involvement
with the climate justice movement in the UK. This double position as
an activist and researcher helps gain access to insights that are
otherwise hidden (Roseneil, 1995), as is often the case with corporate
and governmental surveillance.
However, it inevitably also entails taken- for-granted observations, as
the researcher has both blind spots and shared sympathies (Plows,
2008). In this case, this has particularly had ethical implications, as
activists often experience repression from those they contest (Hintz &
Milan, 2010). Therefore, informed consent has been obtained from all
individuals who have contributed data for this study, both in relation
to Subject Access files and interviews. Furthermore, all individuals
have been anonymised.

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 185 ---
In exploring the case of BP, the chapter particularly focuses on the
company’s practices of visibility management in the UK. BP is listed
on the London Stock Exchange, and is a constituent of the Financial
Times Stock Exchange 100 Index in the UK. Therefore, much of their
effort to manage their visibility vis- à-vis non- institutional actors is
focused on the UK, and also when it relates to issues in countries
where the extraction takes place such as Nigeria (Cherry
& Sneirson, 2011; Holzer, 2008).
Controversy, sponsorships and critique
Since the late 1990s, oil companies have tried to counter the
(reputational) impact of social and environmental problems brought
about by their operations
Between visibility and surveillance 121
by participating in various forms of voluntary initiatives and
sponsorships, typically under the heading of sustainability and
corporate social responsibility (Livesey, 2001). The growing
engagement of corporations in these issues reflects how the oil
industry has become an arena for controversies and conflicts (Den
Hond et al., 2014; Du & Vieira, 2012).
Controversial issues in the oil industry
One of the most notorious events in the oil industry is the Brent Spar
incident.
In 1995, Shell’s plans to dump the Brent Spar oil storage platform in
the North Atlantic were blocked, as Greenpeace initiated a campaign
that brought the plans into the limelight (Livesey, 2001). The same
year Shell was criticised for not making an effort to intervene when
the Nigerian military regime of Sani Abacha executed nine Ogoni
activists who had opposed Shell for exploiting their people and land
and for the devastating environmental and social consequences of oil
exploration and production in the Niger Delta (Livesey, 2001).
In 2010, BP was brought into the limelight when their Deepwater
Horizon platform spilled 780,000 m3 of oil into the Gulf of Mexico
(Du & Viera, 2012).
Following BP’s attempt to clean up the area, local communities are
still experiencing oil surface in areas deemed ‘clean’ by BP, sickness
from toxic exposure, and a collapse in fish stocks and local

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 186 ---
livelihoods. Not only major environmental and social disasters have
caused controversy, but also growing political and public attention to
climate change, with civil society actors contesting the political
economy and ethics of fossil fuel extraction more broadly. Most
recently, both BP’s and Shell’s plans to drill in the Arctic have
generated criticism.
Countering controversy: CSR and sponsorships
BP has responded to mounting criticism by establishing CSR
(corporate social responsibility) departments, publishing sustainability
reports and inviting NGOs to develop partnerships with them. The
company withdrew from the Global Climate Coalition, a lobby group
against reductions in greenhouse gas emis-sions, in 1997 (Livesey,
2001). BP was rebranded as ‘bp: beyond petroleum’ in 2000, intended
to conjure up an image of BP as an energy company rather than just
an oil company (Beder, 2002). BP also approached NGOs such as Save
the Children and Oxfam to propose ‘partnerships’ (Beder, 2002).
These CSR initiatives can be seen as part of oil companies’
management of visibility. As such they are complemented by yet
another set of activities: sponsorships. The sponsorships that BP
provides cover a wide range of activities, including conferences on
climate change, educational initiatives, and cultural-and sports-
related activities and events. This study focuses on sponsorship of
cultural- and sports- related activities. Such sponsorships constitute a
popular strategy for companies to appear as conscientious corporate
citizens (Lam et al.,
122 Julie Uldam
2013). Over the course of the last two decades, BP has been increasing
its sponsorship of art and culture in the UK (Chong, 2013; Liberate
Tate, 2012). In 2011, BP renewed its sponsorship of four of the UK’s
major cultural institutions, the British Museum, the National Portrait
Gallery, the Royal Opera House, and Tate Britain. Between them, the
four cultural institutions will receive nearly
£10 million from 2011 to 2016 (BP, 2011). BP was one of the ‘Tier
One’ sponsors of the 2012 Olympics. In addition, the company
sponsored the Cultural Olympiad, which included a Shakespeare
Festival. The sponsorship involved the appointment of BP as
Sustainability Partner of the Games. BP publicises the sponsorships as
‘a meaningful contribution to society … enabling people around the
country and the world to connect through the experience of

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 187 ---
outstanding exhibitions and performances, promoting ideas and
encouraging creativity’ (BP, 2011). Together, sustainability initiatives
and sponsorships help portray oil companies such as BP as good
corporate citizens that make a positive contribution to our society.
However, activists contest these initiatives as green- washing,
designed to divert attention from their business operations and their
effect on people and the environment.
Visibility as recognition: activism and the empowering
potential of social media
A wide variety of activist groups and networks have criticised oil
companies’
management of visibility, including the ways in which they have
engaged with the issues of sustainability and sponsorship. The
diversity of the actors can be seen as a spectrum ranging from
mainstream NGOs at one end to radical groups campaigning for
systemic change at the other. The mainstream end of the spectrum
includes established NGOs such as Oxfam and WWF, who work within
existing structures of governance by lobbying, and sometimes forming
partnerships with, governments, policy- makers and corporations. In
addition to lobbying, their repertoires for action revolve around
petitions, research and aid (Den Hond & De Bakker, 2007).
In the UK, for more than a decade, a variety of activist groups and
organisations have provided critique specifically of oil companies’
sponsorships, contesting them as measures to protect the companies’
reputation and distract attention from controversial issues.
In bringing visibility to these practices and their consequences, the
groups draw on a range of different tactics. Despite their diverse
organisational and resource- related make- up, the groups collaborate
on their common causes as a part of the climate justice movement. In
protesting against oil sponsorships of the UK’s cultural institutions and
events, their repertoires for action involve creative interventions.
In 2012, these interventions included the Greenwash Gold campaign,
which contested the choice of BP, Dow Chemicals and Rio Tinto as
sponsors for the London 2012 Olympic Games by inviting members of
the public to vote online
Between visibility and surveillance 123
for the worst corporate sponsor of the Olympics. It culminated with a

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 188 ---
mock award ceremony in Trafalgar Square, where three activists who
pretended to be corporate representatives from BP, Dow and Rio Tinto
were awarded gold medals for being the worst corporate sponsors of
the Olympics, before having green custard poured over their heads.
Another 2012 intervention consisted of a series of pop- up
performances of Shakespeare- inspired scripts at the BP-sponsored
Shakespeare exhibition at the British Museum and before the Royal
Shakespeare Company’s performances at the World Shakespeare
Festival in Stratford- upon-Avon.3
The online mediation of these interventions plays a key role in
reaching wider publics. To achieve this, the groups use a range of
online platforms. These include both commercial and alternative
online media: their websites, YouTube, Vimeo, Facebook, Twitter and
Indymedia. These online platforms serve two main purposes when
drawing attention to the discrepancies between oil companies’
operations and their reputation construction through sponsorships: (1)
to facilitate the viral circulation of visual and multimodal
documentation of actions after the event has taken place; and (2) to
access traditional mass media. The former relies on the capacity of
social media for reaching beyond the confines of the activist
community, but due to the internet’s properties as a ‘pull medium’
which is argued to merely connect likeminded users (Cammaerts,
2007), traditional mass media still play a central role in reaching new
sympathisers and influencing public opinion. The online mediation of
offline actions, such as spectacular or exceptional videos or images,
can help generate mass media attention (Uldam, 2013).
This was a key aspect of the campaigns discussed here so as to ensure
visibility beyond those present at the events. The best- attended
interventions had up to 2500 spectators (inside the Royal Festival
Hall), while some interventions had as few as a few hundred.
Therefore, social media played a key role in enabling footage and
photos from the interventions go viral, both in terms of numbers and
reach.
Without online mediation – and its uptake in traditional mass media –
the interventions would remain largely unnoticed. For example, the
video and tweets from the Reclaim Shakespeare Company’s first
guerrilla Shakespeare intervention at the Royal Shakespeare
Company’s performance of the Tempest in April 2012
helped gain coverage in mainstream media outlets, including the
Guardian 4 and LA Times.5 In this way, social media potentially
enable the circulation of visual and multimodal documentation of

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 189 ---
actions so as to reach users beyond their immediate constituency of
activists and access traditional mass media. However, this mediated
visibility simultaneously enables oil companies to monitor activists’
activities in these online media.
Visibility and arcana empirii: BP’s monitoring of
activists in social media
This section analyses how BP, as part of its broader projects of
management of visibility, monitors activists’ activities in online social
media. This is often done
124 Julie Uldam
through contracting with risk assessment and Public Relations
agencies. One example is the risk assessment agency, Exclusive
Analysis. Exclusive Analysis produces analyses of socio- political
environments so as to ‘forecast reputation risks’ for a variety of
sectors, including the oil and gas industry.6 In May 2012, the Head of
Indicators and Warning from Exclusive Analysis contacted a freelance
documentary photographer who had covered political protests in the
UK
for news media, including the Guardian. In the email, he described his
job as
‘produc[ing] objective forecasts of civil unrest in the UK’. He
explained that he had been ‘analysing the actions of many of the
groups that you [the freelance documentary photographer] have
encountered over the years’, just as he had
‘follow[ed] Climate Camp, Rising Tide, UK Tar Sands Network and UK
Uncut and others regularly on social media’ (email, 2 May 2012). This
illustrates the importance that companies attach to the role of social
media in their assessment of risk, including reputational risks, and
that attention is paid to specific groups rather than just social
movements more broadly.7 While there is no specific mention of oil
companies, Exclusive Analysis specialises in providing risk assessment
to the energy sector. Moreover, several of the groups that the agency
monitors are concerned with issues of climate change, and with a
history of criticising oil companies such as BP.
Two strategies of management of visibility can be discerned: (1) a
strategy of anticipation; and (2) a strategy of containment. Both

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 190 ---
strategies work to contain criticism and thus eliminate the visibility of
conflict from the public sphere.
Social media constitute the main arena in which these practices of
surveillance take place. In emails obtained under the Data Protection
Act, BP8 report on the activities of their critics based on their online
mediation via social media, including Vimeo and Twitter. As a
covering letter from BP’s Regional Privacy Adviser explains:
BP plc collects information about you and UK Tar Sands Network from
a range of publicly available sources, including Facebook and Twitter.
(Letter from BP’s Regional Privacy Adviser, 24 October 2012)
The focus in the material obtained through SARs is on individual
citizens rather than the general sentiment of (a particular group of )
activists. The documents include information about and photos of
individuals who have submitted Subject Access Requests to BP.
Monitoring activists: anticipating the visibility of
individuals
Monitoring of critics in social media as part of BP’s regular
stakeholder regime entails the singling out of individuals for further
monitoring. This is illustrated by the two documents ‘Major
Personality Report’ and ‘Individuals of note’ (see
Figures 6.1 and 6.2).

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 191 ---
Between visibility and surveillance 125
Figure 6.1 The front page of BP’s ‘Major Personality Report’ 2012.
Source: image supplied by person who obtained the files through a
Subject Access Request.
Figure 6.2 Snapshot from top of a profile page in BP’s Individuals of
Note document.
Source: profile page supplied by person who obtained the files
through a Subject Access Request.
The Major Personality Report from BP’s 2012 AGM is compiled by the
company’s security division, Group Security. It contains biographical
information, protest history briefs and photos of protesters who have
been ejected from or
‘known to have gained entry’ to the BP AGM (BP, 2012). For example,
the note on Sophie Harvey states that ‘The female below is Sophie
Harvey and was ejected after staging a demonstration inside the
meeting. She also attended 2011 year AGM and was ejected after
taking part in a protest’. The ‘Individuals of Note’ document contains
photos of activists from protest contexts as well as portrait photos
from activists’ online profiles. Much of the biographical data on
individual activists seems to be circulated within BP in connection
with specific events such as the AGM. The email shown in Figure 6.3,
from 14 April 2010, the day before BP’s 2010 AGM, illustrates this.
The attachment to the email includes a photo of Sophie Harvey as well
as biographical details, including her name, position, company,
educational degree, and previous position and work place. This points
towards a practice of identifying specific individuals for further
monitoring.

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 192 ---
126 Julie Uldam
Figure 6.3 BP email from April 2010.
Indeed, the Subject Access Request files also show how BP monitors
known anti- BP protesters in relation to protest activities against other
companies. This is captured in the following email with the subject
line ‘Web footage from CON
protest: anti- BP protesters present’ (CON stands for Counter Olympics
Network). The email identifies two members from the Reclaim
Shakespeare
Company (see Figure 6.4).
The CON protests were organised by the Counter Olympics Network to
protest against the corporatisation of the Olympics. The email thus
illustrates how BP’s monitoring of activists’ participation extends
beyond critique levelled specifically against BP to protest activities
against sponsorships more generally. Furthermore, BP monitors
protest events against other companies on Facebook to anticipate
potential criticism levelled against the company. This is further
exemplified by the email shown in Figure 6.5 in which BP is alerted
that ‘two known anti- BP activists’
are on the list of 105 invited participants for a protest event organised
by the cam-
paign group Drop Dow Now and targeted against the company Dow

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 193 ---
Chemicals.9,10
The main concern in the email is whether BP will be targeted
specifically and if the protest will have a large attendance and thus
increased visibility.
While both these perceived risks are taken to be unlikely, the sender
nonethe-less suggests monitoring Twitter. While anticipating civil
society criticism may serve the purpose of improving standards or
engaging in debate (whether by providing counter- arguments or
trying to find common ground) (Den Hond & De Bakker, 2007;
Whelan et al., 2013), the concern with individuals and on protests
against other companies in this email exchange suggests that it also
serves the purpose of preparing for the containment of the visibility of
critics.
Between visibility and surveillance 127
Figure 6.4 BP email, sender and receiver redacted by BP, activist
names anonymised by author.

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 194 ---
Source: copy of email provided by person who obtained the files
through a Subject Access Request.
Monitoring activists: containing the visibility of
individuals
In some cases, the monitoring of individuals leads to further action so
as to ensure the containment of criticism. The ways in which BP’s
mechanisms of containment are brought into play are illustrated by
the company’s response to the ‘f***ing the future’ campaign, which
attempted to subvert billboards with BP’s ‘fuelling the future’
advertisements in connection with BP’s sponsorship of the 2012
Olympics and the Cultural Olympiad, and particularly its role as
‘Sustainability Partner’ (Figure 6.6).
BP took action to have first the websites f- ingthefuture.org and later
f- ingthefuture.org.uk removed. The f- ingthefuture.org website was
taken down

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 195 ---
128 Julie Uldam
Figure 6.5 BP email, sender and receiver redacted by BP, activist
names anonymised by author.
Source: copy of email provided by person who has obtained the files
through a Subject Access Request.
Figure 6.6 Images from the f***ing the future website.
following accusations from BP of ‘brand infringement’ (Claire Smith,
November 2012, personal communication). The accusation of brand
infringement highlights BP’s concern with protecting a veneer of
sustainability and legitimacy (Zyglidopoulos & Fleming, 2011). It was
possible for BP to have the f- ingthefuture.org website removed,
because the people behind the f***ing the future campaign had
breached the terms of service11 of the hosting platform
(Wordpress.com) by not providing a real name and address for the site
and domain name registration. Therefore, Wordpress.com
administrators did not hesitate to remove the blog when BP’s
representatives contacted them with
Between visibility and surveillance 129
allegations of ‘brand infringement’ in relation to the website’s domain
name, f- ingthefuture.org (Claire Smith, November 2012, personal
communication). The people behind the f***ing the future campaign
had not provided their real name and address, because Wordpress.com
has a history of handing over the identities of their bloggers.12 This
was seen as an important precaution, as authorities were clamping
down on most protest activity in London in the run- up to and during
the Olympic Games and the activists wanted to avoid accusations of
criminal damage (Tom Parry, November 2012, personal

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 196 ---
communication). Concerns about anonymity are of central importance
for activists using online media, not only in the context of the
contestation of CSR and greenwashing, but also more generally in a
diverse range of other groups and movements, such as media activists
and protesters in the Arab Spring (Hintz & Milan, 2010). BP’s
instructions to Wordpress to take down the f- ingthefuture.org blog
illustrates what Youmans and York (2012, p. 317) call a ‘mismatch
between the commercial logic of platforms … and the needs of
activists’. Profit- driven online platforms such as Wordpress, Facebook
and YouTube rely on boosting revenue generation and access to new
markets, which requires avoiding negative publicity and appealing to
advertisers and a wide range of non- activist users (Youmans & York,
2012). This sometimes translates into terms of service that complicate
activist uses of the platforms (Papacharissi & Fernback, 2005; Hestres,
2013). In this way, the activists were unable to keep their identities
invisible and actions visible using Wordpress.com. Moreover, using
accusations of brand infringement to dispute a domain name provides
an example of the co- optation of the design and operations of internet
governance technologies (DeNardis, 2012).
These interrelations between the governance and architecture of
internet technologies on the one hand and visibility asymmetries on
the other, render invisible several aspects of the logics and
mechanisms that drive social media practices. In contrast,
independent platforms that rely on open source technologies and
volunteer support can better disregard take down notices from
companies and allow critical content to remain online. In this way, the
content from the f- ingthefuture.org blog was moved to the hosting
platform Network 23
with the new domain name f- ingthefuture.org.uk. Network 23 is an
independent hosting platform set up by tech activists who volunteer
their skills and time to help activists stay anonymous when they
publicise their campaigns online – to achieve ‘visible external effects
cum an invisible internal core’ (Brighenti, 2010a, p. 164). The
platform uses open source WordPress software, but is hosted on
Network 23’s site rather than the commercial Wordpress.com. As the
new f–ingthefuture blog appeared, BP also contacted Network 23,
requesting to have it taken down (Claire Smith, November 2012,
personal communication).
However, the site’s independent status enabled its administrators to
circumvent this request, e.g. by not keeping users’ details and by
continuing to host the

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 197 ---
website at https://network23.org/f- ingthefuture/.
An increasing politicisation of hackers (Deibert, 2003) has contributed
to the proliferation of a wide range of ‘grassroots tech groups’ who
provide activists
130 Julie Uldam
with technical assistance and resources for protecting their online
security (Milan & Hintz, 2013, p. 8). In the UK, one example is
HacktionLab who were also involved in setting up Network 23
(https://hacktionlab.org/). However, grassroots tech groups and
independent platforms such as HacktionLab and Network23 have very
limited resources and only have the capacity to assist and host a
fraction of campaigns and causes (Milan & Hintz, 2013). In this way,
the current governance and architecture of social media privilege
corporate and elite interests over civil society interests (Curran et al.,
2012; Dahlgren, 2013; DeNardis, 2012). In the case of the f***ing the
future campaign, this enabled BP to impede the circulation of the
campaign’s criticism and thus eliminate the visibility of disagreement
from the public sphere.
Conclusion
This chapter has investigated the ways in which the oil company BP
monitors individual activists in social media with the purpose of
impeding the circulation of their criticism to wider publics. Drawing
on conceptions of visibility, it has argued that social media entail both
empowering and disempowering potential.
Social media can help enable activists gain visibility for their
campaigns.
However, social media also make activists more vulnerable by
enabling companies that want to contain the visibility of their critics
able to do so. This poses challenges of visibility asymmetries that
privilege corporate actors, as activists move from alternative/citizen
media platforms (Atton, 2001; Downing, 2001; Rogriguez, 2001) to
profit- driven social media. Some activists try to use alternative/
citizen media platforms – as in the case of the f***ing the future
campaign moving from WordPress.com to Network23 – but this
entails two main challenges: (1) alternative/citizen media platforms
have limited resources, including limited capacity for hosting websites
and for providing technical support; and (2) to achieve visibility
among wider publics. activists need to publicise their campaigns

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 198 ---
where internet users will see them. As most social media users use
commercial platforms rather than alternative/citizen platforms, they
are central to the possibilities for reaching beyond like- minded
activists.
The chapter contributes to research on corporate organisations’
surveillance of activists in social media for identifying (reputational)
risks, demonstrating how not only governments but also companies
can both instil social norms among activists and enforce these by
threating legal action (see Dutton et al., 2010). The cases of BP’s
surveillance of activists in this analysis should not be taken as
representative of all multinational corporations – or all BP’s
interactions with civil society stakeholders for that matter. However,
along with previous research on corporate surveillance of activists
(Juris, 2005; Lubbers, 2012) they do gesture towards a wider response
to the visibilities that social media afford. More research is needed to
provide a fuller picture of these tendencies and their intersections
with internet governance.
Between visibility and surveillance 131

Chapter 7
The impact of video tracking
routines on crowd behaviour and
crowd policing
Marco Krüger
Introduction
Police cameras accompanying crowds belong to the standard security
routines in today’s crowd policing. However, the multitude of hand-
held mobile cameras as well as stationary overview cameras are rarely
mutually connected so that a gapless observation of particular
individuals is difficult, if possible at all. German police forces
identified the resulting gaps in the chain of evidence as one major
shortcoming in producing legally robustly usable video material.
Consequently, the German Federal police co- founded a research
project aimed at developing a video- tracking system for crowd
policing operations. The research project, called ‘Muskat’,1 started in
September 2014 and was completed in November 2017. It deployed
football policing as a sample scenario for a broader engagement with
video tracking in complex crowd policing operations. Throughout the
project, a tracking framework emerged which builds on existing
camera equipment while taking the current surveillance ability to a
new level.

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 206 ---
The introduction of video tracking as a means for crowd policing is an
example of, what Gary T. Marx (2015, p. 23) calls, ‘counter-
neutralization technologies’, which increase the pressure for those
who are involuntarily under surveillance to develop new and more
sophisticated strategies of resistance. Video tracking aims at
countering the neutralisation efforts of individuals or groups who
attempt to resist surveillance. Due to the system design, the utilisation
of video tracking affects both the strategy of the police as well as the
behaviour of the members of the supervised crowd. Prima facie, video
tracking appears as a panoptical side through which the many are
watched by the invisible few.
However, even this comprehensive surveillance system is not able to
unfold the totalitarian disciplinary effect of Bentham’s vision, but
leaves space for subver-sion and resistance. Although tracking routines
in general are by no means a new invention, their exploitation for
routine crowd policing operations is new.
In the course of this chapter, I seek to outline how the utilisation of
tracking routines impacts the dynamics of crowd policing and, thus,
requires changes in the strategies of resistance.
136 Marco Krüger
The analysis is based on the continuous participation in research
project meetings, the examination of project results as well as on 11
qualitative interviews conducted between October 2014 and March
2015 with police officers of different ranks, football supporter
representatives from German clubs, and social workers dealing with
football supporters. The description of the tracking routine and the
design of the surveillance system is drawn from the demonstration
model, which was developed over the course of the research project.
Although live tracking routines for the use in complex environments
are still under development, both tracking algorithms and
interconnected camera systems already exist. A connected video
tracking system combines currently deployed surveillance devices to a
larger system, which allows for more comprehensive surveillance than
its currently unconnected parts. At the moment, a tracking system for
police operations is still under development. Thus, looking into the
functioning of a connected video tracking system means dealing with
the future of crowd policing. To examine the consequences of video
tracking for both crowd policing and crowd behaviour, this chapter
proceeds in four steps. The chapter starts with tracing the link
between football policing and protest policing.

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 207 ---
Thereby, I demonstrate that football is used as a testing field for
security practices that are later deployed in political contexts.
Moreover, football crowds, as well as crowds of protestors, reveal
some similarities, at least from a police perspective. Second, I will
introduce the fundamental functionality of the developed video
tracking system. This description serves as the basis for the subsequent
analysis of video tracking’s impact on crowd policing and crowd
behaviour. The subsequent sections represent the core of the analysis.
In the third step, I demonstrate that, although the system design
resembles a panoptical site (Foucault, 1995), it alters and
simultaneously opens avenues for resistance. Video tracking does not
leave observed individuals as mere objects of surveillance, but needs
to be analysed as a social relation between the watcher and the
watched. Finally, and fourth, the chapter concludes by considering
how crowd behaviour might alter due to the utilisation of video
tracking systems.
On the link between football policing and
protest policing
At first glance, somewhat confusingly, the research project at hand is
situated in a football scenario. However, the project title, which reads
‘Multisensory based detection of offenders in crowds in complex police
operations’,2 already implies that the project results are to be
transferred to police operations other than football policing. In fact,
the police expect the system to facilitate crowd policing in various
settings by generally increasing prosecution capabilities.
Furthermore, the connection between football policing and protest
policing has already been manifested in the past transfer of policing
technologies. One example is the transfer of the digital ‘Perpetrator of
Violence’ file from football to the political realm. In 1994, the
‘Perpetrator of Violence – Sport’3 file
The impact of video tracking routines 137
was launched to collect data on potentially violent football supporters.
Currently, the file embraces approximately 13,500 individuals (Ruch
& Feltes, 2016, p. 63). Not only violent offenders, but also those
supporters who are assumed to be prone to violence are listed in the
file. Even persons who have undergone a police check while they were
in company with an individual classified as violent can be included in
the file. Consequently, critics have repeatedly pointed to the legally
questionable procedure and the connected stigmatisation of football

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 208 ---
supporters (Ruch & Feltes, 2016, p. 63). Rather than taking these
criticisms seriously, the football file served as a blueprint for political
‘Perpetrators of Violence’ files. In this case, football policing was used
as a laboratory for political policing, including protest behaviour. I
argue that this is not a coincidence, but a deliberate choice. Thierry
Balzacq (2015, p. 3) stated that ‘[s]ecurity practices draw their
efficiency from legitimacy, as legitimacy confers them a normative
status’. Securitisation in turn predates security practices and is shaped
by the practices at play, once they are established (Balzacq, 2015, p.
2). The intersubjectively shared assumption that a certain issue is to
be regarded as a security threat legitimises the introduction of a given
practice. Various scientific studies have investigated the link between
football and security and stated that either football in general (Frevel
& Riederer, 2014; Giulianotti, 2011) or some supporter groups in
particular, e.g.
Hooligans (Bigo, 2008a, 2008b) or Ultras (Gabler, 2011), are widely
perceived as a security issue, and hence should be securitised.
Football, as testing field for security practices, owes its role to the
similarity with protest environments. This is the case for three
reasons. First, football games are mass events. Football supporters
often approach the stadium collectively and create a complex
operational scenario for police forces. Second, due to fan equipment,
the distinct colours of the respective football club and at least partly
organised fan scenes, a good proportion of football supporters have a
rather uniform appearance. Organised fan groups act collectively and
systematically, which in turn resembles the types of action that are
used by organised political groups. The mere fact that the consortium
of the research project, and particularly the represented police forces,
has opted for a football scenario as vantage point to analyse video
tracking in crowd settings implies the assumed transferability of the
results drawn from football to other crowd scenarios. However, there
is an important distinction between football and at least some kind of
authorised political protest: approved political manifestations are
protected by fundamental rights such as the freedom of speech and
the freedom of assembly, guaranteed, for example, by the articles 11
and 12 of the EU Charter of Fundamental Rights. Large crowds of
football supporters who met only for the purpose of jointly
approaching the stadium do, in contrast, usually not fall under this
category and, consequently, are not legally protected to this extent.
Yet, this legal protection is limited to authorised peaceful political
manifestations and, thus, not the case for every form of political
protest.

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 209 ---
138 Marco Krüger
The legal situation, as well as the anyway securitised matter,
facilitates the justification of the deployment of surveillance
technology in a football context.
From a police perspective, assemblies of football supporters are, thus,
not only comparable but even preferable to political protest in order
to test new policing technology. Consequently, analysing football
policing might allow one to look into the future of protest policing.
Given this legal situation, it is hard to imagine that video tracking
might be used for the surveillance of legal and peaceful political
manifestations in countries with a proper level of fundamental rights.
However, there is a broad range of other protest forms and crowd phe-
nomena, which do not fall under this particular legal protection.
Football policing is thereby only one example for the deployment of
tracking routines in crowd policing. Moreover, once developed,
security technologies become goods in a growing private security
market (e.g. Guittet & Jeandesboz, 2010; Hoijtink, 2014; Zedner,
2006) and, thus, are globally traded. In this vein, Ayse Ceyhan (2008)
analysed the spread of biometrics on a globalised security market.
Although developed within a national research consortium, it is
sensible to consider the global scale of security markets for the spread
of security technology.
This and the narrow range of political protest, i.e. authorised
manifestations, which are subject to a higher legal protection, result in
plenty of opportunities for deploying tracking systems in political
contexts.
Connected surveillance: the case of video tracking
This section describes the design and the functionality of the video
tracking system that has been developed over the course of the
research project. The developed video tracking system consists of two
essential features: a system of connected cameras, which allows for
the observation of people, and a tracking algorithm, which enables
police forces to follow a particular individual. The capacity of the
system depends on the scope of the area which is under surveillance
and on the ability to recognise a person. It is therefore crucial to use a
set of overview cameras whose angles are overlapping and which are
placed as high as possible and, in the best case, provide the police
with a bird’s-eye view on a crowd. Current camera resolutions allow
for usable images from considerable distances. Therefore, the angle on

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 210 ---
the crowd is decisive, as it is crucial that the tracked individual is not
concealed by another person. These overview cameras are
supplemented by mobile hand- held cameras that are carried by police
officers in order to deliver close- up images of a particular individual.
The live tracking process itself is characterised by three phases: (1)
tagging; (2) tracking; and (3) the observed approach of an individual.
In phase (1), police officers (G1 and G2) equipped with hand- held
cameras monitor a crowd of people. In the depicted scenario, police
unit G2 witnesses an offence and marks the alleged perpetrator (R1)
on the camera display (Figure 7.1). Using the geographic coordinates
of the previously calibrated camera, the position data of both the
camera and the marked person (R1), who is situated within a
The impact of video tracking routines 139

Figure 7.1 Tagging phase.
Source: author’s own diagram.
crowd of uninvolved people (unmarked spots), are sent to the
operation centre and to the respective overview camera, which covers
the area of interest.
After marking a person, phase (2) and therewith the actual tracking
process, begins. The officer- in-charge at the operation centre now
receives a real- time image of the overview- camera’s angle (triangle
in Figures 7.2 and 7.3) as well as a map- based scheme showing the
locations of the tagged person (R1) and the police forces (G1/G2). The
overview camera within the moving crowd now follows R1 while live
data are permanently transmitted to the operation centre
(Figure 7.2). Thereby, the tracking process can be realised in various

--- Bölüm/Sayfa 211 ---
ways, which are to different extents ethically problematic, e.g. in
terms of intrusion into a person’s privacy or discrimination. For
example, the individual biometric characteristics of a person, in this
case through facial recognition, would allow for tracking, as this
person becomes identifiable throughout the various video sequences.
Due to legal considerations, the deployment of this method was
abandoned. Another approach to track people is to analyse a pixel
cluster and to follow this specific composition of pixels. In this
tracking procedure, a police operator tags a certain pixel cluster. The
algorithm is subsequently searching in each new image of the video
stream for exactly this particular cluster. However, this heuristic
knowledge generation demands a human operator check for the
correctness of the process, as tracks are prone to flaws in rapidly
changing observation settings.
Although this method appears to be less intrusive than biometrics, as
it neither records biometric nor other individual characteristics of a
particular person, the overview camera still delivers a normal video
live stream to the operation centre on which the tagged person is
visible. Accordingly, ordinary photographs can be drawn from the
video material and potentially compared with entries in an underlying
database or even saved in a database. One way or another, the track
follows a person who can be photographed by the overview
140 Marco Krüger

camera and the mobile police teams in order to get usable information
on the tracked individual. Phase (2) lasts until the observed person
either moves out of the angles of the connected overview cameras or
gets into a convenient position for the police forces to enter phase (3).
In the final phase (Figure 7.3), the suspect (R1) is approached by
those police forces (G1) who are best situated to ensure quick access
to the observed person with the least possible interference of others.
The approaching process is observed by the operation centre through
the overview camera. This procedure aims at minimising the
emergence of solidarity among members of the crowd, which might
hinder the police from approaching R1. As soon as R1 is approached,
the tracking process ends.
